# Edexcel A-level Statistics (9ST0)

Complete notes for this qualification, generated from `curriculum/notes` by `curriculum/scripts/compile.py`. Do not edit this file; edit the notes and run the script again. Spec references are not yet checked by a person (`refs_checked: false`).

Order and contents follow `notes/a-level/statistics/map.md`. 40 notes files.

## Spec map

# A-level Statistics: spec map

Every spec point of Pearson Edexcel A-level Statistics (9ST0), in the spec's own order, with the notes that cover it. Edexcel is the only board that offers A-level Statistics, so there is one table.

- The spec numbers its content 1.1 to 21.2 (21 topics), then lists the Statistical Enquiry Cycle (SEC) in five lettered stages, A Initial planning (1 to 6), B Data collection (1 to 4), C Data processing and presentation (1 to 3), D Interpretation of results (1 to 5) and E Evaluation and review (1 to 4). Here the SEC points are written SEC A1 to SEC E4.
- Appendix 4 (use of calculators, spreadsheets, databases and graph-plotting software) is examinable language, written here as App4.
- Paper 1 (Data and Probability, 9ST0/01) sets topics 1 to 6, 7 (except 7.2), 11, 12, 13 and 18. Paper 2 (Statistical Inference, 9ST0/02) sets 7.2, 8 to 10, 13 to 17 and 19 to 21. Paper 3 (Statistics in Practice, 9ST0/03) may set anything. The SEC is on all three papers. Each paper is 2 hours and 80 marks.
- Many ideas are already written up in the A-level Mathematics, A-level Further Mathematics and GCSE Statistics notes. Those files are linked, not repeated. The files in this folder add what 9ST0 asks for beyond them (for example published visualisations, judgmental and snowball sampling, Bayes' theorem for three events, the two-proportion test, experimental design, ANOVA and effect size), and restate each spec point in the 9ST0 wording so the folder works on its own.

Paper key: P1, P2 = the paper that sets it (Paper 3 can set anything). Main file = the file in this folder written for that point; Also see = existing notes that cover the same ground.

`refs_checked: false`: references were taken from the specification PDF (Pearson Edexcel Level 3 Advanced GCE in Statistics 9ST0, Issue 2, January 2024; code 9ST0 confirmed there) and still need a person to check them.

## The statistical enquiry cycle

This file covers the five stages of the statistical enquiry cycle (SEC) as A-level Statistics uses them: initial planning, data collection, data processing and presentation, interpretation of results, and evaluation and review. The SEC is examined on all three papers, usually as part of a longer question set in a real context, and it carries most of the AO3 marks (critically assessing methods and conclusions).

### Overview

The cycle is a loop, not a line. You plan, collect, process, interpret and evaluate, and the evaluation usually sends you back to planning with a sharper question. At GCSE the cycle is described in [GCSE Statistics 01](../../gcse/statistics/01-statistical-enquiry-cycle-and-planning.md) and data collection in [GCSE Statistics 04](../../gcse/statistics/04-collecting-and-cleaning-data.md). At A-level you are expected to go further: run exploratory data analysis before committing to a method, choose a formal test, judge reliability against sample size and sampling method, and write for a named audience. Questions often include unfamiliar acronyms or real data you have not met; read the context carefully and use it in every answer.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel is the only board with an A-level in Statistics. The SEC is listed after topic 21 as stages A to E; it has no numbers of its own in the spec, so here the points are written SEC A1 to SEC E4.
- The SEC is set on all three papers (9ST0/01, /02, /03). Paper 3 (Statistics in Practice) leans on it most.

### SEC A Initial planning

*Refs: Edexcel SEC A1, A2, A3, A4, A5, A6.*

#### What you need to know

- A1: list the factors that may be related to the problem, including possible confounding factors you will need to control or record.
- A2: turn a vague aim into a precise question or a testable hypothesis (or several).
- A3: decide which variables to measure, the units, whether data are primary or secondary, the sampling method and sample size, and how data will be recorded, with a reason for each choice.
- A4: carry out exploratory data analysis (EDA): quick summaries and plots of a pilot or existing data set to see shape, spread, outliers and possible relationships before the main analysis.
- A5: plan how the data will be processed and shown (which summary measures, diagrams and tests), with reasons linked to the data type and question.
- A6: justify the plan, explaining how it keeps bias low.

#### Explained

**From aim to hypothesis.** "Does exercise help sleep?" is an aim. A hypothesis is precise enough to be tested: "Adults aged 18 to 30 who do at least 150 minutes of moderate exercise a week have a higher median sleep duration than those who do less." It names the population, the variables and the direction.

**Factors.** A good plan lists variables that could affect the response other than the one you care about. For sleep these include age, caffeine, screen time and shift work. You either control them (keep them fixed), record them (so you can compare groups fairly), or randomise to spread them evenly (see [12-experimental-design.md](12-experimental-design.md)).

**Exploratory data analysis.** EDA is looking before you test. With a small pilot sample you might draw a box plot and a histogram. If the data are strongly skewed, a test that assumes normality (a t-test) may be unsuitable and a non-parametric test (Wilcoxon) better. If a scatter diagram is curved, Pearson's correlation is unsuitable and Spearman's may be better. EDA can also reveal recording errors (a sleep time of 80 hours) and suggest new hypotheses.

**Strategy for processing.** Match the method to the data:

| Data and question | Summary and diagram | Possible test |
| --- | --- | --- |
| one numerical variable, two groups | medians and IQRs, back-to-back box plots | two-sample t, Wilcoxon rank-sum |
| one numerical variable, paired | differences, mean and sd of differences | paired t, Wilcoxon signed-rank, sign |
| two numerical variables | scatter diagram, correlation | pmcc or Spearman test, regression |
| two categorical variables | two-way table, compound bar chart | chi-squared test for association |
| one numerical variable, three or more groups | box plots side by side | one-way ANOVA |

**Justifying the plan.** Examiners reward reasons tied to the context: "random sampling from the full staff list so that every department is represented without the researcher choosing who takes part" is better than "to avoid bias".

#### Worked example

A council asks whether a new 20 mph limit has reduced speeds on residential streets. Plan the enquiry.

1. Factors (A1): time of day, day of week, weather, road width, school terms, traffic volume.
2. Hypothesis (A2): the mean speed of cars on residential streets is lower after the limit than before.
3. Data (A3): primary data, speeds from a radar gun at the same 12 sites, at the same times and days of the week, for one week before and one week after. Record time, weather and site with each speed.
4. EDA (A4): a pilot hour at two sites shows a few very high speeds (motorbikes overtaking). Speeds are slightly positively skewed, so medians should be reported as well as means.
5. Strategy (A5): for each site, the mean speed before and after gives paired data across 12 sites. Draw a box plot of the 12 differences and use a paired t-test if the differences look roughly normal, otherwise a Wilcoxon signed-rank test.
6. Justification (A6): the same sites and time slots remove differences between sites and times; choosing sites at random from the list of affected streets stops the council choosing streets where speeds have obviously fallen.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing an aim ("investigate speeds") instead of a testable hypothesis with a population and direction.
- Listing factors without saying how each will be controlled, recorded or randomised.
- Choosing a test before looking at the data's shape.

### SEC B Data collection

*Refs: Edexcel SEC B1, B2, B3, B4.*

#### What you need to know

- B1: design primary data collection to reduce bias: random sampling, clear definitions, consistent measurement, trained data collectors, piloting questionnaires.
- B2: secondary data (reference publications, official statistics, the internet, the media) is quick and cheap but you did not control how it was collected; check source, date, definitions, sample size and purpose.
- B3: always state how data were collected and acknowledge sources, so others can judge reliability and repeat the work.
- B4: leading questions bias responses, either by accident (careless wording) or on purpose (agenda-driven design, for example by a pressure group or a company advertising its product).

#### Explained

**Primary data.** You control it, so you can match it to the question, but it costs time and money. Bias creeps in through who is asked (sampling bias), who answers (non-response bias), how they are asked (question wording, interviewer effect) and how values are measured (an uncalibrated scale).

**Secondary data.** Ask: who collected it and why? When? How were terms defined (does "unemployed" mean the same in two countries)? Was it a sample or a census? A charity survey of "1,000 people" who answered a pop-up on its own website is not a random sample of the public.

**Declaring methodology.** A report that says "a random sample of 200 Year 12 students from five colleges, selected from enrolment lists using random numbers, answered an online questionnaire in March" lets a reader judge the scope. Acknowledging sources (for example, the Office for National Statistics table and its date) gives credit and lets others check.

**Leading questions.** "Don't you agree that the new car park is a waste of money?" pushes towards yes. "Do you think the new car park is good value for money?" with balanced response options is neutral. Agenda-driven surveys may also offer unbalanced options (Excellent, Very good, Good, Fair) or omit "don't know".

#### Worked example

A newspaper headline says "Three in four parents back longer school day". The article says the paper asked readers on its website: "Would a longer school day help working parents and raise standards?" 3,200 people voted, 2,400 yes. Criticise the claim.

1. Sampling: self-selected website readers, not a random sample of parents; voters may not be parents at all.
2. Wording: the question is leading (it assumes the longer day helps) and asks two things at once.
3. 2,400 ÷ 3,200 = 0.75, so "three in four" is correct for respondents, but cannot be generalised to all parents.
4. Methodology: the article does not say whether multiple votes were blocked. A fair report would declare this.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying secondary data is "unreliable" without a specific reason.
- Thinking a large sample removes bias; a large biased sample is still biased.
- Spotting a leading question but not rewriting it neutrally when asked.

### SEC C Data processing and presentation

*Refs: Edexcel SEC C1, C2, C3.*

#### What you need to know

- C1: organise raw data (sorting, filtering, grouping, cleaning) and use technology: spreadsheets, databases, calculators and graph-plotting software. The language of these tools is in [02-diagrams-summary-measures-and-outliers.md](02-diagrams-summary-measures-and-outliers.md).
- C2: choose diagrams and summary measures that let you make inferences about the population, and read outputs produced by software (for example a regression output with "df", "Coef" and "p").
- C3: avoid misrepresentation: honest scales, a sensible choice of measure, and no cherry-picked subsets.

#### Explained

**Cleaning.** Before calculating, remove or correct impossible values (negative ages), duplicate records and inconsistent units (heights in m and cm mixed). Note how many records you removed and why.

**Choosing measures.** For symmetrical data without outliers, the mean and standard deviation use every value. For skewed data or data with outliers, the median and interquartile range are more representative.

**Reading software output.** You may be shown a block such as:

| | Coef | SE Coef | T | P |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Constant | 12.4 | 3.1 | 4.00 | 0.001 |
| Hours | 2.35 | 0.62 | 3.79 | 0.002 |

Pick out what you need: the regression line is y = 12.4 + 2.35x, and the small p-value for "Hours" means the gradient is significantly different from zero at 5%.

#### Worked example

A spreadsheet holds 480 rows of house sales with fields Town, Bedrooms and Price. You want the median price of 3-bedroom houses in each of two towns, and a fair diagram to compare them.

1. Filter Bedrooms = 3 and sort by Town (C1).
2. Remove rows with a missing price or a price of 0 (cleaning); note the number removed.
3. House prices are usually positively skewed with high outliers, so use medians and IQRs, with back-to-back box plots on the same scale (C2).
4. Do not use a pictogram with house icons scaled by area, and do not start a bar chart's axis above zero (C3).

#### Common mistakes

- Using the mean for skewed data with outliers.
- Drawing two box plots on different scales.
- Copying every number from a software output instead of picking the relevant ones.

### SEC D Interpretation of results

*Refs: Edexcel SEC D1, D2, D3, D4, D5.*

#### What you need to know

- D1: interpret diagrams and calculated measures in context (compare a measure of location and a measure of spread, with units).
- D2: draw conclusions that answer the original question or hypothesis.
- D3: use an appropriate hypothesis test to decide whether an effect is statistically significant (tests are in files 08 to 17).
- D4: discuss reliability: sample size, sampling method, measurement error, whether the sample represents the population.
- D5: communicate clearly and concisely for the audience: a technical report for researchers, plain language and one clear chart for the public.

#### Explained

A conclusion has three parts: what the data show, whether that is significant, and how far it can be trusted. "The median sleep of the exercise group was 7.4 hours, 0.6 hours more than the other group, and the spread was similar. A Wilcoxon rank-sum test at 5% gave significant evidence of a difference. The sample was 40 volunteers from one university, so the result may not apply to all adults."

Statistically significant does not mean large or important (see effect size in [17-effect-size.md](17-effect-size.md)). Not significant does not prove there is no difference; it means there was not enough evidence.

#### Worked example

A study of 25 students finds that the correlation between hours of revision and test score is r = 0.42. The 5% one-tailed critical value for n = 25 is 0.3365. Write a conclusion for (a) a statistics teacher, (b) a school newsletter.

1. (a) 0.42 > 0.3365, so reject H₀: ρ = 0. There is evidence at the 5% level of positive correlation between revision hours and test score in the population of students. The correlation is only moderate and n = 25 is small; the sample came from one school.
2. (b) Students who revised for longer tended to score higher, although the link was not strong and some students who revised a lot still scored low. The study was small, so we will check again with more students.

#### Common mistakes

- Concluding "revision causes higher scores" from correlation.
- Stating a test result as certain ("this proves").
- Ignoring the audience when the question names one.

### SEC E Evaluation and review

*Refs: Edexcel SEC E1, E2, E3, E4.*

#### What you need to know

- E1: identify weaknesses in how data were collected or displayed.
- E2: recognise limits from the sample size (small samples give wide confidence intervals and low power) and the sampling method (non-random samples may not represent the population).
- E3: suggest specific improvements: a larger random sample, stratification, a control group, blinding, a better diagram.
- E4: refine the process to clarify the hypothesis, often starting the cycle again with a narrower question.

#### Explained

Improvements should be specific and should fix the weakness you named. "Use a bigger sample" is weak; "increase the sample from 20 to 80, chosen by stratified random sampling across the four year groups, so the confidence interval for the mean is about half as wide and every year is represented" is strong. The interval width halves because it is proportional to 1/√n, and √80 = 2√20.

#### Worked example

A café owner records the number of cakes sold on 10 sunny days and 10 rainy days in July. Sunny days averaged 42 cakes and rainy days 35. She concludes "rain cuts cake sales by a sixth". Evaluate and suggest improvements.

1. E1: only July; sunny days may also be weekends or school holidays (a confounding factor).
2. E2: 10 days per group is small; a difference of 7 may be due to chance. 7 ÷ 42 ≈ 0.17, so "a sixth" describes this sample only.
3. E3: record sales over a whole year, note day of the week, and compare sunny and rainy days of the same weekday (blocking), then use a test for two means.
4. E4: refine the hypothesis to "on weekdays in summer, mean daily cake sales are lower on rainy days than on dry days".

#### Common mistakes

- Generic improvements that do not match the weakness.
- Forgetting to say how the improvement would change the conclusion.

### Check questions

1. A gym says "Members who use our app lose more weight." Write a precise hypothesis that could be tested, naming the population and variables. [2 marks]
2. Give two factors other than diet that could affect weight loss, and say how one of them could be controlled. [3 marks]
3. A pilot sample of reaction times is strongly positively skewed. Explain how this affects your choice of summary measures and of a test comparing two groups. [3 marks]
4. A survey asks "How much do you agree that the unfair parking charges should be scrapped?" Explain why this question may cause bias and rewrite it. [3 marks]
5. Give two checks you would make before using a table of regional crime figures found on a website. [2 marks]
6. A test of a new teaching method on 12 pupils in one class gives p = 0.03. Give two reasons to be cautious about applying this to all pupils in England. [2 marks]
7. A study compares mean commuting times in two towns using samples of 15. Suggest one improvement and explain its effect on the confidence interval for the difference. [3 marks]
8. Write one sentence reporting the finding "median journey time fell from 34 to 29 minutes after the new timetable" for a local newspaper. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. Population named, e.g. adult members of the gym over 3 months (1); clear direction and variables, e.g. members who log in to the app at least weekly have a greater mean weight loss than members who do not (1).
2. Any two, e.g. amount of exercise, starting weight, age, sleep, medical conditions (2); a control, e.g. compare members with similar starting weights / record exercise hours and compare like with like (1).
3. Use median and IQR rather than mean and standard deviation (1) because they are not distorted by the long tail or outliers (1); prefer a non-parametric test such as Wilcoxon rank-sum, as a t-test assumes normality (1).
4. The word "unfair" is leading / assumes an opinion (1), so people are pushed to agree (1); a neutral version, e.g. "Should parking charges be kept, changed or scrapped?" with balanced options (1).
5. Any two: who published it and why; date; definitions of crimes; how collected / sample or census; whether regions are comparable (population sizes) (2).
6. Any two: small sample; one class only, not random; class or teacher effect; no control group mentioned; p = 0.03 is evidence, not proof (2).
7. Increase sample size (e.g. to 60 per town) / use random sampling (1); the standard error falls with larger n (1), so the interval is narrower, here about half as wide since √60 = 2√15 (1).
8. e.g. "After the new timetable, the typical journey took 5 minutes less, 29 minutes instead of 34." (1)

<!-- source: notes/a-level/statistics/02-diagrams-summary-measures-and-outliers.md -->

## Diagrams, summary measures, outliers and technology

This file covers interpreting statistical diagrams (including published and multivariate visualisations), what makes a representation fair or misleading, comparing data sets with measures of location and spread, calculating those measures, outliers and their causes, and the language of spreadsheets, databases and graph-plotting software.

### Overview

How to read each diagram, and how to calculate the mean, median, quartiles, variance and standard deviation, are written up in [A-level Maths 20](../maths/20-data-presentation-and-interpretation.md); GCSE-level construction is in [GCSE Statistics 05](../../gcse/statistics/05-tables-and-pictorial-representations.md), [06](../../gcse/statistics/06-graphs-histograms-and-cumulative-frequency.md), [07](../../gcse/statistics/07-averages.md) and [08](../../gcse/statistics/08-spread-outliers-and-skewness.md). A-level Statistics adds three things: you are never asked to draw a diagram, only to read, judge and criticise one; diagrams may show several variables at once (multivariate data); and you must explain where an outlier came from, not just find it.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 9ST0 topic 1 (1.1 to 1.8) is on Paper 1. Appendix 4 lists the spreadsheet, database and software language you may meet on any paper.
- The diagrams that may appear are: bar charts, pie charts (assumed knowledge), stem and leaf diagrams, box plots, cumulative frequency diagrams, histograms with equal or unequal classes, time series (with trend lines) and scatter diagrams (with regression lines). No other visualisation types are set, apart from published tables.
- Edexcel's outlier rules to learn: below LQ − 1.5 × IQR or above UQ + 1.5 × IQR, or outside μ ± 3σ. A question will say which to use.

### 1.1 Interpreting statistical diagrams

*Refs: Edexcel 1.1. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Read and interpret bar charts, stem and leaf diagrams, box plots, cumulative frequency diagrams, histograms (equal or unequal class widths), time series and scatter diagrams.
- Comment on the main features of published visualisations, including those showing multivariate data (three or more variables, often using colour, symbol shape or panels for the extra variables).
- In a histogram, area is proportional to frequency; the height is the frequency density.

```latex
\text{frequency density} = \frac{\text{frequency}}{\text{class width}}
```

#### Explained

**Main features.** For one variable, comment on location (where the centre is), spread, shape (symmetric, positive or negative skew, one or two peaks) and unusual values. For a time series, comment on trend, seasonal pattern and any one-off spikes. For a scatter diagram, comment on direction, strength, whether the pattern is linear, and any clusters or outliers.

**Multivariate visualisations.** A scatter diagram of petal length against petal width with points coloured by species shows three variables. Look for whether the groups form separate clusters, whether the relationship has the same direction in each group, and whether one group is much more spread out. A pattern in the whole data set can disappear or reverse within groups, so always look at the groups as well as the whole.

#### Worked example

A histogram shows journey times (minutes) with classes 0–10, 10–20, 20–30, 30–50 and 50–90 and frequency densities 0.8, 1.4, 1.0, 0.3 and 0.05. Find the frequencies, the total, an estimate of the mean, and describe the shape.

1. Frequency = density × width: 0.8 × 10 = 8, 1.4 × 10 = 14, 1.0 × 10 = 10, 0.3 × 20 = 6, 0.05 × 40 = 2. Total 40.
2. Midpoints 5, 15, 25, 40, 70.

```latex
\bar{x} = \frac{\sum f x}{\sum f} = \frac{8(5) + 14(15) + 10(25) + 6(40) + 2(70)}{40} = \frac{880}{40} = 22.0 \text{ minutes}
```

3. The modal class is 10–20 and the tail stretches far to the right, so the distribution is positively skewed.

#### Common mistakes

- Reading the height of an unequal-width histogram bar as the frequency.
- Describing a multivariate plot as if it were one group, missing that the groups behave differently.

### 1.2 Features of an appropriate representation

*Refs: Edexcel 1.2. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- A fair diagram has: a title, labelled axes with units, a scale that starts at zero for bar heights (or a clear axis break), equal intervals on each axis, a key for colours or symbols, and the same scale when two diagrams are compared.
- Misrepresentation happens through truncated axes, unequal scales, 3D effects that distort size, pictograms scaled by length instead of area, missing data, cherry-picked time windows, and pie charts with too many or overlapping categories.

#### Explained

The eye judges a bar by its length and a picture by its area. If an axis starts at 90 instead of 0, a bar of 95 looks twice the height of a bar of 92.5 even though the values differ by under 3%. If a coin picture is doubled in width and height to show a value doubled, its area quadruples, so the increase looks four times as big.

#### Worked example

A bar chart shows sales of 92 and 98 thousand units, with the vertical axis starting at 90. By what factor does the second bar appear larger, and by what factor is it actually larger?

1. Visible heights: 92 − 90 = 2 and 98 − 90 = 8, so it looks 8 ÷ 2 = 4 times larger.
2. Actual ratio: 98 ÷ 92 ≈ 1.07, so only about 7% larger.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a diagram is misleading without naming the feature and its effect.

### 1.3 Justifying a representation and commenting on published ones

*Refs: Edexcel 1.3. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Choose the diagram that suits the data type and purpose, and give a reason.
- Comment critically on published diagrams: what they show well, what they hide, and how they could mislead.

#### Explained

| Purpose | Suitable | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| compare two distributions of a numerical variable | back-to-back box plots or stem and leaf | shows median, spread and skew side by side |
| show raw values in a small data set | stem and leaf | keeps every value |
| grouped continuous data with unequal classes | histogram | area keeps frequencies fair |
| estimate median and percentiles from grouped data | cumulative frequency | quartiles and percentiles read directly |
| change over time | time series | shows trend and seasonality |
| relationship between two numerical variables | scatter diagram | shows direction, strength and form |
| proportions of a whole | pie chart or bar chart | bar chart is easier to compare |

A box plot hides the number of values and any gaps or second peak; a histogram hides individual values; a pie chart hides totals.

#### Worked example

A report shows the ages of 2,000 festival-goers in a pie chart with 15 age groups. Suggest a better diagram and justify it.

1. Age is continuous and grouped, and 15 sectors are hard to compare.
2. A histogram (with frequency density if the groups have unequal widths) shows the shape of the age distribution, and a cumulative frequency diagram would let the organiser read off the median and the percentage under 18.

#### Common mistakes

- Recommending a pie chart for continuous data or for comparing two large data sets.

### 1.4 and 1.5 Comparing data sets and calculating measures

*Refs: Edexcel 1.4, 1.5. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Compare data sets with a measure of location (mean, median, mode) and a measure of spread (range, IQR, percentiles, variance, standard deviation), always in context.
- From raw data, get the mean, variance and standard deviation directly from a calculator. Know when to use the population formula (divide by N) and the sample formula (divide by n − 1); the formulae booklet gives both.

```latex
\sigma^2 = \frac{\sum x^2}{N} - \mu^2, \qquad s^2 = \frac{1}{n-1}\left(\sum x^2 - \frac{(\sum x)^2}{n}\right)
```

- For grouped data, use midpoints; the results are estimates.

#### Explained

Use σ when the data are the whole population and you want to describe it. Use s when the data are a sample and you want to estimate the population's spread; dividing by n − 1 makes s² an unbiased estimator of σ² (see [09-hypothesis-testing-principles-and-one-sample-tests.md](09-hypothesis-testing-principles-and-one-sample-tests.md)). On a calculator, σx (or σn) is the divisor-n value and sx (or σn−1) is the divisor-(n − 1) value.

A comparison needs two sentences: one on location, one on spread, each with numbers and context. "On average Route B is quicker (median 19 min against 22 min), but its times are more variable (IQR 11 min against 6 min), so Route A is more reliable."

#### Worked example

Ten commuting times (minutes): 12, 15, 18, 18, 21, 22, 25, 27, 30, 52. Find the mean, median, quartiles, and both the population and sample standard deviation.

1. Σx = 240, so the mean is 24.0 minutes.
2. Median: average of the 5th and 6th values = (21 + 22) ÷ 2 = 21.5.
3. LQ: 10 ÷ 4 = 2.5, round up to the 3rd value = 18. UQ: 30 ÷ 4 = 7.5, round up to the 8th value = 27. IQR = 9.
4. Σx² = 6900.

```latex
\sigma = \sqrt{\frac{6900}{10} - 24^2} = \sqrt{114} = 10.68, \qquad s = \sqrt{\frac{6900 - 240^2/10}{9}} = \sqrt{126.67} = 11.25
```

5. The mean (24.0) is above the median (21.5) because of the long journey of 52 minutes; the data are positively skewed, so the median and IQR describe a typical journey better.

#### Common mistakes

- Using σ when the question gives a sample and asks for an estimate of the population standard deviation.
- Comparing only averages and forgetting spread.
- Writing "the range is higher" without saying what that means in context.

### 1.6 and 1.7 Outliers: finding them and explaining them

*Refs: Edexcel 1.6, 1.7. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Identify outliers by inspection (a value far from the rest on a diagram) and by calculation:

```latex
x < Q_1 - 1.5 \times \text{IQR} \quad \text{or} \quad x > Q_3 + 1.5 \times \text{IQR}, \qquad \text{or } x \text{ outside } \mu \pm 3\sigma
```

- Decide the nature of an outlier by thinking about the population and how the data were collected: a recording or measuring error (remove or correct it), a value from a different population (exclude it and say why), or a genuine extreme value (keep it, and perhaps use resistant measures).

#### Explained

An outlier is a question, not a mistake. A height of 17.5 m in a survey of trees might be real; a height of 175 m is almost certainly a typing error. A rainfall reading of 0 mm in a wet month might be a broken gauge. A very high income in a sample of nurses may belong to someone who is also a company director, so is a genuine value but perhaps from outside the intended population. Never delete a value just because it is an outlier; state the reason.

The two rules can disagree: the IQR rule ignores extreme values when setting the fences, while μ ± 3σ uses a mean and standard deviation that the outlier itself inflates.

#### Worked example

For the commuting times in 1.4 (LQ 18, UQ 27, μ = 24.0, σ = 10.68), test 52 with both rules and comment.

1. IQR rule: upper fence = 27 + 1.5 × 9 = 40.5. Since 52 > 40.5, it is an outlier.
2. 3σ rule: 24.0 + 3 × 10.68 = 56.0. Since 52 < 56.0, it is not an outlier by this rule.
3. The 52 inflates σ, which widens the 3σ fence. Without it, the other nine values have mean 20.9 and s = 5.8, and 52 would be far outside 20.9 ± 3 × 5.8 = (3.5, 38.3).
4. Nature: a 52-minute commute is plausible (a train delay or a different route), so keep it unless there is evidence of an error, and report the median and IQR.

#### Common mistakes

- Using 1.5 × IQR added to the median instead of to the upper quartile.
- Removing an outlier without giving a reason linked to the context.

### 1.8 Misrepresentation out of context or through misleading visualisation

*Refs: Edexcel 1.8, SEC C3. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Data can mislead when taken out of context: totals instead of rates, a short or selected time window, missing comparisons, or changes in definitions.
- Visual tricks (truncated axes, area distortion, dual axes with different scales) can exaggerate or hide a pattern.

#### Explained

"Cycle accidents doubled in our city" may be true while the number of cyclists tripled, so the accident rate per cyclist fell. Always ask "per what?" and "compared with what?". A graph showing only the last three months may hide that the long-term trend is the opposite.

#### Worked example

Town A had 30 burglaries in 2024 and 45 in 2025. Town B had 300 and 330. A leaflet says "Town A's burglary problem is growing far faster". The populations are 6,000 (A) and 120,000 (B). Comment.

1. Percentage increase: A, 15 ÷ 30 = 50%; B, 30 ÷ 300 = 10%. The leaflet's claim is true for percentage change.
2. Rates per 1,000 people in 2025: A, 45 ÷ 6 = 7.5; B, 330 ÷ 120 = 2.75. A does have a higher rate.
3. But A's numbers are small, so a change of 15 could be chance; one year's data is too short a window to call a trend.

#### Common mistakes

- Comparing totals between groups of very different sizes.

### App4 and SEC C1, C2 Technology: calculators, spreadsheets, databases and software output

*Refs: Edexcel App4, SEC C1, SEC C2. All papers.*

#### What you need to know

- Calculators: summary statistics (mean, standard deviations, regression and correlation coefficients) and probabilities from binomial, Poisson and normal distributions.
- Spreadsheets: filter, sort, functions that count or sum (COUNT, SUM, COUNTIF), and functions for averages and spread (AVERAGE, MEDIAN, STDEV).
- Databases: a record is one row (one item or person); a field is one column (one variable). Filter, sort, and write a query that groups by a field, counts or sums another field, and joins two tables on a shared field.
- Graph-plotting software: read the outputs listed in 1.1 and common abbreviations such as df (degrees of freedom), SE (standard error), Coef (coefficient), N (sample size), StDev and p.

#### Explained

**Filter versus sort.** Sorting reorders all records; filtering hides records that do not meet a condition. Neither changes the data.

**Query by grouping.** "Group by Region, count Customer_ID" returns one row per region with the number of customers in it. "Group by Region, sum Spend" gives total spend per region.

**Join.** If a Sales table has fields Shop_ID and Amount, and a Shops table has Shop_ID and Town, joining on Shop_ID lets you total Amount by Town.

#### Worked example

A database table Results has fields Student_ID, Subject and Mark, with 1,200 records. A second table Students has Student_ID, Year and Gender. Describe how to find the mean Statistics mark for each year group.

1. Join Results to Students on Student_ID, so each record has a Year.
2. Filter Subject = "Statistics".
3. Group by Year and use an average function on Mark.
4. The output is one row per year group with its mean mark.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing a field (a variable) with a record (one case).
- Taking every value from a software output instead of the one the question needs.

### Check questions

1. A histogram of masses has a bar for 40–60 g of height 1.8 and a bar for 60–65 g of height 6.0. Which class has more items, and by how many? [3 marks]
2. A scatter diagram shows price against age for 60 used cars, coloured by three makes. Give two things to look for in the diagram. [2 marks]
3. A bar chart compares two exam pass rates, 81% and 84%, with the axis starting at 78%. Explain how it misleads. [2 marks]
4. Eight weights (kg) are 3.2, 3.5, 3.6, 3.6, 3.8, 4.0, 4.1, 5.9. Find the median, the quartiles and test 5.9 for being an outlier with the IQR rule. [4 marks]
5. For the data in question 4, find the mean and the sample standard deviation s, and test 5.9 with the μ ± 3σ rule using these estimates. [4 marks]
6. The weights in question 4 are of newborn babies. Discuss whether 5.9 kg should be removed. [2 marks]
7. Give a reason why a box plot might be a poor choice to show 15 values that form two clear clusters. [1 mark]
8. A table Orders has fields Order_ID, Customer_ID, Date, Value. Describe a query to find the total value of orders for each customer. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 40–60: 1.8 × 20 = 36 (1); 60–65: 6.0 × 5 = 30 (1); 40–60 has 6 more (1).
2. Any two: whether makes form separate clusters; whether price falls with age in each make; differences in gradient or spread between makes; outliers (2).
3. Visible bar heights 3 and 6 suggest one rate is double the other (1), but 84 ÷ 81 ≈ 1.04, only about 4% higher (1).
4. Median = (3.6 + 3.8) ÷ 2 = 3.7 (1); LQ = (3.5 + 3.6) ÷ 2 = 3.55, UQ = (4.0 + 4.1) ÷ 2 = 4.05 (1); fence 4.05 + 1.5 × 0.5 = 4.8 (1); 5.9 > 4.8 so outlier (1).
5. Mean = 31.7 ÷ 8 = 3.9625 (1); s = 0.8331 (1); 3.9625 + 3 × 0.8331 = 6.46 (1); 5.9 < 6.46, not an outlier by this rule (1).
6. 5.9 kg is heavy but possible for a newborn (1), so it is likely a genuine value and should be kept unless there is evidence of a recording error (1).
7. A box plot shows only five summary values, so the two clusters (gap in the middle) are hidden (1).
8. Group by Customer_ID (1) and sum Value (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/statistics/03-probability-and-bayes-theorem.md -->

## Probability and Bayes' theorem

This file covers set notation in probability, tree diagrams, Venn diagrams and two-way tables, the addition and multiplication laws, conditional probability, testing independence, and Bayes' theorem for up to three events.

### Overview

Topic 2 matches the probability in A-level Mathematics, written up in [A-level Maths 21](../maths/21-probability.md) (mutually exclusive and independent events, conditional probability, modelling). This file restates the 9ST0 points briefly with fresh examples and adds topic 11, Bayes' theorem, which reverses a conditional probability: from P(evidence | cause) to P(cause | evidence). Bayes questions are usually set in medical testing, quality control or forensic contexts, and often ask you to interpret a surprisingly small answer.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 9ST0 topics 2 and 11 are on Paper 1. The complement of A is written A′.
- The formulae P(A ∩ B) = P(A)P(B), P(A | B) = P(A) and P(B | A) = P(B) for independent events must be learnt. Bayes' theorem for up to three events is given in the formulae booklet.

### 2.1 and 2.2 Set language, diagrams and tables

*Refs: Edexcel 2.1, 2.2. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- A ∩ B (A and B), A ∪ B (A or B or both), A′ (not A), P(A | B) (A given B), ∅ (the empty set), and the sample space S.
- Represent probabilities with Venn diagrams, tree diagrams and two-way tables, and read probabilities from them.

#### Explained

A Venn diagram suits two or three overlapping events. A tree diagram suits events in stages, where later probabilities may depend on earlier outcomes. A two-way table suits counts classified by two categorical variables; conditional probabilities come from a single row or column.

#### Worked example

In a sixth form of 200 students, 120 study a science (S) and 70 study a language (L); 35 study both. Find P(S ∪ L), P(S′ ∩ L′) and P(L | S).

1. Only S: 120 − 35 = 85. Only L: 70 − 35 = 35. Neither: 200 − 85 − 35 − 35 = 45.
2. P(S ∪ L) = (85 + 35 + 35) ÷ 200 = 155 ÷ 200 = 0.775.
3. P(S′ ∩ L′) = 45 ÷ 200 = 0.225.
4. P(L | S) = 35 ÷ 120 = 0.2917.

#### Common mistakes

- Putting 120 in the "S only" region instead of 120 − 35.
- Dividing by 200 instead of 120 for a "given S" probability.

### 2.3 and 2.4 Calculating probabilities and the laws of probability

*Refs: Edexcel 2.3, 2.4. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B)
```

```latex
P(A \cap B) = P(A) \times P(B \mid A), \qquad P(B \mid A) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(A)}
```

- Mutually exclusive: P(A ∩ B) = 0, so P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B).
- Independent: P(A ∩ B) = P(A)P(B).

#### Explained

Mutually exclusive means the events cannot happen together; independent means one happening does not change the chance of the other. Two events with non-zero probabilities cannot be both: if they are mutually exclusive, knowing A happened tells you B did not.

#### Worked example

A bag has 5 red and 3 blue counters. Two are taken without replacement. Find the probability they are different colours, and the probability the second is blue given they are different colours.

1. P(R then B) = (5/8)(3/7) = 15/56. P(B then R) = (3/8)(5/7) = 15/56.
2. P(different) = 30/56 = 15/28 = 0.5357.
3. P(second blue | different) = (15/56) ÷ (30/56) = 0.5.

#### Common mistakes

- Multiplying probabilities for events that are not independent without using the conditional probability.

### 2.5 Deciding whether events are independent

*Refs: Edexcel 2.5. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- A and B are statistically independent if any one of these holds (each implies the others): P(A ∩ B) = P(A)P(B); P(A | B) = P(A); P(B | A) = P(B).

#### Explained

In a real data set, observed proportions will rarely match exactly even when two variables are unrelated, so in practice a large data set calls for a chi-squared test for association (see [10-contingency-tables-and-goodness-of-fit.md](10-contingency-tables-and-goodness-of-fit.md)). For probability models and textbook data, check the condition exactly.

#### Worked example

P(A) = 0.4, P(B) = 0.35 and P(A ∪ B) = 0.61. Are A and B independent?

1. P(A ∩ B) = 0.4 + 0.35 − 0.61 = 0.14.
2. P(A) × P(B) = 0.4 × 0.35 = 0.14.
3. They are equal, so A and B are independent.

#### Common mistakes

- Concluding independence from P(A ∩ B) = 0; that shows mutual exclusivity, which (with non-zero probabilities) means the events are dependent.

### 11.1 Bayes' theorem

*Refs: Edexcel 11.1. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Calculate and use conditional probabilities, including Bayes' theorem for up to three events (a partition A₁, A₂, A₃ of the sample space), using tree diagrams.

```latex
P(A_j \mid B) = \frac{P(A_j)\, P(B \mid A_j)}{\sum_{i} P(A_i)\, P(B \mid A_i)}
```

- The denominator is the total probability of B, found by adding the branches of a tree that end in B.

#### Explained

The tree is drawn in the order the information is given: first the cause (which machine, whether a person has a disease), then the evidence (defective item, positive test). Bayes asks the reverse question: given the evidence, how likely is each cause?

**Base rates matter.** When a condition is rare, most positive results can be false positives even for an accurate test, because the many healthy people produce more false positives than the few ill people produce true positives. This is why screening programmes follow a positive result with a second, more specific test.

**Updating.** The answer P(A | B) becomes the new prior if more evidence arrives. Two independent positive tests raise the probability far more than one.

#### Worked example

Three machines make bolts: A makes 50%, B 30% and C 20%. The proportions defective are 2%, 3% and 5%. A bolt is chosen at random and is defective. Find the probability it came from C.

1. Branches ending in defective: 0.5 × 0.02 = 0.010; 0.3 × 0.03 = 0.009; 0.2 × 0.05 = 0.010.
2. P(D) = 0.010 + 0.009 + 0.010 = 0.029.

```latex
P(C \mid D) = \frac{0.010}{0.029} = 0.345
```

3. C makes only 20% of bolts but about 34.5% of defective ones.

A disease affects 1% of a population. A test detects it in 95% of people who have it and gives a false positive for 4% of people who do not. Find the probability that a person who tests positive has the disease, and interpret.

1. P(D ∩ +) = 0.01 × 0.95 = 0.0095. P(D′ ∩ +) = 0.99 × 0.04 = 0.0396.
2. P(+) = 0.0095 + 0.0396 = 0.0491.

```latex
P(D \mid +) = \frac{0.0095}{0.0491} = 0.193
```

3. Fewer than 1 in 5 positives actually have the disease, because the disease is rare and false positives from the large healthy group outnumber true positives.

The person takes a second, independent test with the same accuracy and is positive again. Find the new probability.

1. Prior is now 0.193. P(D ∩ +) = 0.193 × 0.95 = 0.1838; P(D′ ∩ +) = 0.807 × 0.04 = 0.0323.
2. P(D | second +) = 0.1838 ÷ (0.1838 + 0.0323) = 0.851.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing P(+ | D) (the test's sensitivity, 0.95) with P(D | +) (0.193).
- Using only the "true" branch in the denominator and forgetting the false positives.
- Rounding the prior heavily before a second update.

### Check questions

1. P(A) = 0.5, P(B) = 0.3, P(A | B) = 0.6. Find P(A ∩ B) and P(A ∪ B), and state with a reason whether A and B are independent. [4 marks]
2. In a survey of 150 adults, 90 own a car, 60 own a bicycle and 25 own neither. Find the probability that an adult chosen at random owns both, and the probability they own a bicycle given they own a car. [4 marks]
3. Show that two events with P(A) = 0.2 and P(B) = 0.3 cannot be both mutually exclusive and independent. [2 marks]
4. A factory has three suppliers. 60% of parts come from X, 25% from Y and 15% from Z. The probabilities of a faulty part are 0.01, 0.04 and 0.06. A part is faulty. Find the probability it came from Y. [4 marks]
5. A security scanner flags 98% of bags containing a banned item and 3% of bags that do not. 1 in 500 bags contains a banned item. Find the probability that a flagged bag contains a banned item, and comment. [5 marks]
6. In question 5, the flagged bag is scanned again by an independent scanner with the same accuracy, and is flagged again. Find the new probability. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. P(A ∩ B) = 0.6 × 0.3 = 0.18 (1); P(A ∪ B) = 0.5 + 0.3 − 0.18 = 0.62 (1); P(A | B) = 0.6 ≠ P(A) = 0.5 (1), so not independent (1).
2. Car or bicycle: 150 − 25 = 125 (1); both = 90 + 60 − 125 = 25, P = 25/150 = 1/6 (1); P(bicycle | car) = 25/90 (1) = 5/18 ≈ 0.278 (1).
3. Mutually exclusive gives P(A ∩ B) = 0 (1); independent gives P(A ∩ B) = 0.06 ≠ 0, a contradiction (1).
4. Branches: 0.006, 0.010, 0.009 (1); P(F) = 0.025 (1); P(Y | F) = 0.010 ÷ 0.025 (1) = 0.4 (1).
5. P(B ∩ flag) = 0.002 × 0.98 = 0.00196 (1); P(B′ ∩ flag) = 0.998 × 0.03 = 0.02994 (1); P(flag) = 0.0319 (1); P(B | flag) = 0.0614 (1); most flagged bags are innocent because banned items are rare, so a flag should lead to a manual check, not an accusation (1).
6. Prior 0.0614: 0.0614 × 0.98 = 0.0602 (1); 0.9386 × 0.03 = 0.0282 (1); 0.0602 ÷ 0.0884 = 0.681 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/statistics/04-populations-and-sampling.md -->

## Populations and sampling

This file covers simple and unrestricted random samples, choosing a random sample with random number tables or a calculator, the sampling methods named in the spec (simple random, systematic, cluster, judgmental, snowball, and stratification in proportional and disproportional ratios), their strengths and weaknesses, choosing a method in context, and the practical limits on collecting unbiased data.

### Overview

Populations, sampling frames, simple random, systematic, stratified, quota, opportunity and cluster sampling are written up in [A-level Maths 19](../maths/19-statistical-sampling.md), with GCSE-level work in [GCSE Statistics 03](../../gcse/statistics/03-population-and-sampling.md). A-level Statistics adds the formal definition of a random sample (with and without replacement), random number tables, judgmental and snowball sampling, disproportional stratification with weighting, and reasoned choices for named contexts such as exit polls and quality assurance.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 9ST0 topic 3 is on Paper 1.
- The spec names judgmental and snowball sampling and disproportional stratification, which are not in A-level Mathematics. Quota and opportunity sampling are not named in 9ST0 topic 3 but may still be met as context.

### 3.1 Simple and unrestricted random samples

*Refs: Edexcel 3.1. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- A simple random sample of size n is taken without replacement: each member can appear at most once.
- An unrestricted random sample is taken with replacement: a member can be picked more than once.
- For a random sample of size n: every member of the population is equally likely to be included, every subset of size n is possible, and (for a simple random sample) every possible sample of size n is equally likely.

#### Explained

The third condition is the strong one. Systematic sampling gives every member an equal chance of being included, but only a few samples are possible (once the start is chosen, the rest are fixed), so it is not a simple random sample.

With replacement, each draw is independent and has the same probabilities, which makes the maths simpler. In practice, sampling without replacement from a large population is almost the same, because removing one member barely changes the population.

#### Worked example

A population has 6 members. How many different samples of size 2 are possible (a) without replacement, (b) with replacement, counting order? Explain why systematic sampling of 2 from 6 (every third member) is not a simple random sample.

1. (a) Choose 2 from 6:

```latex
\binom{6}{2} = 15
```

Each of the 15 samples has probability 1/15 in a simple random sample.

2. (b) 6 choices for each draw: 6 × 6 = 36 ordered samples.
3. Systematic: start at 1, 2 or 3, giving only the samples {1, 4}, {2, 5}, {3, 6}. Each member is equally likely (1/3) to be chosen, but 12 of the 15 subsets are impossible.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "every member has an equal chance" is enough to define a random sample.

### 3.2 Using random number tables or a calculator

*Refs: Edexcel 3.2. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Number the sampling frame (for example 001 to 750). Read random digits in groups of the same length, ignoring numbers outside the range and, for a simple random sample, ignoring repeats.
- A calculator's random integer function (for example RanInt#(1, 750)) does the same job; again, discard repeats for sampling without replacement.

#### Explained

Decide in advance where to start in the table and in which direction to read, so you cannot pick a "nice-looking" start. Using three digits for a frame of 750 wastes the numbers 751 to 999 and 000, which is fine; the remaining numbers are still equally likely.

#### Worked example

A frame is numbered 001 to 750. The random digits are 4729 1803 5472 6648 0112 9. Choose a simple random sample of 4.

1. Group in threes: 472, 918, 035, 472, 664, 801, 129.
2. 472 (take), 918 (too big, skip), 035 (take), 472 (repeat, skip), 664 (take), 801 (skip), 129 (take).
3. Sample: 472, 35, 664, 129.

#### Common mistakes

- Keeping a repeated number in a sample without replacement.
- Reading digit groups of the wrong length.

### 3.3 Random and non-random sampling methods

*Refs: Edexcel 3.3. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- **Simple random:** every possible sample of size n is equally likely; needs a full sampling frame.
- **Systematic:** choose a random start between 1 and k, then every kth member, where k = N ÷ n.
- **Cluster:** split the population into naturally occurring groups (schools, streets, batches), choose some clusters at random, then sample all (or a random sample) of each chosen cluster.
- **Judgmental (purposive):** the researcher uses expert judgement to choose members thought to be typical or informative. Non-random.
- **Snowball:** start with a few members of a hard-to-reach population, who recruit others they know, and so on. Non-random. Used for groups with no sampling frame, for example drug users or people with a rare condition.
- **Stratification** (done before sampling): split the population into strata (age groups, departments), then sample randomly within each.
  - Proportional: each stratum's sample size is in the same ratio as its size in the population.
  - Disproportional: some strata are sampled more heavily (often small strata, so there are enough to analyse); estimates for the whole population must then be weighted by the stratum sizes.

```latex
n_h = \frac{N_h}{N} \times n \qquad \text{(proportional allocation)}
```

```latex
\bar{x}_{\text{weighted}} = \frac{\sum N_h \bar{x}_h}{N} \qquad \text{(combining disproportional strata)}
```

#### Explained

**Cluster versus stratified.** Strata are different from each other and each is sampled; clusters are meant to be similar mini-populations and only some are sampled. Stratifying improves precision; clustering saves travel and cost but usually reduces precision, because people in the same cluster tend to be alike.

**Why disproportional?** If a company has only 200 night-shift workers out of 2,000, a proportional sample of 50 gives only 5 night workers, too few to say anything about them. Sampling 30 from each shift lets each group be studied, but the night shift is over-represented, so overall averages must be weighted.

#### Worked example

A firm has 1,200 day staff, 600 evening staff and 200 night staff. (a) Find a proportional stratified sample of 50. (b) Instead 30 are sampled from each shift, with mean overtime hours 4.2, 5.1 and 7.8. Estimate the mean for the whole firm.

1. (a) N = 2000. Day: 1200/2000 × 50 = 30. Evening: 600/2000 × 50 = 15. Night: 200/2000 × 50 = 5.
2. (b) The plain average (4.2 + 5.1 + 7.8) ÷ 3 = 5.7 over-weights the night shift.

```latex
\bar{x} = \frac{1200(4.2) + 600(5.1) + 200(7.8)}{2000} = \frac{9660}{2000} = 4.83 \text{ hours}
```

#### Common mistakes

- Calling cluster sampling "stratified": in cluster sampling only some groups are sampled.
- Forgetting to weight results from a disproportional stratified sample.
- Describing snowball sampling as random.

### 3.4 Advantages and limitations

*Refs: Edexcel 3.4. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

| Method | Advantages | Limitations |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Simple random | free of selection bias; theory of tests applies | needs a full frame; can be costly; may by chance miss small groups |
| Systematic | simple, spreads the sample over the frame | needs a frame; biased if the list has a pattern with period k |
| Cluster | cheap and practical when the population is spread out; frame only needed for chosen clusters | less precise; clusters may differ from each other |
| Judgmental | quick, uses expertise, good for pilots | not random; depends on the judge; bias cannot be measured |
| Snowball | reaches hidden populations with no frame | not random; people recruit similar people; isolated members missed |
| Stratified | every stratum represented; more precise | needs strata sizes and a frame for each stratum; strata must not overlap |

#### Explained

Only random methods allow the sampling error to be measured (standard errors, confidence intervals, tests). That is the main reason to prefer them when a frame exists.

#### Worked example

A list of 1,000 flats is ordered floor by floor, 10 flats per floor, with flat 1 on each floor being the corner flat. A sample of 100 is taken by every 10th flat. Explain the risk.

1. k = 1000 ÷ 100 = 10, which matches the 10 flats per floor.
2. Every chosen flat will be in the same position on its floor (for example all corner flats), which may differ in size, rent or noise. The sample is biased by the list's pattern.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying systematic sampling is "random" without qualification.

### 3.5 Choosing a method in context

*Refs: Edexcel 3.5. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Choose and justify a method for a given context, such as market research, exit polls, experiments and quality assurance.

#### Explained

- **Market research:** often quota or stratified samples by age and gender; stratified random is better if a frame exists.
- **Exit polls:** cluster sampling of polling stations (chosen at random, often stratified by region or past voting pattern), then systematic sampling of every kth voter leaving, which avoids the interviewer choosing whom to ask.
- **Experiments:** random allocation of available subjects to treatments matters more than how the subjects were found (see [12-experimental-design.md](12-experimental-design.md)).
- **Quality assurance:** systematic sampling from a production line (every 50th item) is simple and spreads checks over time; random sampling within each hour (stratified by time) protects against a machine cycle matching k.

#### Worked example

A charity wants to survey people who sleep rough in a large city about access to health care. Recommend a method.

1. There is no list of rough sleepers, so simple random, systematic and stratified sampling are not possible.
2. Snowball sampling: start with contacts made through shelters and outreach workers, who introduce others.
3. Limitation: people connected to shelters may have better health care access than those who avoid them, so the sample may be biased; the report should say so.

#### Common mistakes

- Recommending a method that needs a sampling frame when none exists.

### 3.6 Practical constraints on collecting unbiased data

*Refs: Edexcel 3.6. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Constraints include cost, time, no available or out-of-date sampling frame, non-response, access, ethics and consent, and destructive testing (testing that destroys the item, so a census is impossible).

#### Explained

Non-response is a bias, not just a smaller sample: people who reply to a survey on working hours may be those with the most time. Remedies include follow-up reminders, short questionnaires, incentives and comparing respondents with the population on known variables. An out-of-date frame (last year's electoral roll) misses people who have just moved, often younger people.

#### Worked example

A random sample of 400 households is sent a postal survey on internet use; 160 reply. Explain why the results may be biased and give one improvement.

1. Response rate 160 ÷ 400 = 40%. Households who do not reply may differ systematically, for example busier households or those less comfortable with forms.
2. Improvement: follow up non-responders by phone or in person, or offer an online option and compare the age profile of responders with census figures.

#### Common mistakes

- Treating non-response as only a loss of sample size.

### Check questions

1. Explain the difference between a simple random sample and an unrestricted random sample. [2 marks]
2. Members of a club are numbered 01 to 80. Use the random digits 8 3 1 7 9 0 0 5 1 7 4 4 6 2 to choose a simple random sample of 4. [3 marks]
3. A school has 420 pupils in Key Stage 3, 300 in Key Stage 4 and 180 in the sixth form. Find the numbers from each in a proportional stratified sample of 60. [3 marks]
4. A disproportional sample of 20 per key stage in question 3 gives mean screen times of 3.5, 4.0 and 4.6 hours. Estimate the mean screen time for the school. [3 marks]
5. Explain why an exit poll might use cluster sampling followed by systematic sampling. [3 marks]
6. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of judgmental sampling. [2 marks]
7. A light bulb maker tests lifetimes by running bulbs until they fail. Explain why a sample is used rather than a census. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. Simple random: without replacement, each member at most once (1); unrestricted: with replacement, so a member may be chosen more than once (1).
2. Pairs 83, 17, 90, 05, 17, 44, 62 (1); reject 83 and 90 (out of range) and the repeated 17 (1); sample 17, 05, 44, 62 (1).
3. N = 900 (1); 420/900 × 60 = 28, 300/900 × 60 = 20 (1); 180/900 × 60 = 12 (1).
4. Weighted: (420 × 3.5 + 300 × 4.0 + 180 × 4.6) ÷ 900 (1) = (1470 + 1200 + 828) ÷ 900 (1) = 3.89 hours (1).
5. No list of voters leaving is available (1); polling stations are natural clusters, so only some need staffing, saving cost (1); asking every kth voter removes interviewer choice of whom to ask (1).
6. Advantage: quick, uses expert knowledge, good for a pilot (1). Disadvantage: not random, depends on the judge's bias, sampling error cannot be estimated (1).
7. Testing destroys the bulbs, so a census would leave none to sell (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/statistics/05-random-variables-binomial-and-normal.md -->

## Random variables, binomial and normal distributions

This file covers the language of random variables, discrete distributions with their expectation and variance, the properties of continuous distributions and the rectangular (uniform) distribution, the binomial distribution with its mean and variance, the normal distribution (properties, the 1, 2 and 3 standard deviation rules, probabilities, unknown parameters, the distribution of the sample mean, and the normal approximation to the binomial), and choosing between binomial, normal, Poisson and exponential models.

### Overview

The binomial and normal distributions are written up in [A-level Maths 22](../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md); expectation and variance of a discrete random variable and the binomial mean and variance in [Further Maths 30](../further-maths/30-discrete-random-variables.md); continuous distributions and the rectangular distribution in [Further Maths 35](../further-maths/35-continuous-random-variables.md). A-level Statistics needs no calculus here: continuous distributions are met through their graphs, and the normal is used through a calculator. It adds the Edexcel 2/3, 95%, 99.8% rule, the sample mean result, and specific conditions for the normal approximation to the binomial.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 9ST0 topics 4, 5, 6 and 12.1 are on Paper 1.
- E(X) = Σxp and Var(X) = E(X²) − μ² must be learnt; the binomial formula, mean np and variance np(1 − p) are in the formulae booklet.
- Edexcel 6.2 quotes approximately 2/3 within μ ± σ, 95% within μ ± 2σ and 99.8% within μ ± 3σ (the exact value is 99.73%).
- Edexcel 6.6 gives the conditions for the normal approximation to the binomial as n ≥ 20 and p ≈ 0.5, or np > 10 or n(1 − p) > 10, with a continuity correction.

### 4.1 to 4.3 Random variables and discrete distributions

*Refs: Edexcel 4.1, 4.2, 4.3. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- A random variable takes numerical values determined by chance. It is discrete if it takes separate (countable) values, and continuous if it can take any value in a range.
- Two random variables are independent if the value of one does not affect the probabilities of the other; otherwise they are dependent.
- A discrete distribution can be given in words, a table or a probability function; the probabilities sum to 1.

```latex
E(X) = \mu = \sum x_i p_i, \qquad \operatorname{Var}(X) = \sigma^2 = \sum x_i^2 p_i - \mu^2 = E(X^2) - \mu^2
```

- Use discrete random variables to model real situations, such as winnings in a game or the number of items bought.

#### Explained

E(X) is the long-run mean: the average value over many repetitions. Var(X) measures spread around it; the standard deviation σ = √Var(X) is in the same units as X.

#### Worked example

X has distribution P(X = 0) = 0.1, P(X = 1) = 0.3, P(X = 2) = 0.4, P(X = 3) = 0.2. Find E(X), Var(X) and the standard deviation.

1. E(X) = 0(0.1) + 1(0.3) + 2(0.4) + 3(0.2) = 1.7.
2. E(X²) = 0 + 1(0.3) + 4(0.4) + 9(0.2) = 3.7.
3. Var(X) = 3.7 − 1.7² = 3.7 − 2.89 = 0.81, so σ = 0.9.

A charity game costs £2. A player wins £10 with probability 0.05, £3 with probability 0.2, and nothing otherwise. Find the expected profit per game for the charity.

1. Player's expected winnings: 10(0.05) + 3(0.2) = 0.5 + 0.6 = £1.10.
2. Charity's expected profit per game: 2 − 1.10 = £0.90.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to subtract μ² when finding the variance.
- Leaving the variance as the answer when the standard deviation is asked for.

### 4.4 to 4.6 Continuous distributions and graphs of distributions

*Refs: Edexcel 4.4, 4.5, 4.6. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- A continuous distribution is described by a curve (a probability density function) with total area 1 under it; probabilities are areas; P(X = a) = 0 for any single value, so P(X < a) = P(X ≤ a).
- Interpret a bar chart or table of a discrete distribution: mode, symmetry or skew, where most probability lies.
- Interpret a rectangular (continuous uniform) distribution on [a, b]: a horizontal line of height 1/(b − a), mean (a + b)/2 by symmetry, and probability = width × height.

#### Explained

The bar chart of B(10, 0.3) peaks at 3 and has a longer tail to the right, so it is positively skewed; B(10, 0.5) is symmetrical. The rectangle of a uniform distribution says every equal-width interval inside [a, b] is equally likely, which fits rounding errors and arrival times within a known window.

#### Worked example

A train is equally likely to arrive at any time between 2 and 10 minutes after 08:00. Find the height of the rectangle, the probability it arrives between 3 and 6 minutes past, and the mean arrival time.

1. Width 10 − 2 = 8, so height = 1/8.
2. P(3 < T < 6) = (6 − 3) × 1/8 = 3/8 = 0.375.
3. Mean = (2 + 10) ÷ 2 = 6 minutes past 08:00.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving a non-zero probability to a single value of a continuous variable.

### 5.1 to 5.3 The binomial distribution

*Refs: Edexcel 5.1, 5.2, 5.3. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- X ~ B(n, p) when there is a fixed number n of trials, each with two outcomes, a constant probability p of success, and independent trials.

```latex
P(X = x) = \binom{n}{x} p^x (1-p)^{n-x}, \qquad E(X) = np, \qquad \operatorname{Var}(X) = np(1-p)
```

- Use the formula, cumulative tables or a calculator (advised) for probabilities. You do not need to derive the mean and variance.

#### Explained

Modelling assumptions should be criticised in context. Twenty patients in one ward catching an infection are not independent; the chance of a free throw being scored may change as a player tires. If the sample is taken without replacement from a small population, p changes from trial to trial, so the binomial is only an approximation.

The variance np(1 − p) is largest when p = 0.5 and shrinks as p moves towards 0 or 1.

#### Worked example

40% of customers at a café buy a cake. In a random sample of 15, find P(X = 6), P(X ≤ 4), P(X ≥ 8), the mean and the standard deviation.

1. X ~ B(15, 0.4).

```latex
P(X = 6) = \binom{15}{6}(0.4)^6(0.6)^9 = 0.2066
```

2. P(X ≤ 4) = 0.2173 (calculator or tables).
3. P(X ≥ 8) = 1 − P(X ≤ 7) = 1 − 0.7869 = 0.2131.
4. Mean = 15 × 0.4 = 6. Variance = 6 × 0.6 = 3.6, so standard deviation = 1.897.

#### Common mistakes

- P(X ≥ 8) = 1 − P(X ≤ 8); the correct form is 1 − P(X ≤ 7).
- Giving the variance np instead of np(1 − p).

### 6.1 to 6.4 The normal distribution

*Refs: Edexcel 6.1, 6.2, 6.3, 6.4. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- X ~ N(μ, σ²): continuous, bell-shaped, symmetrical about μ (mean = median = mode), with almost all values within μ ± 3σ.
- Data from a normal population will look roughly like this, but each sample varies; a histogram of a small sample can look irregular even when the population is normal.
- Approximately 2/3 of observations lie within μ ± σ, 95% within μ ± 2σ, and 99.8% within μ ± 3σ.
- Find probabilities with a calculator (advised) or by standardising. Find an unknown μ or σ (or both) from given probabilities using inverse normal values.

```latex
Z = \frac{X - \mu}{\sigma} \sim N(0, 1)
```

#### Explained

**Checking a normal model from data.** If roughly 2/3 of a data set is within one standard deviation of the mean and about 95% within two, and the histogram is roughly symmetrical, a normal model is reasonable. If the mean minus two standard deviations is negative for a quantity that cannot be negative (times, masses), the data are probably skewed.

**Unknown parameters.** Each given probability becomes an equation in z. One unknown needs one equation; two unknowns need two equations, solved simultaneously.

#### Worked example

Adult heights in a group are modelled by N(170, 8²) cm. Use the 1-standard-deviation rule to describe the middle of the distribution, then find P(H > 180).

1. About 2/3 of heights lie between 170 − 8 = 162 and 170 + 8 = 178 cm. (Exact: 0.6827.)
2. P(H > 180): z = (180 − 170) ÷ 8 = 1.25, P(Z > 1.25) = 0.1056.

Bags of rice have masses with σ = 4 g, and 10% weigh less than 50 g. Find μ.

```latex
\frac{50 - \mu}{4} = -1.2816 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \mu = 50 + 5.126 = 55.1 \text{ g}
```

X is normal with P(X < 20) = 0.15 and P(X > 40) = 0.2. Find μ and σ.

1. z-values: Φ⁻¹(0.15) = −1.0364 and Φ⁻¹(0.8) = 0.8416.
2. 20 = μ − 1.0364σ and 40 = μ + 0.8416σ.
3. Subtract: 20 = 1.8780σ, so σ = 10.65 and μ = 20 + 1.0364 × 10.65 = 31.0.

#### Common mistakes

- Using σ² in place of σ when standardising: N(170, 64) has σ = 8.
- Using the 0.2 instead of 0.8 for an upper-tail probability without changing the sign of z.

### 6.5 The distribution of the sample mean

*Refs: Edexcel 6.5. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- If X ~ N(μ, σ²) and X̄ is the mean of a random sample of size n, then

```latex
\bar{X} \sim N\!\left(\mu, \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right)
```

- The standard deviation of X̄, σ/√n, is the standard error of the mean.

#### Explained

Averages vary less than single values. With n = 9 the standard error is a third of σ; with n = 100 it is a tenth. If X is not normal, X̄ is still approximately normal for large n by the central limit theorem (see [13-central-limit-theorem-and-confidence-intervals.md](13-central-limit-theorem-and-confidence-intervals.md)).

#### Worked example

Cartons hold X ml of juice, X ~ N(500, 12²). A random sample of 9 cartons is taken. Find the probability the sample mean is below 495 ml.

1. X̄ ~ N(500, 12²/9), so the standard error is 12 ÷ 3 = 4.
2. z = (495 − 500) ÷ 4 = −1.25.
3. P(X̄ < 495) = 0.1056.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing the standard deviation by n instead of √n (or the variance by √n).

### 6.6 The normal approximation to the binomial

*Refs: Edexcel 6.6. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- B(n, p) may be approximated by N(np, np(1 − p)). Edexcel's conditions: n ≥ 20 and p ≈ 0.5, or np > 10 or n(1 − p) > 10. In practice the approximation is best when both np and n(1 − p) are large.
- Use a continuity correction: a discrete value x covers the interval x − 0.5 to x + 0.5.

| Binomial | Normal |
| --- | --- |
| P(X ≤ 30) | P(Y < 30.5) |
| P(X < 30) | P(Y < 29.5) |
| P(X ≥ 30) | P(Y > 29.5) |
| P(X = 30) | P(29.5 < Y < 30.5) |

#### Explained

Calculators now give exact binomial probabilities, so the approximation matters mainly for hand calculation, large n, and as the basis of the z-test for a proportion (see [09-hypothesis-testing-principles-and-one-sample-tests.md](09-hypothesis-testing-principles-and-one-sample-tests.md)).

#### Worked example

X ~ B(60, 0.45). Use a normal approximation to find P(X ≤ 30) and compare with the exact value.

1. np = 27 and n(1 − p) = 33, both above 10. Variance = 60 × 0.45 × 0.55 = 14.85.
2. Y ~ N(27, 14.85). P(X ≤ 30) ≈ P(Y < 30.5).

```latex
z = \frac{30.5 - 27}{\sqrt{14.85}} = 0.908, \qquad P(Z < 0.908) = 0.818
```

3. Exact binomial: 0.8183. The approximation is very close.

#### Common mistakes

- Leaving out the continuity correction, or applying it in the wrong direction.

### 12.1 Choosing a distribution

*Refs: Edexcel 12.1. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- Know when binomial, normal, Poisson and exponential models are appropriate and valid in a real situation, and give reasons.

#### Explained

| Model | Use for | Key conditions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| binomial B(n, p) | number of successes in n trials | fixed n, two outcomes, constant p, independent trials |
| normal N(μ, σ²) | continuous measurements | symmetrical, bell-shaped, about 95% within 2σ |
| Poisson Po(λ) | number of events in a fixed interval of time or space | events occur singly, independently, at a constant average rate; mean ≈ variance |
| exponential Exp(λ) | time (or distance) between Poisson events | constant rate, memoryless waiting |

The Poisson and exponential distributions are in [06-poisson-and-exponential-distributions.md](06-poisson-and-exponential-distributions.md).

#### Worked example

Suggest a model for each, with a reason: (a) the number of faulty screens in a batch of 50 when 3% are faulty; (b) the number of emails arriving in an hour; (c) the time until the next email; (d) the masses of apples from one orchard.

1. (a) B(50, 0.03): fixed number of screens, each faulty or not, independently with constant probability.
2. (b) Poisson: a count of events in a fixed time, arriving singly at a roughly constant rate.
3. (c) Exponential: the waiting time between Poisson events.
4. (d) Normal: a continuous measurement, likely symmetrical about a mean.

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing binomial for a count with no fixed number of trials.

### Check questions

1. X takes values 1, 2, 3, 4 with probabilities 0.2, 0.3, 0.4, 0.1. Find E(X) and Var(X). [4 marks]
2. A delivery is equally likely to arrive at any time between 10:00 and 10:40. Find the probability it arrives between 10:25 and 10:40. [2 marks]
3. 20% of plants of a variety have white flowers. In a random sample of 25, find P(X = 5), P(X ≤ 3), P(X ≥ 7), and the mean and variance of X. [6 marks]
4. Lengths of fish are N(65, 4²) cm. Without a calculator, estimate the proportion between 57 and 73 cm. Then find P(L < 60) and P(62 < L < 70). [5 marks]
5. The weights of a product are normal with mean 250 g and standard deviation 6 g. A sample of 16 is taken. Find P(X̄ > 253). [3 marks]
6. Y ~ B(80, 0.4). Use a normal approximation to estimate P(Y > 35), justifying its use. [5 marks]
7. Give a reason why a normal model would not be suitable for the number of children in UK households. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. E(X) = 0.2 + 0.6 + 1.2 + 0.4 = 2.4 (1); E(X²) = 0.2 + 1.2 + 3.6 + 1.6 = 6.6 (1); Var(X) = 6.6 − 5.76 (1) = 0.84 (1).
2. Height 1/40 (1); 15/40 = 0.375 (1).
3. B(25, 0.2) (1); P(X = 5) = 0.1960 (1); P(X ≤ 3) = 0.2340 (1); P(X ≥ 7) = 1 − P(X ≤ 6) = 0.2200 (1); mean 5 (1); variance 4 (1).
4. 57 and 73 are μ ± 2σ, so about 95% (1); P(L < 60): z = −1.25 (1), 0.1056 (1); P(62 < L < 70): z from −0.75 to 1.25 (1), 0.6677 (1).
5. X̄ ~ N(250, 6²/16), SE = 1.5 (1); z = 2 (1); 0.0228 (1).
6. np = 32 > 10 and n(1 − p) = 48 > 10 (1); N(32, 19.2) (1); P(Y > 35) ≈ P(W > 35.5) (1); z = 0.799 (1); 0.212 (1).
7. It is discrete with small whole-number values and positively skewed (it cannot be negative) (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/statistics/06-poisson-and-exponential-distributions.md -->

## The Poisson and exponential distributions

This file covers when a Poisson model is appropriate, Poisson probabilities with their mean and variance, when an exponential model is appropriate and how it links to the Poisson as the time between randomly occurring events, and exponential probabilities with their mean and variance.

### Overview

The Poisson distribution is written up in [Further Maths 31](../further-maths/31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md) and the exponential distribution (with calculus) in [Further Maths 35](../further-maths/35-continuous-random-variables.md). A-level Statistics uses them without calculus: you need the conditions, the formulae for probabilities, the means and variances, and above all the link that if events happen as a Poisson process at rate λ, the time between them is exponential with the same λ. Scaling λ to a new interval length is the most common step in these questions.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 9ST0 topic 18 is on Paper 1; goodness of fit tests for these distributions are topic 19 on Paper 2 (see [10-contingency-tables-and-goodness-of-fit.md](10-contingency-tables-and-goodness-of-fit.md)).
- You do not need to derive the means and variances. The Poisson probability formula is in the formulae booklet, and cumulative Poisson tables are provided; a calculator is advised.

### 18.1 When a Poisson model is appropriate

*Refs: Edexcel 18.1, 12.1. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- X ~ Po(λ) models the number of events in a fixed interval of time or space when events occur:
  - singly (no two at exactly the same moment or place);
  - independently of each other;
  - at random, at a constant average rate λ per interval.
- A sign that a Poisson model may fit data: the sample mean and sample variance are close.

#### Explained

Think about each condition in context. Customers arriving at a shop are not independent if they come in families; calls to a helpline are not at a constant rate across the day; breakdowns on a motorway may cluster in bad weather. A Poisson model can still be reasonable over a short period when the rate is roughly steady, such as calls between 2 pm and 3 pm on a weekday.

If the variance of the data is much larger than the mean, the events are probably clustered (over-dispersed) and a Poisson model is poor.

#### Worked example

A traffic officer counts cars passing a point in 60 one-minute intervals: the mean is 4.1 per minute and the variance is 4.3. On a second road near a set of traffic lights, the mean is 4.0 and the variance is 11.2. Comment on Poisson models for each.

1. First road: mean ≈ variance, consistent with a Poisson model, and cars on an open road arrive roughly independently.
2. Second road: variance far exceeds the mean. The lights release cars in bunches, so arrivals are not independent and a Poisson model is unsuitable.

#### Common mistakes

- Stating the conditions without applying them to the context.
- Forgetting that the rate must be constant over the interval being modelled.

### 18.2 When an exponential model is appropriate, and its link to the Poisson

*Refs: Edexcel 18.2, 12.1. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

- If events occur as a Poisson process with rate λ per unit time, the time T between consecutive events (or from any moment until the next event) follows the exponential distribution, T ~ Exp(λ).
- The link: "the wait for the next event is longer than t" is the same as "no events in an interval of length t".

```latex
P(T > t) = P(\text{no events in time } t) = e^{-\lambda t}
```

- The same holds in space: the distance between flaws along a cable follows an exponential distribution if flaws occur as a Poisson process along it.

#### Explained

The exponential distribution is continuous, positively skewed, starting at its highest point at t = 0 and decaying. Short waits are the most likely; long waits are possible but rare.

It is memoryless: having already waited 10 minutes does not change the distribution of the remaining wait. That fits random events (radioactive decays, calls to a switchboard), but not things that wear out (the time until a light bulb fails becomes more likely as it ages), so an exponential model of lifetimes assumes no ageing.

#### Worked example

Calls arrive at a helpline as a Poisson process at 3.5 per hour. Find the probability that the gap between two calls is more than 30 minutes, using (a) the exponential distribution, (b) the Poisson distribution.

1. (a) T ~ Exp(3.5), with t = 0.5 hours: P(T > 0.5) = e^(−3.5 × 0.5) = e^(−1.75) = 0.1738.
2. (b) Number of calls in 30 minutes ~ Po(1.75). P(no calls) = e^(−1.75) = 0.1738. The answers agree, as they must.

#### Common mistakes

- Using different time units for λ and t (calls per hour with t in minutes).

### 18.3 Poisson and exponential probabilities, means and variances

*Refs: Edexcel 18.3. Paper 1.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
P(X = x) = \frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^x}{x!}, \quad x = 0, 1, 2, \dots \qquad E(X) = \operatorname{Var}(X) = \lambda
```

```latex
T \sim \operatorname{Exp}(\lambda): \quad P(T > t) = e^{-\lambda t}, \quad P(T \le t) = 1 - e^{-\lambda t}, \quad E(T) = \frac{1}{\lambda}, \quad \operatorname{Var}(T) = \frac{1}{\lambda^2}
```
