- Misreporting includes: treating "not significant" as "no effect"; treating "significant" as "large" or "important"; testing many hypotheses and reporting only the significant ones; changing to a one-tailed test after seeing the data; and claiming cause from an observational study.
- Low-power studies often miss real effects, and the significant results they do find tend to overstate the size of the effect.

#### Explained

If 20 independent tests are each run at 5% when all the null hypotheses are true, the chance of at least one false "significant" result is

```latex
1 - 0.95^{20} = 0.642
```

so a single significant result among many tests is weak evidence. Look for a pre-stated hypothesis, an adequate sample size (high power), a report of the effect size (see [17-effect-size.md](17-effect-size.md)) and a confidence interval, not just a p-value.

#### Worked example

A blog reports: "Scientists tested 20 foods; eating blueberries was linked to better memory (p = 0.04)". Evaluate the claim.

1. With 20 tests at 5%, the probability of at least one false positive is 0.642 if no food has any effect.
2. p = 0.04 is only just below 0.05, so the evidence is weak even for one test.
3. The size of the memory difference is not reported, and "linked" may come from an observational study, so it does not show cause.
4. A new study testing only blueberries, with a pre-stated hypothesis and enough power, would be needed.

#### Common mistakes

- Judging a study only on whether p < 0.05.

### 15.7 Critical regions or p-values

*Refs: Edexcel 15.7. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

- Critical region approach: fix the significance level, find the region, and see whether the test statistic falls in it.
- p-value approach: find the probability of the observed result or more extreme under H₀, and compare with the significance level.
- They always reach the same decision at the same level.
- p-values are not used for correlation coefficient tests in this specification.

#### Explained

| | Critical region | p-value |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Advantages | works with tables; region can be set before collecting data; needed to find Type II errors and power | shows the strength of evidence; lets readers use their own significance level; standard in software output |
| Limitations | gives only reject or not | invites the misuse "p < 0.05 means important" |

#### Worked example

In the compost test, x̄ = 103.5 with n = 50. Show that both approaches give the same decision and say what extra information the p-value gives.

1. Critical region: x̄ > 102.79. 103.5 is in it, so reject H₀.
2. p-value: z = 2.06, P(Z > 2.06) = 0.0196 < 0.05, so reject H₀.
3. The p-value also shows the evidence would be rejected at 2.5% but not at 1%.

#### Common mistakes

- Comparing a two-tailed p-value with half the significance level as well as doubling (doubling twice).

### Check questions

1. H₀: p = 0.2, H₁: p > 0.2, n = 25, 5% level. Find the critical region and the probability of a Type I error. [4 marks]
2. For question 1, find the probability of a Type II error and the power if p = 0.35. [3 marks]
3. A test of H₀: μ = 50 against H₁: μ ≠ 50 uses σ = 2, n = 16 and a 5% level. Find the critical region for x̄ and the power if μ = 51. [6 marks]
4. State the effect on P(Type II) of (a) increasing n, (b) changing from a 5% to a 1% level. [2 marks]
5. A fire-safety test uses H₀: "the material is safe". Describe a Type I and a Type II error in context and say which is more serious. [3 marks]
6. Give one advantage of a p-value over a critical value. [1 mark]
7. A company runs 10 tests at 5% on independent hypotheses that are all true. Find the probability that at least one is significant. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. X ~ B(25, 0.2) (1); P(X ≥ 9) = 0.0468, P(X ≥ 8) = 0.1091 (1); critical region X ≥ 9 (1); P(Type I) = 0.0468 (1).
2. P(X ≤ 8 | p = 0.35) (1) = 0.4668 (1); power = 0.533 (1).
3. SE = 0.5 (1); x̄ < 50 − 1.96 × 0.5 = 49.02 or x̄ > 50.98 (2); if μ = 51, P(49.02 < X̄ < 50.98) (1) = 0.484 (1); power = 0.516 (1).
4. (a) Decreases (1). (b) Increases (1).
5. Type I: concluding the material is unsafe when it is safe (1); Type II: concluding there is no evidence it is unsafe when it is unsafe (1); Type II is more serious because unsafe material would be used (1).
6. It shows the strength of evidence / can be compared with any significance level (1).
7. 1 − 0.95¹⁰ (1) = 0.401 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/statistics/15-tests-for-two-means-and-two-proportions.md -->

## The t-test and tests for two means and two proportions

This file covers the one-sample t-test for a normal mean with unknown variance, the z-test for the difference of two means with known variances, the pooled two-sample t-test when the variances are unknown but equal, the z-test for the difference between two binomial proportions using a pooled estimate, and interpreting all of these in context.

### Overview

The t-test, the two-sample z-test and the pooled t-test are written up in [Further Maths 38](../further-maths/38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md). The test for two proportions is not in A-level Mathematics or Further Mathematics, so it is written in full here. The paired t-test is in [11-non-parametric-and-paired-tests.md](11-non-parametric-and-paired-tests.md). All of these follow the same pattern: estimate − hypothesised value, divided by the standard error.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 9ST0 topic 16 is on Paper 2.
- The booklet gives the test statistics for all four tests, including the pooled variance and the pooled proportion. You do not need to test whether two variances are equal; the question will say they can be assumed equal.
- Use t when n < 30 and σ is unknown.

### 16.1 The t-test for a mean

*Refs: Edexcel 16.1. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
t = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{s / \sqrt{n}} \sim t_{n-1}
```

- Use when σ is unknown and the sample is small (n < 30). Valid only if the population is normal.
- Compare with the critical value tₙ₋₁ from tables, or find the p-value.

#### Explained

The t-distribution is symmetric like Z but with heavier tails, which allows for the uncertainty in using s for σ. The degrees of freedom are n − 1 because s is calculated from deviations about x̄, which sum to zero.

#### Worked example

A machine should fill bottles with a mean of 500 ml. Ten bottles contain 498, 503, 495, 491, 500, 497, 494, 499, 496, 492 ml. Assuming normality, test at 5% whether the mean is less than 500 ml.

1. H₀: μ = 500, H₁: μ < 500.
2. x̄ = 496.5, s = 3.689.

```latex
t = \frac{496.5 - 500}{3.689 / \sqrt{10}} = \frac{-3.5}{1.1667} = -3.00
```

3. ν = 9, critical value −1.833. −3.00 < −1.833 (p-value 0.0075), so reject H₀.
4. There is evidence at 5% that the machine underfills on average.

#### Common mistakes

- Using z with s for a small sample.
- Using ν = n instead of n − 1.

### 16.2 Two means, known variances

*Refs: Edexcel 16.2. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
Z = \frac{(\bar{X} - \bar{Y}) - (\mu_x - \mu_y)}{\sqrt{\dfrac{\sigma_x^2}{n_x} + \dfrac{\sigma_y^2}{n_y}}} \sim N(0, 1)
```

- Two independent random samples from normal populations with known variances (or large samples, using s² for σ² by the central limit theorem).
- Usually H₀: μₓ = μ_y, so μₓ − μ_y = 0.

#### Explained

The variances add because X̄ − Ȳ is a difference of independent variables (see [07-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md](07-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md)).

#### Worked example

Test scores at school A have σ = 8 and at school B σ = 10. A sample of 40 from A has mean 72.4; a sample of 50 from B has mean 68.9. Test at 5% whether the mean scores differ.

1. H₀: μ_A = μ_B, H₁: μ_A ≠ μ_B.

```latex
z = \frac{72.4 - 68.9}{\sqrt{\dfrac{64}{40} + \dfrac{100}{50}}} = \frac{3.5}{\sqrt{3.6}} = 1.845
```

2. Two-tailed critical values ±1.96. 1.845 < 1.96 (p-value 0.065), so do not reject H₀.
3. There is insufficient evidence at 5% that the mean scores at the two schools differ.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding the standard deviations instead of the variances divided by n.

### 16.3 Two means, unknown but equal variances (pooled t-test)

*Refs: Edexcel 16.3. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
s_p^2 = \frac{(n_x - 1)s_x^2 + (n_y - 1)s_y^2}{n_x + n_y - 2}
```

```latex
t = \frac{(\bar{x} - \bar{y}) - (\mu_x - \mu_y)}{\sqrt{s_p^2\left(\dfrac{1}{n_x} + \dfrac{1}{n_y}\right)}} \sim t_{n_x + n_y - 2}
```

- Valid only if both populations are normal and have equal variances.

#### Explained

The pooled variance is a weighted average of the two sample variances, weighted by their degrees of freedom. It combines information from both samples into one estimate of the common σ². If the sample variances are very different (for example one is four times the other), the equal-variance assumption is doubtful and the result should be treated with caution.

#### Worked example

Six plants given feed A grow 23.1, 25.4, 22.8, 26.0, 24.3, 24.9 cm; seven given feed B grow 21.6, 22.9, 23.4, 20.8, 22.1, 23.0, 21.9 cm. Assuming normal distributions with equal variances, test at 5% whether feed A gives greater mean growth.

1. H₀: μ_A = μ_B, H₁: μ_A > μ_B.
2. x̄_A = 24.417, s_A² = 1.6137; x̄_B = 22.243, s_B² = 0.8295.
3. s_p² = (5 × 1.6137 + 6 × 0.8295) ÷ 11 = 1.1860.

```latex
t = \frac{24.417 - 22.243}{\sqrt{1.1860\left(\dfrac{1}{6} + \dfrac{1}{7}\right)}} = \frac{2.174}{0.6058} = 3.59
```

4. ν = 11, critical value 1.796. 3.59 > 1.796, so reject H₀.
5. There is evidence at 5% that feed A gives greater mean growth.

#### Common mistakes

- Using n − 1 for each sample but forgetting to subtract 2 in total for ν.
- Pooling the standard deviations instead of the variances.

### 16.4 Two binomial proportions

*Refs: Edexcel 16.4. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

- H₀: p₁ = p₂. Under H₀ there is one common proportion, estimated by pooling the two samples:

```latex
\hat{p} = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{n_1 + n_2} = \frac{\hat{p}_1 n_1 + \hat{p}_2 n_2}{n_1 + n_2}
```

```latex
z = \frac{\hat{p}_1 - \hat{p}_2}{\sqrt{\hat{p}(1 - \hat{p})\left(\dfrac{1}{n_1} + \dfrac{1}{n_2}\right)}} \sim N(0, 1) \text{ approximately}
```

- Needs two independent random samples, each large enough for the normal approximation.

#### Explained

The pooled proportion is used in the standard error because the test assumes H₀ (one common p) is true. This mirrors the one-sample proportion test, which uses p₀ in the standard error.

#### Worked example

A charity sends two versions of a letter. Version 1 goes to 120 people and 54 donate; version 2 goes to 110 and 38 donate. Test at 5% whether version 1 has a higher donation rate.

1. H₀: p₁ = p₂, H₁: p₁ > p₂.
2. p̂₁ = 54/120 = 0.45, p̂₂ = 38/110 = 0.3455. Pooled p̂ = 92/230 = 0.4.
3. Standard error = √(0.4 × 0.6 × (1/120 + 1/110)) = 0.06467.

```latex
z = \frac{0.45 - 0.3455}{0.06467} = 1.617
```

4. Critical value 1.6449. 1.617 < 1.6449 (p-value 0.053), so do not reject H₀.
5. There is insufficient evidence at 5% that version 1 has a higher donation rate, although the result is close; a larger trial would have more power.

#### Common mistakes

- Using p̂₁ and p̂₂ separately in the standard error for the test.
- Pooling by averaging the two proportions when the sample sizes differ.

### 16.5 Interpreting the tests in context

*Refs: Edexcel 16.5. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

- State the conclusion in context, without certainty, linked to the hypotheses.
- Check and comment on the assumptions: random independent samples, normality (for t-tests), equal variances (for the pooled t-test), large enough samples (for z-tests using the CLT or the normal approximation).
- Consider whether a significant difference is large enough to matter (effect size, see [17-effect-size.md](17-effect-size.md)).

#### Explained

A strong answer has: the decision (reject or not), the evidence (test statistic or p-value against the critical value), the context ("mean growth with feed A"), and a comment on assumptions or practical importance.

#### Worked example

In the feed experiment (16.3), the sample variances were 1.61 and 0.83. Comment on the validity of the test and the conclusion.

1. The ratio of the variances is about 1.9; with samples of 6 and 7 this is not extreme, so equal variances is a reasonable assumption.
2. Normality cannot be checked well with 6 or 7 values; plant growth is often roughly normal.
3. The plants should have been randomly allocated to the feeds, otherwise other factors (light, position) could explain the difference.
4. The mean difference of 2.2 cm is about 2 pooled standard deviations (√1.186 = 1.09), a large effect.

#### Common mistakes

- Ignoring the assumptions the question asks you to state.

### Check questions

1. Eight measurements, assumed normal, are 31.2, 29.8, 32.5, 30.9, 33.1, 31.7, 32.4, 30.6. Test at 5% whether the mean differs from 30. [6 marks]
2. Two populations have σ₁ = 2.5 and σ₂ = 3. Samples of 45 and 60 have means 15.2 and 14.1. Test at 5% whether μ₁ > μ₂. [5 marks]
3. Samples of 10 and 12 have means 58.3 and 54.9 and standard deviations 4.2 and 5.1. Assuming normality and equal variances, test at 5% (two-tailed) whether the means differ. [6 marks]
4. In a survey, 46 of 200 men and 63 of 180 women say they read a daily newspaper. Test at 1% whether the proportions differ. [6 marks]
5. State two assumptions needed for the test in question 3. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. H₀: μ = 30, H₁: μ ≠ 30 (1); x̄ = 31.525, s = 1.1055 (1); t = 1.525 ÷ (1.1055/√8) = 3.90 (1); ν = 7, critical value ±2.365 (1); 3.90 > 2.365, reject H₀ (1); evidence the mean differs from 30 (1).
2. H₀: μ₁ = μ₂, H₁: μ₁ > μ₂ (1); SE = √(6.25/45 + 9/60) = 0.5375 (1); z = 1.1 ÷ 0.5375 = 2.05 (1); > 1.6449 (1); reject H₀: evidence μ₁ > μ₂ (1).
3. H₀: μ₁ = μ₂, H₁: μ₁ ≠ μ₂ (1); s_p² = (9 × 17.64 + 11 × 26.01) ÷ 20 = 22.24 (1); SE = √(22.24 × (1/10 + 1/12)) = 2.019 (1); t = 3.4 ÷ 2.019 = 1.68 (1); ν = 20, critical value ±2.086 (1); do not reject H₀: insufficient evidence the means differ (1).
4. H₀: p₁ = p₂, H₁: p₁ ≠ p₂ (1); p̂₁ = 0.23, p̂₂ = 0.35 (1); pooled p̂ = 109/380 = 0.2868 (1); SE = 0.04647 (1); z = −2.58 (1); |z| > 2.5758, reject H₀ at 1%: evidence that the proportions differ, with women more likely to read a daily paper (1).
5. Both populations normal (1); equal population variances (1) (also accept: independent random samples).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/statistics/16-analysis-of-variance.md -->

## Analysis of variance

This file covers one-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) for a completely randomised design, with its model of additive effects and normal errors; two-way ANOVA without replication for a randomised block design; and the assumptions and interpretation of both.

### Overview

ANOVA is not in A-level Mathematics or Further Mathematics, so this file is new. It tests whether three or more population means are equal, using one F-test instead of many two-sample t-tests (which would inflate the chance of a Type I error, see [14-errors-power-and-p-values.md](14-errors-power-and-p-values.md)). The designs it analyses are in [12-experimental-design.md](12-experimental-design.md). F-tables are given in the exam; the F-test for two variances is in [Further Maths 39](../further-maths/39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md) for background.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 9ST0 topic 20 is on Paper 2. Only one-factor and two-factor ANOVA (without replication) are needed; Latin squares are not.
- The booklet gives the models and the sums of squares formulae (SS_T, SS_B for one-way; SS_T, SS_R, SS_C for two-way), and Table 7 gives percentage points of the F-distribution.

### 20.1 One-way ANOVA

*Refs: Edexcel 20.1. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

- Model for a completely randomised design with k treatments (groups):

```latex
x_{ij} = \mu + \alpha_i + \varepsilon_{ij}, \qquad \varepsilon_{ij} \sim N(0, \sigma^2)
```

where μ is the overall mean, αᵢ is the effect of treatment i (additive), and εᵢⱼ is the experimental error.

- H₀: all treatment means are equal (all αᵢ = 0). H₁: at least one mean differs.
- With T the grand total, Tᵢ the total of group i, nᵢ its size and n the total number of observations:

```latex
SS_T = \sum\sum x_{ij}^2 - \frac{T^2}{n}, \qquad SS_B = \sum_i \frac{T_i^2}{n_i} - \frac{T^2}{n}, \qquad SS_W = SS_T - SS_B
```

- ANOVA table:

| Source | SS | df | MS = SS ÷ df | F |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Between groups | SS_B | k − 1 | MS_B | MS_B ÷ MS_W |
| Within groups (error) | SS_W | n − k | MS_W | |
| Total | SS_T | n − 1 | | |

- Reject H₀ if F > F(k − 1, n − k) at the chosen level.

#### Explained

ANOVA splits the total variation into variation between group means (signal) and variation within groups (noise, the experimental error). If H₀ is true, both mean squares estimate σ², so F should be near 1. If the group means really differ, MS_B is inflated and F is large. The test is always upper-tail.

Software output often labels the rows "Factor" and "Error", and gives a p-value directly.

#### Worked example

Fifteen students are randomly allocated to three revision methods and their test scores recorded:

| Method A | 12 | 15 | 14 | 11 | 13 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Method B | 16 | 18 | 15 | 17 | 19 |
| Method C | 13 | 14 | 16 | 12 | 15 |

Test at 5% whether the mean scores differ.

1. Totals: T_A = 65, T_B = 85, T_C = 70, T = 220, n = 15, Σx² = 3300.

```latex
SS_T = 3300 - \frac{220^2}{15} = 3300 - 3226.67 = 73.33
```

```latex
SS_B = \frac{65^2 + 85^2 + 70^2}{5} - 3226.67 = 3270 - 3226.67 = 43.33
```

2. SS_W = 73.33 − 43.33 = 30.00.

| Source | SS | df | MS | F |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Between | 43.33 | 2 | 21.67 | 8.67 |
| Within | 30.00 | 12 | 2.50 | |
| Total | 73.33 | 14 | | |

3. F(2, 12) at 5% = 3.885. 8.67 > 3.885 (p-value 0.0047), so reject H₀.
4. There is evidence at 5% that mean scores differ between methods. Method B has the highest mean (17 compared with 13 and 14).

#### Common mistakes

- Using n for the total df instead of n − 1.
- Dividing SS by the wrong df; MS_W uses n − k.
- Concluding that every mean differs; ANOVA only shows at least one does.

### 20.2 Two-way ANOVA without replication (randomised block design)

*Refs: Edexcel 20.2. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

- Model, with m rows (often treatments) and n columns (often blocks), one observation per cell:

```latex
x_{ij} = \mu + \alpha_i + \beta_j + \varepsilon_{ij}, \qquad \varepsilon_{ij} \sim N(0, \sigma^2)
```

- With Rᵢ the row totals and Cⱼ the column totals:

```latex
SS_T = \sum\sum x_{ij}^2 - \frac{T^2}{mn}, \quad SS_R = \sum_i \frac{R_i^2}{n} - \frac{T^2}{mn}, \quad SS_C = \sum_j \frac{C_j^2}{m} - \frac{T^2}{mn}
```

```latex
SS_E = SS_T - SS_R - SS_C
```

| Source | SS | df | MS | F |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Rows | SS_R | m − 1 | MS_R | MS_R ÷ MS_E |
| Columns | SS_C | n − 1 | MS_C | MS_C ÷ MS_E |
| Error | SS_E | (m − 1)(n − 1) | MS_E | |
| Total | SS_T | mn − 1 | | |

- Test each factor separately: H₀ for rows (no treatment effect) and H₀ for columns (no block effect).

#### Explained

Blocking takes the variation between blocks out of the error. The error mean square becomes smaller, so treatment differences are easier to detect. Each row total divides by n (the number of values in a row); each column total divides by m.

#### Worked example

Four fertilisers (rows) are tested in three blocks of land (columns), with yields:

| | Block 1 | Block 2 | Block 3 | Row total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A | 42 | 38 | 35 | 115 |
| B | 47 | 44 | 40 | 131 |
| C | 45 | 40 | 36 | 121 |
| D | 50 | 47 | 43 | 140 |
| Column total | 184 | 169 | 154 | 507 |

Σx² = 21657. Test at 5% for differences between fertilisers and between blocks.

1. T²/(mn) = 507² ÷ 12 = 21420.75.
2. SS_T = 21657 − 21420.75 = 236.25.
3. SS_R = (115² + 131² + 121² + 140²) ÷ 3 − 21420.75 = 21542.33 − 21420.75 = 121.58.
4. SS_C = (184² + 169² + 154²) ÷ 4 − 21420.75 = 21533.25 − 21420.75 = 112.50.
5. SS_E = 236.25 − 121.58 − 112.50 = 2.17.

| Source | SS | df | MS | F |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Fertilisers | 121.58 | 3 | 40.53 | 112.2 |
| Blocks | 112.50 | 2 | 56.25 | 155.8 |
| Error | 2.17 | 6 | 0.361 | |
| Total | 236.25 | 11 | | |

6. Fertilisers: F(3, 6) at 5% = 4.757; 112.2 > 4.757, reject H₀. There is evidence the fertilisers give different mean yields (D highest).
7. Blocks: F(2, 6) at 5% = 5.143; 155.8 > 5.143, reject H₀. The blocks differ too, so blocking was worthwhile.
8. Comment: a one-way ANOVA ignoring blocks gives F = 2.83 with F(3, 8) = 4.07, not significant, because the block variation would be left in the error.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing row totals squared by m instead of n.
- Finding SS_E directly instead of by subtraction (it is easy to slip).
- Using the total df in place of the error df for the F critical value.

### 20.3 Assumptions and interpretation

*Refs: Edexcel 20.3. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

- ANOVA assumes: the samples (or cells) come from normal distributions with equal variances; effects are additive; errors are independent. Units should be randomly allocated.
- Interpret in context: which factor is significant, which treatment means are highest or lowest, and whether blocking helped.

#### Explained

Check equal variances by comparing the spread of each group (for example the sample standard deviations or box plots); a ratio of largest to smallest variance well above 4 is a warning sign with small groups. Check normality with a dot plot of each group or of the residuals. If the assumptions fail, the F-test may not be valid.

A significant F tells you there is a difference somewhere, not where. Looking at the group means suggests which treatment stands out; more formal comparisons are beyond the spec.

#### Worked example

For the revision methods in 20.1, the group variances are 2.5, 2.5 and 2.5. Comment on the assumptions and interpret the result for a head of department.

1. The variances are equal, which supports the equal-variance assumption. With 5 values per group, normality is hard to check, but scores are often roughly normal.
2. Students were randomly allocated, so the comparison is fair.
3. Interpretation: the test gives strong evidence that the method affects scores; Method B's mean is about 3 to 4 marks higher than the others, so it is worth adopting, subject to a larger trial.

#### Common mistakes

- Stating "the data are normal" instead of "the populations are normal with equal variances".

### Check questions

1. Three groups of 4 give: P: 8, 10, 9, 11; Q: 12, 13, 11, 14; R: 9, 11, 10, 12. Σx² = 1442. Complete a one-way ANOVA table and test at 5% whether the means differ. F(2, 9) at 5% = 4.256. [8 marks]
2. State the model for one-way ANOVA, explaining each term. [3 marks]
3. Three varieties of wheat (rows) are grown in four blocks (columns), with yields (tonnes per hectare):

| | B1 | B2 | B3 | B4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| X | 6.2 | 5.8 | 7.1 | 6.5 |
| Y | 6.8 | 6.1 | 7.5 | 7.0 |
| Z | 5.9 | 5.5 | 6.6 | 6.2 |

Σx² = 500.5. Carry out a two-way ANOVA at 5%. F(2, 6) = 5.143, F(3, 6) = 4.757. [10 marks]

4. Explain why ANOVA is used instead of three separate two-sample t-tests to compare three means. [2 marks]
5. State two assumptions for the tests in question 3. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Totals 38, 50, 42, T = 130 (1); SS_T = 1442 − 130²/12 = 33.67 (1); SS_B = (38² + 50² + 42²)/4 − 1408.33 = 18.67 (1); SS_W = 15.00 (1); df 2 and 9 (1); MS 9.33 and 1.667 (1); F = 5.60 (1); 5.60 > 4.256, reject H₀: evidence the group means differ (1).
2. xᵢⱼ = μ + αᵢ + εᵢⱼ (1); μ overall mean, αᵢ effect of treatment i (1); εᵢⱼ error ~ N(0, σ²), independent (1).
3. T = 77.2, T²/12 = 496.65 (1); SS_T = 3.847 (1); row totals 25.6, 27.4, 24.2: SS_R = 1.287 (1); column totals 18.9, 17.4, 21.2, 19.7: SS_C = 2.513 (1); SS_E = 0.047 (1); df 2, 3, 6 (1); MS_R = 0.643, MS_C = 0.838, MS_E = 0.00778 (1); F_R = 82.7, F_C = 107.7 (1); varieties: 82.7 > 5.143, evidence varieties differ (Y highest) (1); blocks: 107.7 > 4.757, evidence blocks differ (1).
4. Each test has a 5% chance of a Type I error, so three tests raise the overall chance of a false positive (1); ANOVA uses one test at 5% for all means (and pools the error estimate) (1).
5. Any two: populations normal; equal variances; additive effects (no interaction); independent errors; random allocation within blocks (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/statistics/17-effect-size.md -->

## Effect size and Cohen's d

This file covers effect size as a companion to significance testing, why a p-value depends on both the effect size and the sample size, and calculating and interpreting Cohen's d using the standard guideline boundaries.

### Overview

Effect size is not in A-level Mathematics or Further Mathematics, so this file is new. A significance test answers "is there evidence of a difference?"; an effect size answers "how big is the difference?". Reporting both avoids the misreading of "significant" as "important" discussed in [14-errors-power-and-p-values.md](14-errors-power-and-p-values.md). Cohen's d uses the same pooled standard deviation as the pooled t-test in [15-tests-for-two-means-and-two-proportions.md](15-tests-for-two-means-and-two-proportions.md).

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 9ST0 topic 21 is on Paper 2. The formula for Cohen's d (with the pooled s) is in the formulae booklet; the guideline boundaries must be known.

### 21.1 Effect size alongside significance testing

*Refs: Edexcel 21.1. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

- Effect size quantifies the size of the difference between two means, regardless of whether it is statistically significant.
- A p-value depends on both the effect size and the sample size: a small effect can be highly significant in a very large sample, and a large effect can be non-significant in a small sample.
- How big an effect matters depends on the context.

#### Explained

A test statistic for two means is roughly (effect size) × √(n/2) for two groups of size n. So with the effect fixed, the test statistic grows with √n, and the p-value shrinks. With millions of records, almost every difference is significant; with a handful, almost none is. Effect size does not change with n in this way, so it lets studies of different sizes be compared.

Context matters: a small effect of a cheap, safe change to a whole country's school timetable could matter a great deal; a medium effect of an expensive drug with side effects might not be worth it.

#### Worked example

Two studies compare a new teaching method with the old one, and both find an effect size of d = 0.2 (a small effect). Study 1 has 20 pupils per group; study 2 has 500. Using z ≈ d√(n/2), compare their p-values (two-tailed).

1. Study 1: z ≈ 0.2 × √10 = 0.632; p-value = 0.527. Not significant.
2. Study 2: z ≈ 0.2 × √250 = 3.162; p-value = 0.0016. Highly significant.
3. The effect is the same size; only the sample size differs. Study 2's significance shows the effect is probably real, but it is still small.

#### Common mistakes

- Treating a highly significant result as a large effect.
- Treating a non-significant result from a small study as showing no effect.

### 21.2 Cohen's d

*Refs: Edexcel 21.2. Paper 2.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
d = \frac{\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2}{s}, \qquad s = \sqrt{\frac{(n_1 - 1)s_1^2 + (n_2 - 1)s_2^2}{n_1 + n_2 - 2}}
```

- Guideline boundaries:

| d | Effect size |
| --- | --- |
| 0.2 ≤ d < 0.5 | small |
| 0.5 ≤ d < 0.8 | medium |
| d ≥ 0.8 | large |

- Interpret d as "the means differ by d pooled standard deviations", in context.

#### Explained

Dividing by the pooled standard deviation makes d unit-free, so effects on different scales (marks, seconds, kilograms) can be compared. Use the size of d to judge the effect; its sign only shows which group is higher. Values below 0.2 are usually described as negligible. The boundaries are guidelines, not rules: the meaning depends on the context.

#### Worked example

Class 1 (25 pupils) has mean 64.0 and s = 12.0 in a test; class 2 (30 pupils) has mean 58.5 and s = 10.0. Find Cohen's d and interpret.

1. Pooled s:

```latex
s = \sqrt{\frac{24 \times 144 + 29 \times 100}{53}} = \sqrt{\frac{3456 + 2900}{53}} = \sqrt{119.92} = 10.95
```

2. d = (64.0 − 58.5) ÷ 10.95 = 0.502.
3. 0.5 ≤ 0.502 < 0.8, a medium effect: class 1's mean is about half a standard deviation higher.

For the feeds in [15-tests-for-two-means-and-two-proportions.md](15-tests-for-two-means-and-two-proportions.md), the means differ by 2.174 cm and the pooled variance is 1.186. Find d.

1. s = √1.186 = 1.089; d = 2.174 ÷ 1.089 = 2.00, a large effect, consistent with the very significant t-test.

#### Common mistakes

- Using one group's standard deviation instead of the pooled value.
- Pooling standard deviations directly instead of variances.
- Forgetting the context when describing the effect.

### Check questions

1. Two groups of 40 have means 23.4 and 21.9 and standard deviations 5.0 and 5.4. Find Cohen's d and classify the effect. [4 marks]
2. Two groups of 12 and 15 have means 81 and 74 and standard deviations 6 and 7. Find Cohen's d and classify the effect. [4 marks]
3. A study of 200,000 people finds that a supplement raises mean reaction speed with p < 0.0001 and d = 0.05. Comment for a newspaper headline "Supplement dramatically boosts reactions". [3 marks]
4. Explain why a p-value alone is not a good measure of the size of an effect. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. s = √((39 × 25 + 39 × 29.16) ÷ 78) (1) = 5.204 (1); d = 1.5 ÷ 5.204 = 0.288 (1); small effect (1).
2. s = √((11 × 36 + 14 × 49) ÷ 25) (1) = 6.579 (1); d = 7 ÷ 6.579 = 1.06 (1); large effect (1).
3. The p-value is tiny because the sample is huge (1); d = 0.05 is below the 0.2 boundary, a negligible effect (1); the headline exaggerates: the effect is real but very small (1).
4. The p-value depends on the sample size as well as the size of the difference (1), so a tiny effect can give a small p-value in a large sample, and a large effect a big p-value in a small sample (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/20-data-presentation-and-interpretation.md -->

## Data presentation and interpretation, and the large data set

This file covers diagrams for single-variable data (especially histograms), scatter diagrams, regression lines and correlation, measures of location and spread including standard deviation, outliers, choosing a presentation, cleaning data, and each board's large data set.

### Overview

All AS content. Calculator skills are expected throughout: you should be able to get the mean and standard deviation from raw data or a frequency table using the statistics mode. Correlation coefficients and hypothesis tests on them are in file 23 (A-level only).

#### Board differences

- AQA L1 to L4; Edexcel Statistics 2.1 to 2.4; OCR H240 2.02a to 2.02j; OCR MEI H640 MD1 to MD14.
- **Standard deviation divisor.** OCR H240 uses divisor n. Edexcel expects divisor n but accepts n − 1. OCR MEI defines the sample variance s² and sample standard deviation s with divisor n − 1. AQA's subject content does not fix the divisor (check its formula booklet and mark schemes).
- **Outlier rules.** OCR and MEI expect you to know two rules: more than 1.5 × IQR beyond the nearer quartile, and more than 2 standard deviations from the mean. Edexcel says any rule needed will be given in the question.
- **Edexcel extras.** Coding; linear interpolation for medians and percentiles from grouped data; using logarithms to change variables so that y = axⁿ or y = kbˣ gives a straight line (as in file 11); the terms explanatory (independent) and response (dependent) variable.
- **MEI extras.** Types of data (categorical, discrete, continuous, ranked); cumulative frequency diagrams (MD3); describing distributions, including skew (MD4); and diagrams that mislead (MD5).

### L1 Diagrams for single-variable data

*Refs: AQA L1 · Edexcel Statistics 2.1 · OCR 2.02a, 2.02b · MEI MD1, MD2, MD3, MD4, MD5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Interpret histograms, box plots, cumulative frequency diagrams, frequency polygons, stem-and-leaf diagrams, bar charts and pie charts.
- In a histogram, the area of each bar represents frequency. The height is the frequency density.

```latex
\text{frequency density} = \frac{\text{frequency}}{\text{class width}}
```

- Describe the shape of a distribution: symmetrical, positively skewed (long tail to the right), negatively skewed (long tail to the left).
- Link histograms to probability distributions: the shape of a histogram of a large sample suggests a model, such as the Normal distribution (file 22).

#### Explained

**Histograms.** Used for continuous grouped data with unequal class widths. Frequency = frequency density × class width. If the vertical axis is not labelled, find the scale from one bar whose frequency you know: area (in cm²) is proportional to frequency.

To estimate how many values lie in part of a class, assume the values are spread evenly across the class and take the fraction of the area.

**Box plots** show minimum, lower quartile, median, upper quartile and maximum, with outliers marked separately. They make it easy to compare two data sets.

**Data types.** Categorical (colours, makes of car), discrete numerical (number of goals), continuous (time, mass), ranked (positions in a race). Histograms are for continuous data; bar charts for categorical or discrete.

**Skew.** For positive skew, typically mean > median, and the upper whisker of a box plot is longer. For negative skew, the reverse.

#### Worked example

A histogram has classes 0–10 (frequency 12), 10–15 (18) and 15–30 (24). Find the frequency densities and estimate the number of values between 12 and 20.

1. Frequency densities: 12/10 = 1.2, 18/5 = 3.6, 24/15 = 1.6.
2. From 12 to 15: 3 × 3.6 = 10.8. From 15 to 20: 5 × 1.6 = 8.
3. Estimate: 18.8, about 19.

On a histogram, a bar for the class 20–30 with frequency 15 is 3 cm tall. The bar for 30–40 is 5 cm tall. Find its frequency.

1. The widths are equal, so frequency is proportional to height.
2. 15 × 5/3 = 25.

#### Common mistakes

- Reading the height of a histogram bar as the frequency.
- Drawing gaps between bars of a histogram for continuous data.

### L2 Scatter diagrams, regression lines and correlation

*Refs: AQA L2 · Edexcel Statistics 2.2 · OCR 2.02c, 2.02d, 2.02e · MEI MD6, MD7, MD8. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Interpret scatter diagrams and regression lines for bivariate (two-variable) data. You do not calculate regression lines.
- Recognise when a scatter diagram contains distinct sections of the population.
- Describe correlation informally: positive or negative, strong or weak, or no correlation.
- Correlation does not imply causation.

#### Explained

**Regression line y = a + bx.** b is the change in y for each increase of 1 in x. a is the value of y when x = 0, which may have no sensible meaning in context. Use the line to predict y from x within the range of the data (interpolation). Predictions outside the range (extrapolation) are unreliable because the relationship may change.

The explanatory (independent) variable goes on the x-axis; the response (dependent) variable on the y-axis.

**Distinct sections.** A scatter diagram may contain two groups that each show a pattern, such as heights and masses for adults and children. A single line through both groups can be misleading; analyse each group separately.

**Causation.** Two variables can be correlated because both depend on a third variable (ice cream sales and sunburn both rise in hot weather), or by coincidence.

#### Worked example

For days at a UK weather station, daily mean temperature y (°C) and daily hours of sunshine x satisfy the regression line y = 11.2 + 0.6x, for data with 0 ≤ x ≤ 14. Interpret 0.6, estimate y when x = 8, and comment on using the line when x = 18.

1. Each extra hour of sunshine is linked to a mean temperature about 0.6 °C higher.
2. y = 11.2 + 4.8 = 16.0 °C. This is interpolation, so it is reasonably reliable.
3. x = 18 is outside the data (extrapolation), so the prediction is unreliable; days with that much sunshine may not exist at that station.

A scatter diagram of car engine size against fuel economy shows two clusters: one for petrol cars and one for diesel cars. Why might one line for all cars be misleading?

1. The two fuel types follow different relationships. A single line mixes them and could suggest a pattern that neither group follows. Separate lines for each group are better.

#### Common mistakes

- Claiming one variable causes the other.
- Interpreting the intercept when x = 0 is far outside the data.

### L3 Measures of location and spread

*Refs: AQA L3 · Edexcel Statistics 2.3 · OCR 2.02f, 2.02g · MEI MD10, MD11, MD12. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Measures of location: mean, median, mode. Measures of spread: range, interquartile range, interpercentile ranges, variance, standard deviation.
- Calculate the standard deviation from raw data, from a frequency table and from summary statistics.

```latex
\bar{x} = \frac{\sum x}{n} \qquad S_{xx} = \sum (x - \bar{x})^2 = \sum x^2 - \frac{\left(\sum x\right)^2}{n}
```

```latex
\sigma = \sqrt{\frac{S_{xx}}{n}} = \sqrt{\frac{\sum x^2}{n} - \bar{x}^2} \qquad s = \sqrt{\frac{S_{xx}}{n-1}}
```

For a frequency table, replace Σx by Σfx, Σx² by Σfx², and n by Σf. For grouped data, use class midpoints (the answers are estimates).

- (Edexcel) Coding: if y = (x − a)/b, then ȳ = (x̄ − a)/b and the standard deviation of y is (sd of x)/b.
- (Edexcel) Find the median and percentiles of grouped data by linear interpolation.

#### Explained

Use the median and IQR when the data are skewed or have outliers, because they are not affected by extreme values. Use the mean and standard deviation for roughly symmetrical data; they use every value.

**Interpolation for the median.** Find the position n/2. Find the class containing it. Median = lower class boundary + [(n/2 − cumulative frequency before the class)/(class frequency)] × class width.

**Coding.** Adding or subtracting a constant shifts the mean but does not change the spread. Multiplying or dividing scales both.

#### Worked example

n = 20, Σx = 340, Σx² = 6100. Find the mean and standard deviation.

1. x̄ = 340/20 = 17.
2. Variance (divisor n) = 6100/20 − 17² = 305 − 289 = 16, so σ = 4.
3. With divisor n − 1: Sₓₓ = 6100 − 340²/20 = 320, s² = 320/19 = 16.84, s = 4.10.

Grouped data: 0–10 (f = 12), 10–15 (f = 18), 15–30 (f = 24). Estimate the mean, the standard deviation (divisor n) and the median.

1. Midpoints 5, 12.5, 22.5. Σf = 54. Σfx = 60 + 225 + 540 = 825. x̄ = 15.28.
2. Σfx² = 300 + 2812.5 + 12 150 = 15 262.5. Variance = 15 262.5/54 − 15.278² = 49.23. SD = 7.02.
3. Median: position 27. Cumulative frequencies 12, 30, 54, so it is in 10–15. Median ≈ 10 + (27 − 12)/18 × 5 = 14.17.

(Edexcel) Data are coded with y = (x − 100)/5. The coded mean is 2.4 and the coded standard deviation is 1.2. Find the mean and standard deviation of x.

1. x = 5y + 100. Mean of x = 5(2.4) + 100 = 112.
2. SD of x = 5 × 1.2 = 6.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to subtract x̄² (writing Σx²/n as the variance).
- Applying the added constant to the standard deviation when decoding.

### L4 Outliers, choosing presentations and cleaning data

*Refs: AQA L4 · Edexcel Statistics 2.4 · OCR 2.02h, 2.02i, 2.02j · MEI MD9, MD13, MD14. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Recognise and interpret possible outliers in data sets and diagrams.
- Select or criticise ways of presenting data for a given problem.
- Clean data: deal with missing data, errors and outliers.

#### Explained

**Outlier rules.**

```latex
x < Q_1 - 1.5 \times \text{IQR} \quad \text{or} \quad x > Q_3 + 1.5 \times \text{IQR}
```

```latex
x < \bar{x} - 2\sigma \quad \text{or} \quad x > \bar{x} + 2\sigma
```

An outlier is not automatically wrong. Decide whether it is an error (remove or correct it) or a genuine extreme value (keep it, and perhaps comment on it). Removing outliers changes the mean and standard deviation more than the median and IQR.

**Cleaning data.** Check for missing values (blank, "n/a"), impossible values (a negative rainfall, a 300 kg car), inconsistent units, and codes such as "tr" for a trace amount. Decide how each will be treated and say so.

**Choosing a diagram.** Box plots compare distributions; histograms show the shape of continuous data; scatter diagrams show relationships; pie charts show proportions of a whole. A diagram can mislead through a truncated axis, unequal class widths drawn with heights as frequencies, or 3D effects.

#### Worked example

For a data set, Q₁ = 20, Q₃ = 32. Is 55 an outlier using the 1.5 × IQR rule?

1. IQR = 12. Upper fence = 32 + 18 = 50.
2. 55 > 50, so 55 is an outlier.

The mean of a data set is 26 and the standard deviation is 6. Which values are outliers using the 2σ rule?

1. 26 ± 12: values below 14 or above 38.

A student cleaning weather data finds a daily rainfall of "tr", another of "n/a", and one of −2 mm. How should each be treated?

1. "tr" means a trace (less than the smallest measurable amount); it can be treated as 0 for calculations, and this should be stated.
2. "n/a" means no reading: leave that day out.
3. −2 mm is impossible, so it is an error: remove it (or correct it if the true value can be found).

#### Common mistakes

- Removing an outlier without saying why it is an error.
- Using the 2σ rule when the question asks for the IQR rule, or the reverse.

### The large data set

*Refs: all boards' requirement to use a large data set (DfE "use of data in statistics"); AQA 3.21. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Each board publishes a large data set (LDS) that you must explore before the exam, using a spreadsheet or statistical software.
- Questions may assume you know the context, the variables, their units and typical values, and the main features of the data, without explaining them.
- You may be asked to criticise a sample drawn from the LDS, clean part of it, or interpret summary statistics or diagrams based on it.

#### Explained

The data set is different for each board:

| Board | Data set | What to know |
| --- | --- | --- |
| AQA 7357 | Published on the AQA website only. For exams from 2020 it is a set of car data: details of individual vehicles (make, propulsion type, engine size, mass, CO2 and other emissions, region, and registration year, with two years to compare). | What each variable means and its units; typical values; how the two years and the propulsion types differ; missing and impossible values |
| Edexcel 9MA0 | Weather data from Met Office stations in the UK and overseas, daily readings for May to October of 1987 and of 2015 (temperature, rainfall, sunshine, wind speed and direction, cloud cover, humidity, visibility, pressure). | Where each station is (coastal or inland, north or south); units (knots, oktas, mm); codes such as "tr" for trace and "n/a"; how 1987 compares with 2015 |
| OCR H240 | OCR's pre-released set, drawn from census data on local authorities (for example, age structure and method of travel to work). | What each column counts; working with proportions rather than raw counts; regional differences |
| OCR MEI H640 | MEI's data sets. Until 2026 the exam year rotated through several sets; from September 2026 all two-year students use a single set published by OCR/MEI. | Whichever set your exam year uses: its context, variables, units and main features |

The AQA, Edexcel and OCR descriptions in this table are not stated in the specification PDFs and should be checked against the board's current data set before teaching.

Good habits with the LDS:

1. Read the metadata: what each variable means, units, how it was measured.
2. Look at typical values and the spread of each variable. Know roughly what a normal value looks like, so you can spot errors.
3. Draw a few diagrams with software and note anything surprising.
4. Practise taking random, systematic and stratified samples from it.
5. Practise cleaning it: missing values, codes, outliers.

#### Worked example

(Edexcel weather data) A student takes the first 10 days of daily mean wind speed at one station in 2015 to compare with 1987. Give two criticisms.

1. The first 10 days of May are not typical of the whole May to October period, so the sample is unlikely to be representative.
2. Ten days is a small sample, so the comparison may be dominated by chance; a random sample of, say, 30 days from each year would be better.

(AQA car data) A summary shows a car with a mass of 15 kg. What should be done?

1. 15 kg is impossible for a car, so it is an error, probably a data entry slip. Remove it from the analysis (or correct it if the true value is known), and say so.

#### Common mistakes

- Answering an LDS question without using its context, for example not knowing what units a variable uses.

### Check questions

1. On a histogram, the class 20–30 has frequency 15 and its bar is 3 cm tall. The bar for 30–40 is 5 cm tall and the bar for 40–60 is 2 cm tall. Find the frequencies of these two classes. [3 marks]
2. n = 12, Σx = 96, Σx² = 840. Find the mean, and the standard deviation using divisor n. Then find s using divisor n − 1. [4 marks]
3. Times (minutes): 0–5 (8), 5–10 (14), 10–20 (18), 20–40 (10). (a) Estimate the mean. (b) Estimate the median using interpolation. (c) Comment on the skew. [6 marks]
4. (Edexcel) Data are coded with y = (x − 50)/2. For 12 values, Σy = 36 and Σy² = 300. Find the mean and standard deviation of x. [4 marks]
5. For a data set, Q₁ = 14 and Q₃ = 22. Show that 37 is an outlier. [2 marks]
6. A regression line for daily mean temperature y (°C) on hours of sunshine x is y = 11.2 + 0.6x, for 0 ≤ x ≤ 14. (a) Interpret 0.6. (b) Estimate y when x = 8. (c) Explain why it would be unwise to use the line when x = 18. [4 marks]
7. In a town, the number of cafés and the number of road accidents per year both increased from 2010 to 2020, with strong positive correlation. Explain why this does not show that cafés cause accidents. [2 marks]
8. In your large data set, give one example of a value you would remove when cleaning the data, and one example of a value you would keep even though it is extreme. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Equal widths, so frequency ∝ height for 30–40: 15 × 5/3 = 25 (1); frequency density for 20–30 is 1.5 per 3 cm, so 2 cm ↔ 1.0 (1); 40–60: 1.0 × 20 = 20 (1).
2. Mean 8 (1); variance 840/12 − 64 = 6 (1); σ = 2.45 (1); Sₓₓ = 72, s = √(72/11) = 2.56 (1).
3. (a) Midpoints 2.5, 7.5, 15, 30 (1); Σfx = 695, mean 13.9 (1). (b) 25th value in 10–20 (1); 10 + (25 − 22)/18 × 10 = 11.7 (1). (c) Mean > median (1); positive skew (1).
4. ȳ = 3 (1); variance of y = 300/12 − 9 = 16, sd 4 (1); x̄ = 2(3) + 50 = 56 (1); sd of x = 8 (1).
5. IQR = 8, upper fence 22 + 12 = 34 (1); 37 > 34 (1).
6. (a) Each extra hour of sunshine is linked to about 0.6 °C higher mean temperature (1). (b) 16.0 °C (1). (c) 18 is outside the range of the data (1); extrapolation, so the relationship may not hold (1).
7. Correlation does not imply causation (1); both may be linked to a third factor, such as population growth (1).
8. An impossible value, e.g. a negative measurement or an impossible mass (1); a genuine extreme value, e.g. a very wet day or a very heavy vehicle that is real (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/statistics/05-tables-and-pictorial-representations.md -->

## Tables and pictorial representations

This file covers tables (including two-way tables), tally charts, pictograms, pie charts, stem and leaf diagrams, Venn diagrams, population pyramids, choropleth maps, comparative pie charts and other comparative 2D and 3D pictures, plus choosing, justifying and criticising representations and reading data in different formats. Graphs (bar charts, histograms, cumulative frequency, box plots) are in file 06.

### Overview

All on both tiers except: (HT only) comparative pie charts and comparative 2D and 3D representations.

#### Board differences

- AQA 8382: 3.3.1 (C1a), 3.3.2 (C1b), 3.3.4 (C3a), 3.3.5 (C3b), 3.3.6 (C4a), 3.3.7 (C4b), and 3.5.10 (E3d) on interpreting tables including published secondary data. AQA says you will not be asked to draw 3D representations.
- Edexcel 1ST0: 2a.01, 2a.02, 2a.05 to 2a.08. Edexcel's Higher guidance expects you to calculate radii for comparative pie charts. Edexcel stresses using spreadsheets and statistical software, and choosing a visualisation to suit the audience.
- The frequency density part of misrepresentation (AQA 3.3.5, Edexcel 2a.06) is in file 06.

### 3.3.1 / 2a.01 Tables, pictograms, pie charts, stem and leaf, Venn diagrams

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1 (C1a) · Edexcel 2a.01. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Draw and interpret: tables (frequency tables, two-way tables), tally charts, pictograms, pie charts, stem and leaf diagrams (including back-to-back), Venn diagrams.
- Calculate the key values needed (angles, frequencies).
- Diagrams need a key where appropriate.
- Stem and leaf diagrams must be ordered so you can read off the median and quartiles.

#### Explained

Two-way tables show two categorical variables at once. Rows and columns both have totals, and the grand total appears in the corner.

Pictograms use a symbol for a fixed number of items, shown in the key. Part symbols show fractions of that number.

Pie charts show proportions of a whole:

```latex
\text{angle} = \frac{\text{frequency}}{\text{total}} \times 360^\circ
```

Stem and leaf diagrams keep the raw data while showing the shape. The stem is the leading digit(s); each leaf is one digit. A key such as "3 | 7 means 37" is essential. A back-to-back diagram compares two sets: leaves for one set go to the left, read from the stem outwards.

Venn diagrams show which items belong to which groups; overlaps show items in more than one group; anything outside the circles is in none. A frequency Venn diagram has numbers in each region.

#### Worked example

Pie chart: 120 students chose their favourite subject: Maths 34, English 26, Science 40, Other 20. Find the angles.

1. 360 ÷ 120 = 3° per student.
2. Maths 102°, English 78°, Science 120°, Other 60°. Check: total 360°.

Stem and leaf: draw a stem and leaf diagram for 41, 36, 52, 47, 38, 44, 59, 36, 50, 43, 48, and find the median.

```
3 | 6 6 8
4 | 1 3 4 7 8
5 | 0 2 9
Key: 3 | 6 means 36
```

1. n = 11, so the median is the 6th value: 36, 36, 38, 41, 43, **44**. Median = 44.

Venn diagram: of 50 people, 28 own a dog, 19 own a cat and 9 own both. How many own neither?

1. Dog only: 28 − 9 = 19. Cat only: 19 − 9 = 10. Both: 9.
2. Neither: 50 − (19 + 10 + 9) = 12.

#### Common mistakes

- Unordered leaves, or no key.
- In a Venn diagram, putting 28 in the "dog only" region when 28 is the total for the whole dog circle.
- Rounding pie chart angles so they no longer total 360°.

### 3.3.2 / 2a.02 Population pyramids, choropleth maps and comparative diagrams

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2 (C1b) · Edexcel 2a.02. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Interpret and compare population pyramids and choropleth maps.
- (HT only) Comparative pie charts: the areas are in proportion to the totals, so the radii are in proportion to the square roots of the totals.
- (HT only) Interpret comparative 2D and 3D representations (you will not draw 3D ones).

#### Explained

A population pyramid is two horizontal bar charts back to back: males on one side, females on the other, age groups going up. A wide base means many young people (high birth rate); a wide top means many older people.

A choropleth map shades areas by the value of a variable, darker for higher values, with a key. It shows geographical patterns but hides variation inside each area.

(HT only) Comparative pie charts. If pie A represents 400 people and pie B represents 900, B's area should be 900/400 = 2.25 times A's. Since area = πr², the ratio of radii is the square root:

```latex
\frac{r_B}{r_A} = \sqrt{\frac{n_B}{n_A}}
```

(HT only) Comparative 2D and 3D pictures: if a picture is doubled in height and width, its area is four times as big, so it looks four times bigger. A 3D picture doubled in every direction looks eight times bigger. Judge by the scale, not by eye.

#### Worked example

(HT only) Pie chart A shows the spending of a family with total spending £2500, drawn with radius 4 cm. Pie chart B shows a family with total spending £3600. Find the radius of B.

1. Ratio of areas: 3600 ÷ 2500 = 1.44.
2. Ratio of radii: √1.44 = 1.2.
3. Radius of B: 4 × 1.2 = 4.8 cm.

#### Common mistakes

- (HT only) Making the radius proportional to the total instead of its square root.
- Reading a choropleth map as if the whole area has the same value everywhere.

### 3.3.4 / 2a.05 Choosing a format and accurate visualisation

*Refs: AQA 3.3.4 (C3a) · Edexcel 2a.05. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Justify why a format is suitable, and draw it accurately (labelled axes, title, key, sensible scales).
- Know what spreadsheets and statistical software can do, and avoid using them inappropriately (for example, a 3D chart because it looks good).
- Match the visualisation to the audience: simple for the public, more technical for specialists.

#### Explained

A good diagram is honest and clear. Technology makes drawing easy but also makes misleading choices easy: default 3D effects, odd colours, automatic axes that do not start at zero.

#### Worked example

A charity wants to show the public how each £100 donated is spent. Suggest and justify a diagram.

1. A pie chart, because it shows how a whole (£100) is split into parts, and is easy for the public to understand.

#### Common mistakes

- Justifying a choice with "it is clear" without linking it to the data or audience.

### 3.3.5 / 2a.06 Misleading diagrams

*Refs: AQA 3.3.5 (C3b) · Edexcel 2a.06. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Spot construction errors that mislead: incorrect or uneven scales, truncated axes (not starting at 0), distorted sizing (pictures scaled in 2D or 3D), 3D effects, missing labels.
- Use class boundaries correctly.
- Criticise diagrams from secondary sources (newspapers, adverts).
- (HT only) Misusing the frequency density formula in histograms: see file 06.

#### Explained

| Fault | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| Vertical axis starts at 80 instead of 0 | Small differences look huge |
| Uneven scale (0, 10, 50, 100 equally spaced) | Changes look different sizes from what they are |
| Picture doubled in height and width | Looks 4 times bigger instead of twice |
| 3D pie chart | Front slices look bigger than back slices |
| No labels or units | Cannot tell what is shown |
| Unequal class widths drawn as if equal | Wide classes look more important |

#### Worked example

A bar chart shows sales of 92 and 98 thousand, with the vertical axis from 90 to 100. Explain why it is misleading.

1. The axis does not start at 0. The second bar looks four times as tall (8 against 2 units above 90), but sales only rose by about 6.5%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "the graph is wrong" without explaining the effect on what readers believe.

### 3.3.6 / 2a.07 Comparing data shown in different formats

*Refs: AQA 3.3.6 (C4a) · Edexcel 2a.07. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Extract or calculate matching values (medians, IQRs, means, standard deviations) from data given in different forms, such as a list for one set and a box plot or a software printout for the other.
- Present the same information in more than one format.

#### Worked example

Group A's reaction times are shown on a box plot: median 0.32 s, IQR 0.10 s. Group B's are listed: 0.25, 0.27, 0.28, 0.30, 0.31, 0.33, 0.36. Compare.

1. Group B: n = 7, median = 4th value = 0.30 s. LQ = 2nd value = 0.27, UQ = 6th value = 0.33, IQR = 0.06 s.
2. Group B has a lower median, so typically quicker reactions, and a smaller IQR, so more consistent times.

#### Common mistakes

- Comparing a median from one set with a mean from the other.

### 3.3.7 / 2a.08 Selecting a representation

*Refs: AQA 3.3.7 (C4b) · Edexcel 2a.08. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Choose and justify a representation that suits the type of data.

#### Explained

| Data | Suitable diagrams |
| --- | --- |
| Categorical | Bar chart, pie chart, pictogram |
| Discrete ungrouped | Vertical line (bar line) chart |
| Continuous grouped | Histogram, frequency polygon, cumulative frequency |
| Comparing distributions | Back-to-back stem and leaf, box plots, frequency polygons |
| Bivariate | Scatter diagram |
| Data over time | Time series line graph |
| Geographical | Choropleth map |
| Age structure | Population pyramid |

#### Worked example

Which diagram would you use to show whether taller students have longer stride lengths? Why?

1. A scatter diagram, because the data is bivariate (two measurements per student) and it shows whether there is correlation.

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing a pie chart for data that is not parts of a whole.

### 3.5.10 Interpreting tables

*Refs: AQA 3.5.10 (E3d) (also used throughout Edexcel 2a.01, 2a.07). Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Read and interpret data in many kinds of tables, including published secondary data with several rows, columns and footnotes.

#### Explained

Read the title, headings, units ("thousands", "%") and footnotes first. Check whether figures are counts or percentages, and whether rows or columns add to 100%.

#### Worked example

A published table shows, in thousands, adults by type of travel to work: Car 1840, Bus 410, Train 290, Walk/cycle 360, Total 2900. What percentage travel by public transport?

1. Public transport = bus + train = 410 + 290 = 700 thousand.
2. 700 ÷ 2900 × 100 = 24.1%.

#### Common mistakes

- Ignoring "in thousands" and giving the answer as 700 people.

### Check questions

1. A pie chart shows how 90 people travel to work. The sector for "bus" has an angle of 56°. How many people travel by bus? [2 marks]
2. Draw an ordered stem and leaf diagram for: 23, 35, 18, 29, 31, 24, 17, 38, 26, 22. Give a key. [3 marks]
3. Using your diagram, find the median and the range. [2 marks]
4. In a group of 40 students, 22 study French, 15 study Spanish and 6 study both. Draw a Venn diagram and find how many study neither. [3 marks]
5. Describe what a population pyramid with a narrow base and a wide top tells you about a country. [2 marks]
6. (HT only) A comparative pie chart for a town of 16 000 people has radius 5 cm. Find the radius for a town of 25 000 people. [3 marks]
7. A newspaper shows a picture of a coin twice as tall and twice as wide as another to show that savings have doubled. Explain why this is misleading. [2 marks]
8. State a suitable diagram to compare the masses of apples from two orchards, and give a reason. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 56/360 × 90 (1) = 14 (1).
2. Stems 1, 2, 3 (1); leaves 1 | 7 8; 2 | 2 3 4 6 9; 3 | 1 5 8 ordered (1); key e.g. 1 | 7 means 17 (1).
3. Median: n = 10, mean of 5th and 6th values (24 and 26) = 25 (1). Range 38 − 17 = 21 (1).
4. French only 16, Spanish only 9, both 6 (1 for correct diagram, 1 for two correct regions); neither 40 − 31 = 9 (1).
5. Few young people / low birth rate (1); many older people / long life expectancy (1).
6. 25 000 ÷ 16 000 = 1.5625 (1); √1.5625 = 1.25 (1); 5 × 1.25 = 6.25 cm (1).
7. The area is four times as large (1), so it looks as if savings have quadrupled rather than doubled (1).
8. Back-to-back stem and leaf / two box plots / two frequency polygons on the same axes (1); allows the averages and spreads to be compared directly (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/statistics/06-graphs-histograms-and-cumulative-frequency.md -->

## Graphs, histograms and cumulative frequency

This file covers bar charts (dual, multiple, composite and percentage composite), line graphs, time series graphs, scatter diagrams, bar line (vertical line) charts, frequency polygons, cumulative frequency graphs (including step polygons), box plots and histograms with equal and (Higher) unequal class widths.

### Overview

All on both tiers except: (HT only) frequency density and histograms with unequal class widths, and the misuse of the frequency density formula. Time series analysis is in file 11, scatter diagrams and correlation in file 10, and quartiles and IQR in file 08.

#### Board differences

- AQA 8382: everything is in 3.3.3 (C2), with the frequency density part of misrepresentation in 3.3.5 (C3b). AQA's foundation content includes cumulative frequency step polygons for discrete data.
- Edexcel 1ST0: 2a.03 (both tiers), 2a.04 (Higher only: frequency density and unequal widths), 2a.06 (misrepresentation). Edexcel allows the y-axis of an equal-width histogram to be labelled "frequency". Cumulative frequency polygons and curves are treated the same; frequency polygons may be open or closed.

### 3.3.3 / 2a.03 Bar charts, line graphs and bar line charts

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3 (C2) · Edexcel 2a.03. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Bar charts for categorical or discrete data: bars of equal width with gaps.
- Dual (comparative) bar charts: bars for two or more groups side by side.
- Composite (stacked) bar charts: each bar split into parts. Percentage composite bar charts have every bar the same height (100%).
- Bar line (vertical line) charts for ungrouped discrete data.
- Line graphs and time series graphs: points joined with straight lines (see file 11).
- Scatter diagrams for bivariate data (see file 10).

#### Explained

A percentage composite bar chart compares proportions, not totals: two schools of very different sizes can be compared fairly.

#### Worked example

Two clubs report how members travel. Club X (200 members): car 90, bus 60, walk 50. Club Y (80 members): car 28, bus 20, walk 32. Work out the percentages for a percentage composite bar chart and compare.

1. Club X: car 45%, bus 30%, walk 25%.
2. Club Y: car 35%, bus 25%, walk 40%.
3. A larger proportion of Club Y's members walk (40% against 25%), even though Club X has more walkers in total (50 against 32).

#### Common mistakes

- Comparing totals when the groups are different sizes; use percentages.
- Gaps between bars for continuous data (only histograms have no gaps).

### 3.3.3 / 2a.03 Frequency polygons

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3 (C2) · Edexcel 2a.03. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Plot each frequency against the midpoint of its class, and join the points with straight lines.
- Can be left open or closed (joined down to the axis half a class beyond each end).
- Good for comparing two distributions on the same axes.

#### Worked example

Plot points for: 0 ≤ t < 10 (4), 10 ≤ t < 20 (9), 20 ≤ t < 30 (12), 30 ≤ t < 40 (5).

1. Midpoints: 5, 15, 25, 35.
2. Points: (5, 4), (15, 9), (25, 12), (35, 5). Join with straight lines.

#### Common mistakes

- Plotting at the class boundaries instead of midpoints.
- Joining the first and last points to each other.

### 3.3.3 / 2a.03 Cumulative frequency

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3 (C2) · Edexcel 2a.03. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Cumulative frequency is the running total of frequencies.
- Grouped continuous data: plot each running total against the upper class boundary, starting at (lowest boundary, 0); join with a smooth curve or straight lines.
- Discrete data: a step polygon (cumulative frequency jumps at each value and stays flat in between).
- Use the graph to estimate the median, quartiles, percentiles, and how many are above or below a value.

#### Explained

For n values on a cumulative frequency graph, read across at:

- median: n/2
- lower quartile: n/4
- upper quartile: 3n/4
- kth percentile: k% of n

For "how many more than x", read up from x and subtract from n.

#### Worked example

The times taken by 80 people to finish a puzzle:

| Time t (min) | Frequency | Cumulative frequency |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0 < t ≤ 5 | 6 | 6 |
| 5 < t ≤ 10 | 18 | 24 |
| 10 < t ≤ 15 | 30 | 54 |
| 15 < t ≤ 20 | 18 | 72 |
| 20 < t ≤ 25 | 8 | 80 |

1. Plot (0, 0), (5, 6), (10, 24), (15, 54), (20, 72), (25, 80).
2. Median: read across at 40. Between (10, 24) and (15, 54), 40 is 16/30 of the way up, so t ≈ 10 + 16/30 × 5 ≈ 12.7 min.
3. LQ at 20: between (5, 6) and (10, 24), 14/18 of the way: 5 + 14/18 × 5 ≈ 8.9 min. UQ at 60: between (15, 54) and (20, 72): 15 + 6/18 × 5 ≈ 16.7 min. IQR ≈ 7.8 min.
4. Number taking more than 18 min: CF at 18 ≈ 54 + 3/5 × 18 = 64.8, so about 80 − 65 = 15 people.

(These readings assume straight lines between points; a smooth curve gives values close to these.)

#### Common mistakes

- Plotting at midpoints instead of upper class boundaries.
- Not starting at zero frequency at the bottom of the first class.
- Reading the median at the median of the x-values rather than at n/2 on the vertical axis.

### 3.3.3 / 2a.03 Box plots

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3 (C2) · Edexcel 2a.03. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- A box plot shows the minimum, lower quartile, median, upper quartile and maximum on a scale.
- Outliers may be shown as separate crosses, with whiskers going to the most extreme values that are not outliers (see file 08).
- Use box plots to compare distributions: medians for average, IQRs and ranges for spread, and the box shape for skewness.

#### Worked example

Draw a box plot for: min 12, LQ 18, median 23, UQ 31, max 40.

1. Draw a scale from 10 to 40.
2. Draw a box from 18 to 31 with a line at 23.
3. Draw whiskers from 12 to 18 and from 31 to 40.
4. Comment: the median is nearer the lower quartile (23 − 18 = 5 < 31 − 23 = 8), so the data has positive skew.

#### Common mistakes

- No scale, or the scale does not cover the whole data.
- Comparing box plots using only the maximum values.

### 3.3.3 / 2a.03, 2a.04 Histograms

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3 (C2) · Edexcel 2a.03 (equal width), 2a.04 (unequal width). Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Histograms show grouped continuous data: bars touch, and the horizontal axis is a continuous scale.
- Equal class widths: bar heights can show frequency (Edexcel allows the axis label "frequency" here).
- (HT only) Unequal class widths: the area of each bar represents frequency, so the height is frequency density:

```latex
\text{frequency density} = \frac{\text{frequency}}{\text{class width}}
```

- (HT only) Find frequencies from a histogram: frequency = frequency density × class width.
- (HT only) Label the axis "frequency density" (or use a key showing what an area means).

#### Explained

(HT only) If a class is twice as wide, it would collect about twice as many values even if the data were spread evenly. Dividing by the width corrects for this, so the heights show how crowded each part of the scale is.

(HT only) To estimate how many lie in part of a class, take that fraction of the bar's area, assuming values are spread evenly across the class.

#### Worked example

(HT only) Masses of 100 parcels:

| Mass m (kg) | Frequency | Class width | Frequency density |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 < m ≤ 2 | 16 | 2 | 8 |
| 2 < m ≤ 3 | 22 | 1 | 22 |
| 3 < m ≤ 5 | 30 | 2 | 15 |
| 5 < m ≤ 10 | 25 | 5 | 5 |
| 10 < m ≤ 20 | 7 | 10 | 0.7 |

1. Each frequency density = frequency ÷ width (last column).
2. Draw bars from 0 to 2 at height 8, 2 to 3 at height 22, and so on, with no gaps.
3. Estimate the number of parcels between 4 kg and 7 kg:
   - 4 to 5: half of the 3–5 class: 15 × 1 = 15.
   - 5 to 7: 2 kg of the 5–10 class: 5 × 2 = 10.
   - Total about 25.

(HT only) Reading back: a bar from 20 to 35 has frequency density 1.6. Frequency = 1.6 × 15 = 24.

#### Common mistakes

- (HT only) Plotting frequency instead of frequency density for unequal widths.
- (HT only) Using the wrong class width, especially when data was rounded (class 10–14 recorded to the nearest whole number has width 5).
- Leaving gaps between bars.

### 3.3.5 / 2a.06 Misuse of frequency density

*Refs: AQA 3.3.5 (C3b) · Edexcel 2a.06. Tier: Higher only (this part).*

#### What you need to know

- (HT only) Spot histograms that are misleading because frequency was plotted instead of frequency density, or because the class widths or boundaries were wrong.

#### Explained

(HT only) If a histogram with unequal widths uses frequency as the height, a wide class looks far bigger than it should, because its area is multiplied by its width.

#### Worked example

(HT only) A histogram for classes 0–10 (frequency 20) and 10–40 (frequency 30) uses heights 20 and 30. Explain the problem.

1. The second bar is three times as wide, so its area is 30 × 30 = 900 against 20 × 10 = 200: it looks 4.5 times as big, though its frequency is only 1.5 times as big.
2. Correct heights: 20 ÷ 10 = 2 and 30 ÷ 30 = 1.

#### Common mistakes

- (HT only) Spotting that it is wrong but not saying what it makes readers think.

### Check questions

1. Explain one advantage of a percentage composite bar chart over a composite bar chart. [1 mark]
2. Write down the coordinates you would plot for a frequency polygon for: 10 ≤ h < 20 (frequency 7), 20 ≤ h < 30 (frequency 13), 30 ≤ h < 40 (frequency 4). [2 marks]
3. Complete a cumulative frequency table for 60 test marks: 0–10 (3), 11–20 (9), 21–30 (21), 31–40 (19), 41–50 (8), and state the coordinates you would plot. [3 marks]
4. A cumulative frequency graph for 120 people has the value 30 at 14 minutes, 60 at 19 minutes and 90 at 26 minutes. Write down the median and IQR. [2 marks]
5. Draw a box plot for: smallest 3, LQ 7, median 9, UQ 15, largest 22. Describe the skewness. [3 marks]
6. (HT only) A class 15 < x ≤ 25 has frequency 34. Find its frequency density. [1 mark]
7. (HT only) In a histogram, the bar for 60 < t ≤ 90 has frequency density 2.4. Find the frequency. Estimate how many values lie between 60 and 70. [3 marks]
8. (HT only) Explain why using frequency as the height of bars in a histogram with unequal class widths is misleading. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. Groups of different sizes can be compared by proportion (1).
2. (15, 7), (25, 13), (35, 4) (1 for midpoints, 1 for all correct).
3. CF 3, 12, 33, 52, 60 (1). Points at upper boundaries 10.5, 20.5, 30.5, 40.5, 50.5 (1): (10.5, 3), (20.5, 12), (30.5, 33), (40.5, 52), (50.5, 60), starting at (−0.5, 0) or (0, 0) (1). (Accept boundaries 10, 20, 30, 40, 50 if the marks are treated as whole numbers.)
4. Median 19 min (1); IQR 26 − 14 = 12 min (1).
5. Correct box from 7 to 15 with line at 9 (1); whiskers to 3 and 22 (1); median closer to LQ / UQ − median > median − LQ, so positive skew (1).
6. 34 ÷ 10 = 3.4 (1).
7. 2.4 × 30 = 72 (1). Between 60 and 70: 2.4 × 10 (1) = 24 (1).
8. Wider classes look bigger because the area (not the height) is what the eye compares, so their frequency is overstated (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/statistics/07-averages.md -->

## Averages

This file covers the mode, median and mean of lists, frequency tables and grouped data (including the median by linear interpolation), the effect of changing or transforming data, (Higher) weighted and geometric means, choosing the right average, and comparing data sets using averages. Mean seasonal variation is in file 11.

### Overview

Mode, median and arithmetic mean, choosing an average and comparing averages are on both tiers. (HT only) Weighted mean, geometric mean and mean seasonal variation; on Edexcel, (HT only) grouped data with unequal class widths.

#### Board differences

- AQA 8382: 3.4.1 (D1a) calculate averages, 3.4.2 (D1b) choose an average (including when a geometric mean is more useful), 3.5.7 (E3a) compare data sets using averages. AQA uses "modal group".
- Edexcel 1ST0: 2b.01 calculate (including linear interpolation for the median of grouped data, the effect of adding or removing a value, and the effect of scaling and translating data), 2b.02 choose, 2b.03 compare. Edexcel uses "modal class".
- On both boards "mean" means the arithmetic mean unless "geometric" is stated.

### 3.4.1 / 2b.01 Mode, median and mean

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1 (D1a) · Edexcel 2b.01. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Mode: the most common value. For grouped data, the modal class (group) has the highest frequency.
- Median: the middle value when the data is in order; for n values it is the (n + 1)/2 th value.
- Mean: total of the values ÷ number of values.

```latex
\bar{x} = \frac{\sum x}{n} \qquad \bar{x} = \frac{\sum fx}{\sum f}
```

- For grouped data, estimate the mean using class midpoints.
- For grouped data, estimate the median by reading a cumulative frequency graph or by linear interpolation.
- Know how the averages change when a value is added or removed, and when every value is scaled (multiplied) or translated (added to).

#### Explained

Frequency table (discrete): multiply each value by its frequency, add, and divide by the total frequency. The median is found by counting through the cumulative frequencies to the (n + 1)/2 th value.

Grouped data: the exact values are unknown, so use the midpoint of each class as a stand-in. The answer is an estimate.

Median by linear interpolation: find the class containing the n/2 th value, then assume the values in that class are spread evenly:

```latex
\text{median} \approx L + \frac{\frac{n}{2} - F}{f} \times w
```

where L is the lower boundary of the median class, F the cumulative frequency before it, f its frequency and w its width.

Transformations: if every value has k added, the mean, median and mode all increase by k (the spread does not change). If every value is multiplied by k, all three averages are multiplied by k.

Adding or removing a value:

- The mean changes unless the value equals the mean. New mean = (old total ± value) ÷ new n.
- The median may shift to a neighbouring value.

#### Worked example

Discrete frequency table. Number of pets owned by 30 students:

| Pets x | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Frequency f | 7 | 11 | 6 | 4 | 2 |

1. Mode: 1 (frequency 11).
2. Median: (30 + 1)/2 = 15.5th value. Cumulative: 7, 18, … so the 15th and 16th values are both 1. Median = 1.
3. Mean: Σfx = 0 + 11 + 12 + 12 + 8 = 43. Mean = 43 ÷ 30 = 1.43 (2 d.p.).

Grouped data. Journey times of 50 commuters:

| Time t (min) | Frequency | Midpoint | f × midpoint |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 < t ≤ 10 | 8 | 5 | 40 |
| 10 < t ≤ 20 | 17 | 15 | 255 |
| 20 < t ≤ 30 | 15 | 25 | 375 |
| 30 < t ≤ 40 | 10 | 35 | 350 |

1. Estimated mean = 1020 ÷ 50 = 20.4 min.
2. Modal class: 10 < t ≤ 20.
3. Median: n/2 = 25. Cumulative frequencies 8, 25, 40, 50. The 25th value is at the top of the 10–20 class: median ≈ 10 + (25 − 8)/17 × 10 = 20 min.

Effect of change: the mean mass of 12 parcels is 3.5 kg. One parcel of 6.1 kg is removed. Find the new mean.

1. Old total = 12 × 3.5 = 42 kg.
2. New total = 42 − 6.1 = 35.9 kg; new n = 11.
3. New mean = 35.9 ÷ 11 = 3.26 kg (3 s.f.).

Transformation: temperatures in °C have mean 15. Find the mean in °F, using F = 1.8C + 32.

1. Mean °F = 1.8 × 15 + 32 = 59 °F.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing Σfx by the number of rows instead of Σf.
- Using class boundaries instead of midpoints for the estimated mean.
- Giving the median of a frequency table as the middle frequency.
- Saying the mean of grouped data is exact.

### 3.4.1 / 2b.01 Weighted mean and geometric mean

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1 (D1a) · Edexcel 2b.01. Tier: Higher only (this part).*

#### What you need to know

- (HT only) Weighted mean: each value counts according to its weight:

```latex
\text{weighted mean} = \frac{\sum wx}{\sum w}
```

- (HT only) Geometric mean of n positive values: the nth root of their product:

```latex
\text{geometric mean} = \sqrt[n]{x_1 \times x_2 \times \cdots \times x_n}
```

- (HT only) The geometric mean is the right average for rates of growth and percentage changes that multiply together.

#### Explained

(HT only) Weighted means are used when some parts matter more: course marks where the exam counts more than coursework, or an index where food is a bigger share of spending than clothes (file 09).

(HT only) If an investment grows by multipliers 1.10, 1.30 and 0.90 over three years, the overall multiplier is the product 1.287. The average yearly multiplier m must satisfy m³ = 1.287, so m is the geometric mean. The arithmetic mean of the multipliers (1.10) would give 1.10³ = 1.331, which is too high.

#### Worked example

(HT only) A student scores 64 in coursework (weight 2), 72 in a test (weight 3) and 58 in an exam (weight 5). Find the weighted mean.

1. Σwx = 2 × 64 + 3 × 72 + 5 × 58 = 128 + 216 + 290 = 634.
2. Σw = 10.
3. Weighted mean = 63.4.

(HT only) A town's population grew by 8%, then 2%, then 15% over three years. Find the average annual percentage growth.

1. Multipliers: 1.08, 1.02, 1.15.
2. Product = 1.26684.
3. Geometric mean = ∛1.26684 = 1.0820 (4 d.p.).
4. Average growth ≈ 8.2% per year.

#### Common mistakes

- (HT only) Taking the geometric mean of the percentages (8, 2, 15) instead of the multipliers.
- (HT only) Dividing by the number of values instead of the total weight.

### 3.4.2 / 2b.02 Choosing an average

*Refs: AQA 3.4.2 (D1b) · Edexcel 2b.02. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Justify the choice of average in context.

#### Explained

| Average | Use when | Drawback |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mode | Data is categorical, or you need the most popular item (shoe sizes to stock) | May not exist or there may be several; ignores most data |
| Median | Data is skewed or has outliers (incomes, house prices) | Ignores the actual size of most values |
| Mean | Data is fairly symmetrical with no outliers; you want every value used; you need it for standard deviation | Distorted by outliers |
| Geometric mean (HT only) | Averaging growth rates or ratios | Only for positive values |

#### Worked example

Salaries at a small firm (£000s): 21, 22, 22, 24, 25, 27, 95. Which average best represents a typical salary?

1. Mean = 236 ÷ 7 = 33.7 (£33 700), which is above six of the seven salaries because of 95.
2. Median = 24 (£24 000).
3. The median is better because the data contains an outlier (the 95) that pulls the mean up.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "the mean is most accurate" without considering outliers.

### 3.5.7 / 2b.03 Comparing data sets using averages

*Refs: AQA 3.5.7 (E3a) · Edexcel 2b.03. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Compare data sets using a suitable average (mode, modal class, median or mean), calculated or given (for example, from software output).
- Comparisons must be in context.

#### Explained

A full comparison names the average, says which is higher, and says what that means in the context: "The median time for Group A (14.2 s) is higher than for Group B (12.8 s), so Group A were generally slower." Usually it is paired with a comparison of spread (file 08).

#### Worked example

Software gives these summaries of daily rainfall (mm) in two towns in March: Town P mean 3.4, median 1.2; Town Q mean 2.9, median 2.5. Compare typical rainfall.

1. The means and medians differ a lot in Town P, suggesting a few very wet days (positive skew), so the median is the better comparison.
2. Town Q's median (2.5 mm) is higher than Town P's (1.2 mm), so on a typical day Town Q had more rain, even though Town P's mean is higher.

#### Common mistakes

- Comparing numbers without saying what they mean for the context.

### Check questions

1. Find the mean, median and mode of: 7, 3, 9, 3, 12, 6, 8, 3, 10. [3 marks]
2. A frequency table shows goals scored in 40 matches: 0 goals (9), 1 (14), 2 (10), 3 (5), 4 (2). Find the mean and the median. [4 marks]
3. Estimate the mean for: 0 < m ≤ 4 (frequency 5), 4 < m ≤ 8 (12), 8 < m ≤ 12 (18), 12 < m ≤ 20 (5). [3 marks]
4. Use linear interpolation to estimate the median for the data in question 3. [2 marks]
