5. The mean of 9 numbers is 14. A tenth number, 24, is added. Find the new mean. [2 marks]
6. Every value in a data set is multiplied by 3 and then 5 is added. The old median was 8. Find the new median. [1 mark]
7. (HT only) A shop's sales rose by 12% one year and fell by 4% the next. Find the geometric mean of the yearly multipliers, and hence the average yearly percentage change. [3 marks]
8. (HT only) A judge's scores carry weights 1, 1, 2: scores 7.5, 8.0 and 6.5. Find the weighted mean. [2 marks]
9. Explain why the median might be a better average than the mean for house prices. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. Mean 61 ÷ 9 = 6.78 (1); median: ordered 3, 3, 3, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 12 so 7 (1); mode 3 (1).
2. Σfx = 0 + 14 + 20 + 15 + 8 = 57 (1); mean 57 ÷ 40 = 1.425 (1). Median is between the 20th and 21st values (1); both are 1, so median 1 (1).
3. Midpoints 2, 6, 10, 16 (1); Σfx = 10 + 72 + 180 + 80 = 342 (1); 342 ÷ 40 = 8.55 (1).
4. n/2 = 20; median class 8–12, CF before = 17 (1); 8 + (20 − 17)/18 × 4 = 8.67 (1).
5. Total 9 × 14 + 24 = 150 (1); 150 ÷ 10 = 15 (1).
6. 3 × 8 + 5 = 29 (1).
7. 1.12 × 0.96 = 1.0752 (1); √1.0752 = 1.0369 (1); about 3.7% increase per year (1).
8. (7.5 + 8.0 + 2 × 6.5) ÷ 4 (1) = 28.5 ÷ 4 = 7.125 (1).
9. House prices are skewed by a few very expensive houses, which raise the mean; the median is not affected by them (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/statistics/08-spread-outliers-and-skewness.md -->

## Spread, outliers and skewness

This file covers the range, quartiles, interquartile range and percentiles; (Higher) interpercentile and interdecile ranges and standard deviation; outliers by inspection and (Higher) by calculation; skewness by inspection and (Higher) by calculation; and comparing data sets and samples using measures of average and spread together. Standardised scores are in file 14.

### Overview

On both tiers: range, quartiles, IQR, percentiles, outliers and skewness by inspection, commenting on outliers, comparing with median and IQR. (HT only) Interpercentile and interdecile ranges, standard deviation, outliers by calculation, skewness by calculation, comparing with mean and standard deviation.

#### Board differences

- AQA 8382: 3.4.3 (D2) skewness, 3.4.4 (D3a) spread, 3.4.5 (D3b) outliers, 3.5.8 (E3b) comparing spread, 3.5.9 (E3c) comparing samples, 3.5.13 (E5a) interpreting skewness, 3.5.14 (E5b) commenting on outliers. On AQA, quartile questions use data sets with n one less than a multiple of 4 (such as 7, 11, 15), so quartiles are always data values. The skewness formula is given in the question.
- Edexcel 1ST0: 2a.09 and 2a.10 skewness, 2c.01 to 2c.05. On Edexcel, n can be any value, so quartiles may fall between data values; any clear method is accepted. Edexcel expects you to know the outlier rules and to apply the standard deviation formula (given for a list) to grouped data. The skewness formula is on the formulae sheet.
- Both boards: pair the median with the IQR, and the mean with the standard deviation. Do not mix them.

### 3.4.4 / 2c.01 Measures of spread

*Refs: AQA 3.4.4 (D3a) · Edexcel 2c.01. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Range = largest − smallest.
- Quartiles split ordered data into four equal parts. For n values: lower quartile (LQ) is the (n + 1)/4 th value, median the (n + 1)/2 th, upper quartile (UQ) the 3(n + 1)/4 th.
- Interquartile range: IQR = UQ − LQ. It covers the middle 50% and is not affected by extremes.
- Percentiles split data into 100 parts: the 90th percentile has 90% of values below it. Read them from a cumulative frequency graph.
- (HT only) Interpercentile range, such as the 10th to 90th: the difference between two percentiles. The interdecile range is the 10th to 90th percentile range (the middle 80%).
- (HT only) Standard deviation measures the typical distance of the values from the mean.

```latex
\sigma = \sqrt{\frac{\sum (x-\bar{x})^2}{n}} = \sqrt{\frac{\sum x^2}{n} - \bar{x}^2}
```

```latex
\sigma = \sqrt{\frac{\sum f (x-\bar{x})^2}{\sum f}} = \sqrt{\frac{\sum f x^2}{\sum f} - \left(\frac{\sum fx}{\sum f}\right)^2}
```

- (HT only) For grouped data use class midpoints for x. Calculator statistics functions are allowed.

#### Explained

The range uses only two values, so one extreme value changes it a lot. The IQR ignores the top and bottom quarters, so it is resistant to outliers. (HT only) The standard deviation uses every value; it is in the same units as the data, and is sensitive to outliers, like the mean.

(HT only) The interdecile range is a compromise: it covers more of the data than the IQR but still cuts off the extreme tenth at each end.

#### Worked example

Data: 4, 7, 8, 10, 11, 13, 15, 18, 20, 21, 30 (n = 11).

1. Range = 30 − 4 = 26.
2. LQ: (11 + 1)/4 = 3rd value = 8. Median: 6th value = 13. UQ: 9th value = 20.
3. IQR = 20 − 8 = 12.

(Edexcel, n not one less than a multiple of 4) Data: 2, 5, 6, 9, 12, 14 (n = 6).

1. Median: 3.5th value = (6 + 9)/2 = 7.5.
2. LQ: (6 + 1)/4 = 1.75th value. A simple accepted method: take the median of the lower half (2, 5, 6) = 5. UQ: median of the upper half (9, 12, 14) = 12. IQR = 7. (Show the method you use.)

(HT only) Standard deviation of 3, 5, 6, 8, 13.

1. Mean = 35 ÷ 5 = 7.
2. Σx² = 9 + 25 + 36 + 64 + 169 = 303.
3. σ = √(303/5 − 7²) = √(60.6 − 49) = √11.6 = 3.41 (3 s.f.).

(HT only) Grouped standard deviation.

| Midpoint x | f | fx | fx² |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 5 | 4 | 20 | 100 |
| 15 | 10 | 150 | 2250 |
| 25 | 6 | 150 | 3750 |
| Total | 20 | 320 | 6100 |

1. Mean = 320 ÷ 20 = 16.
2. σ = √(6100/20 − 16²) = √(305 − 256) = √49 = 7.

(HT only) Interdecile range from a cumulative frequency graph of 200 values: read across at 20 (10th percentile) and 180 (90th percentile). If these give 31 and 74, the interdecile range is 74 − 31 = 43.

#### Common mistakes

- Finding quartiles without ordering the data first.
- (HT only) Forgetting to square-root, or subtracting the mean before squaring the total (Σx²/n − x̄², not (Σx − x̄)²/n).
- (HT only) Using Σx² instead of Σfx² for frequency tables.

### 3.4.5, 3.5.14 / 2c.02, 2c.03 Outliers

*Refs: AQA 3.4.5 (D3b), 3.5.14 (E5b) · Edexcel 2c.02, 2c.03. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- An outlier is a value that does not fit the pattern of the rest. Spot it by inspection.
- (HT only) By calculation, a value is an outlier if it is:
  - less than LQ − 1.5 × IQR or more than UQ + 1.5 × IQR, or
  - more than 3 standard deviations from the mean (outside x̄ ± 3σ).
- Comment on outliers in context: they may be genuine unusual values (keep them) or errors in recording (correct or remove them).
- On a box plot, outliers are marked with a cross and the whisker stops at the most extreme value that is not an outlier.

#### Worked example

(HT only) For the data 4, 7, 8, 10, 11, 13, 15, 18, 20, 21, 30 above (LQ 8, UQ 20, IQR 12), is 30 an outlier?

1. Upper limit = 20 + 1.5 × 12 = 38. Lower limit = 8 − 18 = −10.
2. 30 < 38, so 30 is not an outlier.

(HT only) Exam marks have mean 54 and standard deviation 9. Are 82 and 26 outliers?

1. Limits: 54 ± 3 × 9 = 27 to 81.
2. 82 is above 81, so it is an outlier. 26 is below 27, so it is also an outlier.

Comment: a recorded adult height of 2.31 m in a survey of 50 people. This could be a typing error (1.31 m or 2.13 m are also unusual), so check the original record; if it is genuine, keep it but note it.

#### Common mistakes

- Removing every outlier automatically. Only remove errors.
- (HT only) Adding 1.5 × IQR to the median instead of the quartile.

### 3.4.3, 3.5.13 / 2a.09, 2a.10 Skewness

*Refs: AQA 3.4.3 (D2), 3.5.13 (E5a) · Edexcel 2a.09, 2a.10. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Symmetrical: mean = median = mode; median − LQ = UQ − median.
- Positive skew (tail to the right): mean > median > mode; median − LQ < UQ − median.
- Negative skew (tail to the left): mean < median < mode; median − LQ > UQ − median.
- Identify skew by inspection from diagrams or summary values, and interpret it in context.
- (HT only) Calculate skewness using the given formula:

```latex
\text{skew} = \frac{3(\text{mean} - \text{median})}{\text{standard deviation}}
```

  A positive value means positive skew, negative means negative skew, and near zero means roughly symmetrical. You are not asked to judge how strong the skew is (AQA).

#### Explained

With positive skew, most values are small with a few large ones stretching the tail, so the values above the median are more spread out than those below it. Incomes are a classic example. Negative skew suits an easy test, where most people score highly and a few score low.

#### Worked example

Box plot: LQ 22, median 26, UQ 40. Describe the skew.

1. Median − LQ = 4; UQ − median = 14.
2. 4 < 14, so positive skew: the upper half of the data is much more spread out.

(HT only) Data has mean 48.2, median 51.0 and standard deviation 8.4. Calculate the skewness and interpret it.

1. Skew = 3(48.2 − 51.0) ÷ 8.4 = −8.4 ÷ 8.4 = −1.
2. Negative skew: a few low values pull the mean below the median.

#### Common mistakes

- Naming skew by where the peak is. Positive skew has its peak on the left and its tail on the right.

### 3.5.8 / 2c.04 Comparing data sets using spread

*Refs: AQA 3.5.8 (E3b) · Edexcel 2c.04. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Compare spread using range, IQR or percentiles, and (HT only) standard deviation, calculated or given.
- Interpret in context: a smaller spread means more consistent data.

#### Worked example

(HT only) Two machines fill bags of flour. Machine A: mean 1002 g, standard deviation 3.1 g. Machine B: mean 1005 g, standard deviation 7.8 g. Compare.

1. Average: B's mean is 3 g higher, so B tends to put in more flour.
2. Spread: A's standard deviation is smaller, so A's bags are more consistent in mass.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a bigger spread means "higher values". It means more variable values.

### 3.5.9 / 2c.05 Comparing samples and populations

*Refs: AQA 3.5.9 (E3c) · Edexcel 2c.05. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Use median and IQR to compare samples, or a sample with its population.
- (HT only) Or use an interpercentile or interdecile range, or the mean and standard deviation.
- Always use a matching pair: median with IQR (or interpercentile range), mean with standard deviation.

#### Worked example

A national survey gives the population median sleep time for teenagers as 7.6 hours with IQR 1.4 hours. A sample from one school has median 6.9 hours and IQR 2.1 hours. Compare.

1. The school's median is 0.7 hours lower, so students at this school typically sleep less than teenagers nationally.
2. The school's IQR is larger, so sleep times vary more at the school.
3. The sample is from one school, so it may not represent all teenagers.

#### Common mistakes

- Pairing the mean with the IQR.
- Forgetting to mention the context or the reliability of the sample.

### Check questions

1. For 3, 6, 6, 9, 11, 14, 15, 18, 22, 25, 31 find the median, quartiles and IQR. [3 marks]
2. (HT only) Show whether 31 is an outlier in the data in question 1. [2 marks]
3. A data set has LQ 40, median 44, UQ 46. Describe the skewness and give a reason. [2 marks]
4. Explain why the IQR may be a better measure of spread than the range. [1 mark]
5. (HT only) Calculate the mean and standard deviation of 2, 4, 4, 5, 7, 8. [3 marks]
6. (HT only) Estimate the standard deviation for: midpoint 10 (frequency 3), midpoint 20 (frequency 5), midpoint 30 (frequency 2). [3 marks]
7. (HT only) A data set has mean 62, median 58 and standard deviation 10. Calculate the skewness using 3(mean − median) ÷ standard deviation, and interpret it. [2 marks]
8. (HT only) Daily sales have mean 140 and standard deviation 15. Find the limits outside which a day's sales would count as an outlier. [2 marks]
9. Class X test scores: median 64, IQR 10. Class Y: median 58, IQR 22. Compare the two classes. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Median 6th value = 14 (1); LQ 3rd = 6, UQ 9th = 22 (1); IQR 16 (1).
2. UQ + 1.5 × IQR = 22 + 24 = 46 (1); 31 < 46 so not an outlier (1).
3. Median − LQ = 4 > UQ − median = 2 (1), so negative skew (1).
4. The IQR is not affected by extreme values / outliers (1).
5. Mean 30 ÷ 6 = 5 (1); Σx² = 4 + 16 + 16 + 25 + 49 + 64 = 174 (1); σ = √(29 − 25) = 2 (1).
6. Σf = 10, Σfx = 30 + 100 + 60 = 190, mean 19 (1); Σfx² = 300 + 2000 + 1800 = 4100 (1); σ = √(410 − 361) = 7 (1).
7. 3 × 4 ÷ 10 = 1.2 (1); positive skew: a few high values pull the mean above the median (1).
8. 140 ± 3 × 15 (1): below 95 or above 185 (1).
9. Class X has the higher median, so scored better on average (1); Class X has the smaller IQR, so its scores were more consistent (1).

## Probability

This file covers the language and notation of events, mutually exclusive and independent events, Venn diagrams, tree diagrams and two-way tables, conditional probability, and modelling with probability.

### Overview

AS: mutually exclusive and independent events, and using diagrams to calculate probabilities. A-level only: conditional probability with the formula P(A|B) = P(A ∩ B)/P(B), testing independence with it, and modelling with probability.

#### Board differences

- AQA M1 to M3; Edexcel Statistics 3.1 to 3.3.
- OCR H240: 2.03a, 2.03b (AS); 2.03c to 2.03e (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: Mu1 to Mu3 (AS); Mu4 to Mu7 (A-level only). In MEI, Venn diagrams and the general addition rule are in the A-level part (Mu4, Mu5); in OCR H240 diagrams are AS (2.03b). MR13 (modelling with probability and distributions) is also covered here and in file 22.

### M1 Mutually exclusive and independent events

*Refs: AQA M1 · Edexcel Statistics 3.1 · OCR 2.03a, 2.03b · MEI Mu1, Mu2, Mu3, Mu4, Mu5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Notation: A ∩ B (A and B), A ∪ B (A or B or both), A′ (not A).
- Mutually exclusive events cannot happen together: P(A ∩ B) = 0, so P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B).
- Independent events do not affect each other: P(A ∩ B) = P(A) × P(B).
- The general addition rule:

```latex
P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B)
```

- Use Venn diagrams, tree diagrams, sample space diagrams and two-way tables.
- Link to distributions: for a discrete distribution, add probabilities of separate outcomes; for a continuous one, probabilities are areas under a curve.

#### Explained

**Venn diagrams.** Fill in from the middle outwards: put P(A ∩ B) in the overlap first, then the rest of each circle, then the region outside both. The whole rectangle has probability 1.

**Tree diagrams.** Multiply along branches for "and"; add the end results of different branches for "or". Branches from each point add to 1.

**Mutually exclusive versus independent.** They are different ideas. Two events with non-zero probabilities cannot be both: if they are mutually exclusive, knowing one happened tells you the other did not, so they are dependent.

**Testing independence.** Check whether P(A ∩ B) = P(A) × P(B).

#### Worked example

P(A) = 0.4 and P(B) = 0.3. Find P(A ∪ B) if (a) A and B are mutually exclusive; (b) A and B are independent.

1. (a) 0.4 + 0.3 = 0.7.
2. (b) P(A ∩ B) = 0.12, so P(A ∪ B) = 0.4 + 0.3 − 0.12 = 0.58.

P(A) = 0.5, P(B) = 0.35 and P(A ∪ B) = 0.65. Find P(A ∩ B) and decide whether A and B are independent.

1. 0.65 = 0.5 + 0.35 − P(A ∩ B), so P(A ∩ B) = 0.2.
2. P(A) × P(B) = 0.175 ≠ 0.2, so A and B are not independent.
3. Venn regions: A only 0.3, overlap 0.2, B only 0.15, outside 0.35.

A bag has 5 red and 3 blue counters. Two are taken without replacement. Find P(both the same colour).

1. P(RR) = 5/8 × 4/7 = 20/56. P(BB) = 3/8 × 2/7 = 6/56.
2. P(same) = 26/56 = 13/28.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding probabilities of events that are not mutually exclusive.
- Assuming independence without being told or checking.
- Forgetting the region outside both circles in a Venn diagram.

### M2 Conditional probability

*Refs: AQA M2 · Edexcel Statistics 3.2 · OCR 2.03c, 2.03d · MEI Mu6, Mu7. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) P(A | B) means the probability of A given that B has happened.

```latex
P(A \mid B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)} \qquad P(A \cap B) = P(A \mid B)\,P(B)
```

- (A-level only) Use tree diagrams, Venn diagrams and two-way tables for conditional probability.
- (A-level only) A and B are independent if and only if P(A | B) = P(A) (equivalently P(B | A) = P(B)).

#### Explained

"Given B" shrinks the sample space to B. On a Venn diagram, P(A | B) is the overlap divided by the whole of circle B. In a two-way table, it is the cell divided by the row (or column) total for B.

On a tree diagram, the second set of branches already are conditional probabilities. To reverse the condition (find P(first | second)), work out P(second) by adding all routes to it, then divide.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Using P(A) = 0.5, P(B) = 0.35, P(A ∩ B) = 0.2 from above, find P(A | B) and P(B | A′).

1. P(A | B) = 0.2/0.35 = 4/7.
2. P(B ∩ A′) = 0.15 and P(A′) = 0.5, so P(B | A′) = 0.3.

(A-level only) 120 students: 55 take Physics. Of the 50 who take Chemistry, 30 also take Physics. One student is picked at random. Find P(Physics | Chemistry) and decide whether taking the two subjects is independent.

1. P(Physics | Chemistry) = 30/50 = 0.6.
2. P(Physics) = 55/120 = 0.458. These differ, so the events are not independent.

(A-level only) In the counter example (5 red, 3 blue, two taken without replacement), find P(first red | both the same colour).

1. P(RR) = 20/56. P(same) = 26/56.
2. P(first red | same) = (20/56)/(26/56) = 10/13.

(A-level only) 2% of people have a condition. A test is positive for 95% of people with the condition and for 10% of people without it. Find the probability that someone who tests positive has the condition.

1. P(condition and positive) = 0.02 × 0.95 = 0.019.
2. P(no condition and positive) = 0.98 × 0.10 = 0.098.
3. P(positive) = 0.117.
4. P(condition | positive) = 0.019/0.117 = 0.162.
5. Most positive results are false positives, because the condition is rare.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Confusing P(A | B) with P(B | A).
- (A-level only) Dividing by P(A) instead of P(B) in P(A | B).
- (A-level only) Using the "with replacement" probabilities when items are not replaced.

### M3 Modelling with probability

*Refs: AQA M3 · Edexcel Statistics 3.3 · OCR 2.03e · MEI MR13. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) Use probability to model real situations, criticise the assumptions made (such as independence or equally likely outcomes), and judge the likely effect of more realistic assumptions.

#### Explained

Probability models rely on assumptions that are rarely exactly true. The probability that a train is late may not be the same every day (it depends on weather and time of day), and whether it is late today may affect tomorrow (staff shortages last several days). If an assumption fails, say how the answer would change: assuming independence when bad days cluster together usually underestimates the chance of several bad days in a row.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) A model says each day independently has a probability of 0.2 of rain. (a) Find the probability of no rain in a 7-day week. (b) Criticise the model.

1. (a) 0.8⁷ = 0.210.
2. (b) Rain is more likely in some seasons, so 0.2 will not suit every week. Wet days tend to come in runs, so days are not independent; the chance of a fully dry week is probably higher than the model says, because dry spells also cluster.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Criticising a model without reference to the context.

### Check questions

1. P(A) = 0.3, P(B) = 0.45 and A, B are independent. Find P(A ∩ B), P(A ∪ B) and P(A′ ∩ B). [4 marks]
2. In a class of 30, 18 play football, 12 play tennis and 5 play both. (a) Draw a Venn diagram. (b) Find the probability that a student picked at random plays neither. (c) (A-level only) Find P(tennis | football). [5 marks]
3. A bag has 4 green and 6 yellow balls. Three are taken without replacement. Find the probability that exactly two are green. [3 marks]
4. (A-level only) P(A) = 0.6, P(B | A) = 0.3 and P(B | A′) = 0.5. Find P(B) and P(A | B). [5 marks]
5. (A-level only) Events C and D have P(C) = 0.4, P(D) = 0.25 and P(C | D) = 0.4. Are C and D independent? Explain. [2 marks]
6. (A-level only) A factory has two machines. Machine X makes 70% of items and 3% of its items are faulty. Machine Y makes the rest and 5% of its items are faulty. An item is found to be faulty. Find the probability it came from machine Y. [4 marks]
7. (A-level only) A model assumes that each of 10 buses arrives on time independently with probability 0.9. Give one reason why independence might not hold. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. P(A ∩ B) = 0.135 (1); P(A ∪ B) = 0.3 + 0.45 − 0.135 = 0.615 (1); P(A′ ∩ B) = 0.45 − 0.135 (1) = 0.315 (1).
2. (a) Football only 13, both 5, tennis only 7, neither 5 (2). (b) 5/30 = 1/6 (1). (c) 5/18 (2).
3. Arrangements GGY: 3 orders (1); each 4/10 × 3/9 × 6/8 = 72/720 (1); total 216/720 = 0.3 (1).
4. P(A ∩ B) = 0.18 (1); P(A′ ∩ B) = 0.4 × 0.5 = 0.2 (1); P(B) = 0.38 (1); P(A | B) = 0.18/0.38 (1) = 9/19 or 0.474 (1).
5. P(C | D) = 0.4 = P(C) (1); so independent (1).
6. P(Y and faulty) = 0.3 × 0.05 = 0.015 (1); P(faulty) = 0.021 + 0.015 = 0.036 (1); P(Y | faulty) = 0.015/0.036 (1) = 0.417 (1).
7. e.g. traffic delays affect several buses at once, so one being late makes others more likely to be late (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/statistics/13-probability.md -->

## Probability

This file covers estimating probabilities from data (relative frequency), the probability scale, expected frequency, absolute and relative risk, comparing experimental and theoretical results to detect bias, two-way tables, sample space diagrams, tree diagrams and Venn diagrams for up to three events, mutually exclusive and exhaustive events, the addition and multiplication laws, and the notation for independent and conditional probability. The binomial and normal distributions are in file 14.

### Overview

All on both tiers except: (HT only) commenting on differences between experimental and theoretical values in terms of possible bias (AQA 3.5.25, Edexcel 3p.10, for example against a binomial model), and on Edexcel (HT only) the general addition law.

#### Board differences

- AQA 8382: 3.4.9 (D7) estimates from data; 3.5.1 to 3.5.6 (E1a to E2c); 3.5.11 (E4a) and 3.5.12 (E4b) notation; 3.5.25 (E10a) Higher only. AQA lists the probability scale with fractions, decimals and percentages.
- Edexcel 1ST0: 3p.01 to 3p.09 on both tiers; 3p.10 Higher only. Edexcel expects the words of likelihood (certain, likely, evens, unlikely, impossible), the terms mutually exclusive and exhaustive, the addition law P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B), and (HT only) the general addition law. Edexcel states the conditional probability formula must be known.
- Neither board requires formal significance tests.

### 3.4.9, 3.5.5 / 3p.01, 3p.06 Relative frequency and estimating probability

*Refs: AQA 3.4.9 (D7), 3.5.5 (E2b) · Edexcel 3p.01, 3p.06. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Estimate a probability from data using relative frequency:

```latex
\text{relative frequency} = \frac{\text{number of times the event happens}}{\text{number of trials}}
```

- As the number of trials increases, the relative frequency tends towards the theoretical probability (when outcomes are random).
- Larger samples give better estimates of probabilities and of population values.
- Estimate probabilities from relative frequency tables and diagrams.

#### Explained

A relative frequency graph plotted against the number of trials jumps about at first and then settles down near a value. That value is the best estimate of the probability.

#### Worked example

A drawing pin is dropped 250 times and lands point up 95 times. Estimate the probability it lands point up.

1. 95 ÷ 250 = 0.38.

Relative frequencies after 10, 50, 200 and 1000 spins of a spinner landing on red are 0.5, 0.36, 0.31 and 0.30. Which gives the best estimate, and is the spinner fair if it has 3 equal sections, one red?

1. 0.30 from 1000 spins is best (most trials).
2. A fair spinner would give 1/3 ≈ 0.33. 0.30 from 1000 spins is quite close but a little low, so there may be slight bias; more spins would help decide.

#### Common mistakes

- Trusting the estimate from the fewest trials.

### 3.5.1 / 3p.02 The probability scale

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1 (E1a) · Edexcel 3p.02. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Probabilities run from 0 (impossible) to 1 (certain), or 0% to 100%, written as fractions, decimals or percentages.
- Words: impossible, very unlikely, unlikely, evens (0.5), likely, very likely, certain.
- P(not A) = 1 − P(A).

#### Worked example

The probability it rains tomorrow is 35%. Find the probability it does not rain, and describe it in words.

1. 100% − 35% = 65% (0.65).
2. Likely.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing probabilities as ratios ("2:5") or as numbers above 1.

### 3.5.2 / 3p.03 Expected frequency

*Refs: AQA 3.5.2 (E1b) · Edexcel 3p.03. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Expected frequency = probability × number of trials (or × population size).

#### Worked example

The probability that a person in a town has blood group O is 0.46. In a sample of 350 people, how many would you expect to have group O?

1. 0.46 × 350 = 161.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing instead of multiplying.

### 3.5.3 / 3p.04 Absolute and relative risk

*Refs: AQA 3.5.3 (E1c) · Edexcel 3p.04. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Absolute risk: the probability of an event in a group (number affected ÷ number in the group).
- Relative risk: compares the risk in two groups:

```latex
\text{relative risk} = \frac{\text{risk in group A}}{\text{risk in group B}}
```

- Express risks as expected frequencies, for example "about 6 in every 100 people".

#### Explained

A relative risk of 2 means group A is twice as likely to have the event as group B. A relative risk can sound alarming even when the absolute risk is tiny: doubling a 1-in-10 000 risk still leaves it at 2 in 10 000.

#### Worked example

Of 400 learner drivers taught by Instructor P, 252 passed first time. Of 300 taught by Instructor Q, 162 passed first time.

1. Absolute risk (probability) of passing with P: 252 ÷ 400 = 0.63.
2. With Q: 162 ÷ 300 = 0.54.
3. Relative risk of passing with P compared with Q: 0.63 ÷ 0.54 = 1.17. Learners with P were about 1.17 times as likely to pass first time.
4. As expected frequencies: about 63 in every 100 with P pass, against 54 in every 100 with Q.

#### Common mistakes

- Comparing the numbers who passed (252 and 162) rather than proportions, when the groups are different sizes.
- Quoting relative risk without the absolute risk.

### 3.5.4, 3.5.25 / 3p.05, 3p.10 Experimental against theoretical: bias

*Refs: AQA 3.5.4 (E2a), 3.5.25 (E10a) · Edexcel 3p.05, 3p.10. Tier: F & H (3.5.25 and 3p.10 Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Compare experimental results with theoretical predictions to judge whether an experiment, coin, dice or spinner might be biased.
- (HT only) Comment on the size of differences between experimental and theoretical values (for example, observed frequencies against expected frequencies from a binomial model) in terms of possible bias. No formal tests.

#### Explained

Some difference from the expected values is normal because of chance. A big difference over many trials suggests bias. Over few trials, even a large difference may be chance, so say "may be biased" and suggest more trials.

#### Worked example

A dice is rolled 600 times: 1 (88), 2 (97), 3 (102), 4 (95), 5 (91), 6 (127). Is it fair?

1. Expected frequency for each score if fair: 600 × 1/6 = 100.
2. Most are close to 100, but 6 appears 127 times, 27 more than expected over a large number of rolls.
3. The dice may be biased towards 6.

(HT only) Three coins are tossed 80 times and the number of heads recorded: 0 heads (4), 1 head (22), 2 heads (31), 3 heads (23). The binomial model for fair coins gives probabilities 1/8, 3/8, 3/8, 1/8 (see file 14). Comment.

1. Expected: 10, 30, 30, 10.
2. Observed 3 heads 23 times against 10 expected, and 0 heads only 4 against 10.
3. Far more heads than expected over 80 trials, so the coins may be biased towards heads.

#### Common mistakes

- Expecting the results to match the theory exactly.

### 3.5.6 / 3p.07 Diagrams for outcomes

*Refs: AQA 3.5.6 (E2c) · Edexcel 3p.07. Tier: F & H (part Higher only on Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- Use two-way tables, sample space diagrams (systematic lists or grids), tree diagrams and Venn diagrams to show all outcomes for up to three events, and find probabilities from them.
- Mutually exclusive events cannot happen together. Exhaustive events cover all possible outcomes. Probabilities of mutually exclusive and exhaustive events add to 1.
- Addition law for mutually exclusive events: P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B).
- (HT only, Edexcel) General addition law: P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A and B).

#### Explained

A sample space diagram for two dice is a 6 by 6 grid of 36 equally likely outcomes. For tree diagrams, multiply along the branches and add the results of the branches you want. Venn diagrams with three circles have eight regions (including outside all three); fill the centre first and work outwards.

#### Worked example

Sample space: two fair spinners, one numbered 1 to 3 and one numbered 1 to 4, are spun and the scores added. Find P(total = 5).

1. Outcomes: 3 × 4 = 12.
2. Totals of 5: (1, 4), (2, 3), (3, 2). That is 3 outcomes.
3. P = 3/12 = 1/4.

Tree diagram: a bag has 4 green and 6 blue counters. One is taken and not replaced, then another. Find P(one of each colour).

1. P(G then B) = 4/10 × 6/9 = 24/90.
2. P(B then G) = 6/10 × 4/9 = 24/90.
3. Total = 48/90 = 8/15.

Three-circle Venn diagram: 60 students; 30 play football (F), 25 tennis (T), 20 swim (S). 8 do F and T, 6 do F and S, 5 do T and S, and 3 do all three. Find P(a student does none).

1. All three: 3.
2. F and T only: 8 − 3 = 5. F and S only: 6 − 3 = 3. T and S only: 5 − 3 = 2.
3. F only: 30 − 5 − 3 − 3 = 19. T only: 25 − 5 − 2 − 3 = 15. S only: 20 − 3 − 2 − 3 = 12.
4. Total in circles: 3 + 5 + 3 + 2 + 19 + 15 + 12 = 59.
5. None: 60 − 59 = 1, so P = 1/60.

(HT only, Edexcel) P(A) = 0.5, P(B) = 0.4, P(A and B) = 0.15. Find P(A or B).

1. 0.5 + 0.4 − 0.15 = 0.75.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding along tree branches instead of multiplying.
- Forgetting to change the second-branch probabilities when items are not replaced.
- Using P(A) + P(B) for events that are not mutually exclusive.

### 3.5.11 / 3p.08 Independent events

*Refs: AQA 3.5.11 (E4a) · Edexcel 3p.08. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Events are independent if one happening does not change the probability of the other.
- Multiplication law for independent events: P(A and B) = P(A) × P(B).
- Notation: P(A | B) means "the probability of A given that B has happened". For independent events P(A | B) = P(A) and P(B | A) = P(B).
- To test independence, check whether P(A and B) = P(A) × P(B).

#### Worked example

In a survey of 200 people, 80 wear glasses, 50 are left-handed, and 20 both wear glasses and are left-handed. Are wearing glasses and being left-handed independent?

1. P(G) = 80/200 = 0.4. P(L) = 50/200 = 0.25.
2. P(G) × P(L) = 0.1.
3. P(G and L) = 20/200 = 0.1.
4. They are equal, so the events are independent (in this sample).

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing independent with mutually exclusive. Mutually exclusive events cannot both happen, so they are never independent (unless one has probability 0).

### 3.5.12 / 3p.09 Conditional probability

*Refs: AQA 3.5.12 (E4b) · Edexcel 3p.09. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Conditional probability is the probability of an event given that another has happened:

```latex
P(B \mid A) = \frac{P(A \text{ and } B)}{P(A)}
```

- Find conditional probabilities from two-way tables, Venn diagrams, sample space diagrams and tree diagrams: restrict attention to the "given" group.

#### Worked example

A two-way table of 150 students:

| | Walks | Bus | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Year 10 | 36 | 44 | 80 |
| Year 11 | 30 | 40 | 70 |
| Total | 66 | 84 | 150 |

Find P(walks | Year 11).

1. Given Year 11, there are 70 students; 30 walk.
2. P = 30/70 = 3/7.

Find P(Year 11 | walks).

1. 66 students walk; 30 are in Year 11.
2. P = 30/66 = 5/11.

Using the formula: P(A) = 0.6, P(A and B) = 0.15. Find P(B | A).

1. 0.15 ÷ 0.6 = 0.25.

Tree diagram backwards: P(late) = 0.2. If late, P(misses breakfast) = 0.7; if not late, P(misses breakfast) = 0.1. Find P(late | misses breakfast).

1. P(late and misses) = 0.2 × 0.7 = 0.14.
2. P(not late and misses) = 0.8 × 0.1 = 0.08.
3. P(misses) = 0.22.
4. P(late | misses) = 0.14 ÷ 0.22 = 7/11 ≈ 0.636.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing by the grand total instead of the "given" total.
- Mixing up P(A | B) and P(B | A).

### Check questions

1. A biased coin is tossed 400 times and lands heads 248 times. Estimate the probability of heads, and the number of heads expected in 1500 tosses. [2 marks]
2. The probability that a battery is faulty is 0.03. How many faulty batteries would you expect in a box of 250? [1 mark]
3. In a trial, 12 of 800 people taking a medicine had a side effect, against 4 of 800 taking a dummy pill. Find the relative risk and explain what it means, using the absolute risks. [3 marks]
4. A spinner with 5 equal sections numbered 1 to 5 is spun 200 times and lands on 5 a total of 61 times. Is the spinner fair? Explain. [2 marks]
5. Two fair dice are rolled. Find the probability the total is 9. [2 marks]
6. A bag has 5 red and 3 yellow sweets. Two are taken without replacement. Find the probability both are the same colour. [3 marks]
7. P(A) = 0.3, P(B) = 0.5 and A and B are independent. Find P(A and B), and write down P(A | B). [2 marks]
8. In a group of 90 people, 40 have a cat, 35 have a dog, and 12 have both. Find P(has a dog | has a cat). [2 marks]
9. (HT only, Edexcel) P(X) = 0.45, P(Y) = 0.35, P(X or Y) = 0.6. Find P(X and Y). Are X and Y mutually exclusive? [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 248/400 = 0.62 (1); 0.62 × 1500 = 930 (1).
2. 0.03 × 250 = 7.5, so about 7 or 8 (1).
3. Absolute risks 12/800 = 0.015 and 4/800 = 0.005 (1); relative risk 3 (1); people taking the medicine were 3 times as likely to have a side effect, but it was still only 15 in every 1000 (1).
4. Expected 200 × 1/5 = 40 (1); 61 is much more than 40 over 200 spins, so the spinner is probably biased towards 5 (1).
5. Outcomes (3, 6), (4, 5), (5, 4), (6, 3): 4 out of 36 (1) = 1/9 (1).
6. P(RR) = 5/8 × 4/7 = 20/56 (1); P(YY) = 3/8 × 2/7 = 6/56 (1); total 26/56 = 13/28 (1).
7. 0.3 × 0.5 = 0.15 (1); P(A | B) = 0.3 (1).
8. 12 out of the 40 cat owners (1) = 12/40 = 0.3 (1).
9. 0.45 + 0.35 − 0.6 = 0.2 (1); no, because P(X and Y) is not 0 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/19-statistical-sampling.md -->

## Statistical sampling

This file covers populations and samples, censuses, sampling frames, the main sampling techniques, how to choose between them, and why different samples can give different conclusions.

### Overview

All AS content. Sampling ideas come back in hypothesis testing (file 23), where a sample is used to make an inference about a population. Large data set questions often ask you to criticise a sample drawn from the data set (file 20).

#### Board differences

The four boards name different lists of techniques:

| Board | Techniques named |
| --- | --- |
| AQA 7357 (K1) | simple random sampling and opportunity sampling are named; others may be asked about in context |
| Edexcel 9MA0 (Statistics 1.1) | simple random, stratified, systematic, quota and opportunity (convenience) sampling |
| OCR H240 (2.01a to 2.01d) | carry out simple random and opportunity sampling; know and critique, but not carry out, systematic, stratified, cluster and quota sampling. Random samples may be assumed to be taken without replacement from a large population unless stated. |
| OCR MEI H640 (Mp21 to Mp25) | simple random, opportunity, systematic, stratified, quota, cluster and self-selected samples; any other method will be explained in the question |

### K1 Populations, samples and sampling techniques

*Refs: AQA K1 · Edexcel Statistics 1.1 · OCR 2.01a, 2.01b, 2.01c, 2.01d · MEI Mp21, Mp22, Mp23, Mp24, Mp25. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A population is the whole group you want to know about. A sample is part of it.
- A census collects data from every member of the population.
- A sampling frame is a list of the members of the population that the sample is chosen from. A sampling unit is one member that can be sampled.
- Use a sample to make informal inferences about the population (for example, using a sample mean to estimate the population mean).
- Different samples can lead to different conclusions; larger samples tend to vary less.
- Use, select and criticise sampling techniques in context, including recognising possible bias.

#### Explained

**Census or sample?**

| | Census | Sample |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Advantages | complete and accurate picture | cheaper, quicker, less data to process; the only option if testing destroys the item |
| Disadvantages | expensive and slow; hard to reach everyone | may not represent the population; results vary between samples |

**Random methods** (need a sampling frame):

- **Simple random sampling.** Every sample of the required size is equally likely. Number the population 1 to N, then use a random number generator to pick n different numbers. Free from selection bias, but needs a full list and may, by chance, under-represent a group.
- **Systematic sampling.** Choose every kth member, where k = N/n, starting from a random member among the first k. Simple to do, but can be biased if the list has a repeating pattern.
- **Stratified sampling.** Split the population into groups (strata), such as year groups, and take a simple random sample from each, in proportion to its size. Represents each group fairly, but needs to know the size of each stratum and the strata must not overlap.
- **Cluster sampling** (MEI, OCR). Split the population into clusters (such as schools or streets), randomly choose some clusters, then sample within them. Cheaper when the population is spread out, but clusters may not be typical.

**Non-random methods** (no sampling frame needed):

- **Quota sampling.** An interviewer fills set numbers (quotas) of people from each group, choosing who to ask. Quick and needs no list, but the interviewer's choices can introduce bias.
- **Opportunity (convenience) sampling.** Use whoever is available at the time, such as the first 30 people leaving a shop. Easy and cheap, but unlikely to be representative.
- **Self-selected samples** (MEI). People volunteer, such as by replying to an online poll. Those with strong opinions are more likely to respond.

**Bias.** A sample is biased if the method makes some members more likely to be chosen in a way that affects the result. Examples: surveying a train station at 8 a.m. about commuting habits, or asking only one class about school dinners.

**Informal inference.** A sample statistic (mean, proportion) estimates the population value. It will not be exact, and another sample would give a different estimate. Bigger random samples give more reliable estimates.

#### Worked example

A sixth form has 500 students in Year 12 and 700 in Year 13. A stratified sample of 60 is wanted. How many from each year?

1. Total 1200. Year 12: 60 × 500/1200 = 25. Year 13: 60 × 700/1200 = 35.
2. Then take a simple random sample of 25 from the Year 12 list and 35 from the Year 13 list.

Describe how to take a systematic sample of 30 from a numbered list of 600 customers.

1. k = 600/30 = 20.
2. Choose a random start between 1 and 20, say 7, using a random number generator.
3. Take customers 7, 27, 47, ..., 587.

Describe how to take a simple random sample of 10 days from the 184 days in a data set.

1. Number the days 1 to 184.
2. Use a calculator's random integer function to generate numbers from 1 to 184.
3. Ignore repeats until there are 10 different numbers; select those days.

A researcher wants to estimate the mean time teenagers in a town spend on homework. She asks the first 40 students who leave the library on a Saturday. Name the method and give two criticisms.

1. Opportunity sampling.
2. Students at the library on a Saturday are likely to study more than average, so the estimate is likely to be too high.
3. Only one place and time is used, so students with jobs or other weekend commitments are missed. A random sample from a list of all teenagers in the town would be better.

Two students each take a random sample of 20 days from a data set and work out the mean daily rainfall. They get 1.8 mm and 2.6 mm. Explain why, and say how to get a more reliable estimate.

1. Random samples vary: each contains different days, so the sample means differ.
2. A larger sample (or combining the two samples into one of 40) gives a more reliable estimate of the population mean.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling any sample "random" just because it was not planned. Random sampling needs a list and a random process.
- Forgetting to say how a random number generator is used and what happens to repeats.
- In stratified sampling, taking equal numbers from each stratum instead of proportional numbers.
- Criticising a method without linking the criticism to the context.

### Check questions

1. Explain the difference between a population and a sample, and give one reason for using a sample rather than a census. [2 marks]
2. Define a sampling frame and give an example of one for a survey of members of a gym. [2 marks]
3. A company has 240 office staff, 360 factory staff and 120 drivers. A stratified sample of 60 is required. How many from each group? [3 marks]
4. Describe how to select a systematic sample of 25 from a list of 500 names. [3 marks]
5. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of quota sampling compared with stratified sampling. [2 marks]
6. A council wants to know residents' views on a new bus route. It posts a survey on its website and analyses all replies. (a) Name this type of sample. (b) Explain why the results might be biased. [3 marks]
7. Explain why two random samples from the same population may lead to different conclusions, and how this effect can be reduced. [2 marks]
8. A student wants to estimate the mean daily maximum temperature at one weather station using 15 days from a data set of 184 days. She uses the first 15 days. Criticise this method and suggest a better one. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. The population is the whole group of interest; a sample is a subset of it (1); one reason, e.g. cheaper, quicker, or testing would destroy every item (1).
2. A list of all members of the population from which the sample is drawn (1); e.g. the gym's membership list (1).
3. Total 720 (1); office 20, factory 30 (1); drivers 10 (1).
4. k = 500/25 = 20 (1); random start between 1 and 20 using random numbers (1); then every 20th name after it (1).
5. Advantage: no sampling frame needed / quick / cheap (1). Disadvantage: not random, so interviewer bias is possible (1).
6. (a) Self-selected (volunteer) sample (1). (b) Only people who visit the website and choose to reply are included (1); people with strong views, or regular bus users, are more likely to respond, so the sample may not represent all residents (1).
7. Each sample contains different members, so sample statistics vary (1); use larger samples (1).
8. The first 15 days all come from the start of the period, so they may be warmer or cooler than the rest (1); a simple random sample of 15 days, numbering days 1 to 184 and using random numbers (1); or a systematic sample with a random start (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/statistics/03-population-and-sampling.md -->

## Population and sampling

This file covers populations, sampling frames and samples, and the sampling methods: judgement, opportunity (convenience), cluster, quota, simple random, systematic and stratified.

### Overview

All on both tiers. (HT only) On Edexcel, stratifying by more than one category at once is Higher only.

#### Board differences

- AQA 8382: 3.2.8 (B3a) to 3.2.13 (B3f). AQA names judgement, opportunity, random, systematic and quota sampling and stratification; it does not name cluster sampling. AQA says you will not have to actually select a sample using dice, cards or random numbers in the exam, only show you understand how.
- Edexcel 1ST0: 1c.01 to 1c.06. Edexcel also names cluster sampling (with judgement, opportunity and quota). Edexcel may ask you to select items using random numbers, including dealing with repeats and numbers out of range, and to calculate strata sizes. (HT only) Stratifying by two categories at once (for example gender and year group) is Higher only on Edexcel.
- Both boards: stratification is something done before sampling, not a sampling method on its own.

### 3.2.8 / 1c.01 Population, sampling frame and sample

*Refs: AQA 3.2.8 (B3a) · Edexcel 1c.01. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Population: everything or everyone that could be studied for the investigation.
- Sampling frame: a list of the members of the population, from which a sample is chosen.
- Sample: the members actually chosen.
- A census collects data from every member of the population.
- Not every member of the population may be available for sampling: the sampling frame may be incomplete.

#### Explained

| | Census | Sample |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Advantages | Unbiased; complete picture; every member included | Cheaper; quicker; less data to process; the only option if testing destroys the item |
| Disadvantages | Expensive; slow; hard to reach everyone; impossible if testing destroys items | May not represent the population; results vary from sample to sample |

Sampling frames are rarely perfect. A school register is a good sampling frame for students at that school. The electoral roll misses people who are not registered to vote, so it is a poor frame for "all adults in a town".

#### Worked example

A factory makes 5000 light bulbs a day. It wants to know how long they last. Should it use a census or a sample? Suggest a sampling frame.

1. A sample: testing a bulb's lifetime means using it until it fails, so a census would destroy every bulb.
2. Sampling frame: a numbered list of the bulbs produced that day (for example, by serial number).

#### Common mistakes

- Mixing up the sampling frame (the list) and the sample (those chosen).
- Forgetting the "destructive testing" reason for sampling.

### 3.2.9 / 1c.02 Meaning of population in context

*Refs: AQA 3.2.9 (B3b) · Edexcel 1c.02. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- "Population" means whatever group the question is about: it need not be people.

#### Explained

The population could be all employees in one office, all women in the UK, all the trees in a park or all the cans made by a machine in a week. Defining it clearly is the first step in planning, because it decides the sampling frame and what conclusions can be drawn. A sample of Year 7 students tells you about Year 7, not the whole school.

#### Worked example

A council wants to know what residents of a village think of a new bus route. State the population.

1. All residents of the village (perhaps all adult residents, if only adults are asked). Visitors and people from other villages are not part of it.

#### Common mistakes

- Defining the population too broadly ("everyone") or too narrowly (only bus users, when the question asks about all residents).

### 3.2.10 / 1c.03 Judgement, opportunity, cluster and quota sampling

*Refs: AQA 3.2.10 (B3c) · Edexcel 1c.03. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Judgement sampling: the researcher uses their knowledge to choose members they think are typical.
- Opportunity (convenience) sampling: the first people available who fit, such as people walking past.
- Cluster sampling: the population is split into natural groups (clusters, for example schools or streets); some clusters are chosen at random and everyone (or a sample) within them is surveyed.
- Quota sampling: the researcher must find a set number from each group (for example 20 men and 20 women) but chooses who freely.
- Reasons for using them: cheap, quick, convenient, no sampling frame needed.
- Risks: they are not random, so they can be biased.

#### Explained

These methods are popular because random sampling needs a full list and can be slow.

- Judgement depends on the researcher's opinion; a different researcher might pick very different people.
- Opportunity sampling at a station at 8 am finds commuters and misses those who work from home.
- Cluster sampling saves travel: survey 5 schools fully rather than a few students in 200 schools. But the chosen clusters may differ from the rest.
- Quota sampling matches the proportions of the population (like stratified sampling), but within each group the interviewer chooses, so they may pick friendly-looking people.

#### Worked example

A market researcher stands outside a supermarket and asks the first 30 men and first 30 women who walk past about their shopping habits. Name the method and give one source of bias.

1. Quota sampling (fixed numbers of men and women, interviewer chooses).
2. Bias: only people who shop at that supermarket, at that time of day, are asked; people who shop online are missed.

#### Common mistakes

- Thinking quota sampling is random. It is not.
- Confusing cluster sampling (whole groups chosen) with stratified sampling (some from every group).

### 3.2.11 / 1c.04 Random, systematic and quota sampling to avoid bias

*Refs: AQA 3.2.11 (B3d) · Edexcel 1c.04. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Choose a sampling method that suits the problem and avoids bias.
- Simple random sampling: every member has the same chance of being chosen.
- Systematic sampling: choose a random starting point, then take every kth member from a list, where k = population size ÷ sample size.
- Systematic and quota sampling are generally not random.
- Systematic sampling can be biased if the list has a repeating pattern that matches the interval.
- Know the advantages and disadvantages of each method.

#### Explained

| Method | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Simple random | Unbiased; every member equally likely | Needs a full sampling frame; may by chance under-represent a group; can be slow to reach chosen people |
| Systematic | Simple and quick; spreads the sample through the list | Needs a list; biased if the list has a pattern with the same period |
| Quota | Quick; no sampling frame; matches proportions of groups | Not random; interviewer bias; non-response is replaced, which hides it |
| Stratified (see 3.2.13) | Each group fairly represented | Needs details of the strata and a frame for each |

A pattern problem: a factory machine makes a fault every 10th item. Sampling every 10th item could pick all faulty items or none.

#### Worked example

A club has 480 members listed alphabetically. Describe how to take a systematic sample of 30.

1. k = 480 ÷ 30 = 16.
2. Choose a random start from 1 to 16, for example 7.
3. Take members 7, 23, 39, 55, … (adding 16 each time) until 30 members are chosen. The last is 7 + 29 × 16 = 471.

#### Common mistakes

- Starting at member 1 every time: the start must be random.
- Saying systematic sampling is random. Only the start is random.

### 3.2.12 / 1c.05 Simple random samples

*Refs: AQA 3.2.12 (B3e) · Edexcel 1c.05. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- In a simple random sample every member, and every possible group of the sample size, is equally likely to be chosen.
- Methods: number every member of the sampling frame, then choose numbers using dice, cards or names drawn from a hat, random number tables or lists, or a calculator or computer random number function.
- Ignore random numbers that are repeated or out of range.

#### Explained

Steps for a random sample:

1. Get a sampling frame and number every member (for 300 members, 001 to 300).
2. Generate random numbers of the right length (three digits here).
3. Ignore 000, any number above 300, and repeats.
4. Continue until you have the sample size.

On a calculator, a random integer function (RanInt or similar) gives whole numbers in a range directly.

#### Worked example

A class has 60 students numbered 01 to 60. Use these random digits, read in pairs from the start, to choose 4 students:

72 15 60 08 15 93 41 00 27

1. 72: out of range, ignore.
2. 15: choose student 15.
3. 60: choose student 60.
4. 08: choose student 8.
5. 15: repeat, ignore.
6. 93: out of range, ignore.
7. 41: choose student 41.
8. Sample: 15, 60, 8, 41.

#### Common mistakes

- Including out-of-range numbers or repeats.
- Numbering with different lengths (1, 2 … 60) and reading digits in pairs, so 6 and 60 get confused. Use 01, 02 … 60.

### 3.2.13 / 1c.06 Stratification

*Refs: AQA 3.2.13 (B3f) · Edexcel 1c.06. Tier: F & H (part Higher only on Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- Stratification splits the population into groups (strata) such as gender or age group before sampling, so that each group is represented in proportion to its size.
- It is not a sampling method on its own: you still choose within each stratum, usually at random.
- Use it when the strata are likely to give different answers.
- Strata size = (size of stratum ÷ size of population) × sample size.
- (HT only, Edexcel) Stratify by more than one category at once, such as gender and year group.

#### Explained

If a school is 60% girls, a stratified sample makes 60% of the sample girls. A simple random sample might by chance be 75% girls.

After rounding, the strata sizes must still add up to the sample size. If rounding makes them total one too many or too few, adjust the stratum whose decimal part was closest to .5.

#### Worked example

A sports centre has 240 members aged under 18, 520 aged 18 to 59 and 140 aged 60 or over. A stratified sample of 50 is needed. How many from each age group?

1. Total 240 + 520 + 140 = 900.
2. Under 18: 240/900 × 50 = 13.33 → 13.
3. 18 to 59: 520/900 × 50 = 28.89 → 29.
4. 60 or over: 140/900 × 50 = 7.78 → 8.
5. Check: 13 + 29 + 8 = 50.
6. Then choose 13 under-18s at random from the list of under-18 members, and so on.

(HT only, Edexcel) Two categories: a year group has 70 boys and 50 girls in Year 10, and 60 boys and 60 girls in Year 11. A sample of 40 stratified by year and gender:

1. Total 240.
2. Year 10 boys: 70/240 × 40 = 11.67 → 12. Year 10 girls: 50/240 × 40 = 8.33 → 8.
3. Year 11 boys: 60/240 × 40 = 10. Year 11 girls: 10.
4. Check: 12 + 8 + 10 + 10 = 40.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing the sample equally between strata instead of in proportion.
- Calling stratification a sampling method and stopping there, without saying how members are chosen within each stratum.
- Rounding so the total is not the sample size.

### Check questions

1. Give two reasons why a sample might be used instead of a census. [2 marks]
2. Explain the difference between a sampling frame and a sample. [2 marks]
3. A company has 1200 employees listed by staff number. Describe how to take a systematic sample of 40. [3 marks]
4. Give one reason why systematic sampling might give a biased sample. [1 mark]
5. Students are numbered 001 to 450. Using the random digits 512 087 450 087 239 961 314, starting at the left, write down the first four students chosen. [2 marks]
6. A town has 3600 households in Area P, 2100 in Area Q and 1300 in Area R. A stratified sample of 70 households is taken. How many are taken from each area? [3 marks]
7. Explain one difference between quota sampling and stratified random sampling. [1 mark]
8. A researcher picks 4 of a county's 30 primary schools at random and questions every teacher in those 4 schools. Name this sampling method and give one disadvantage. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Any two (1 each): cheaper; quicker; less data to process; testing may destroy items; population too large or not all reachable.
2. Sampling frame: the list of members of the population (1). Sample: the members chosen from it to take part (1).
3. 1200 ÷ 40 = 30 (1); choose a random start between 1 and 30 (1); then take every 30th employee after that (1).
4. The list may have a repeating pattern with the same interval, so the chosen items may all share a feature (1).
5. 087 (1 for ignoring 512 as out of range and starting correctly); 087, 450, 239, 314 (1, ignoring the repeat 087 and 961).
6. Total 7000 (1). P: 3600/7000 × 70 = 36; Q: 21; R: 13 (1 for one correct, 2 for all three, total 70).
7. In quota sampling the interviewer chooses who to ask within each group, so it is not random; in stratified random sampling members of each stratum are chosen at random (1).
8. Cluster sampling (1). The 4 schools chosen may not be typical of all 30 / teachers in one school may share views (1).

<!-- source: notes/gcse/statistics/04-collecting-and-cleaning-data.md -->

## Collecting and cleaning data

This file covers where data comes from (experiments, simulation, questionnaires, observation, reference sources, census and sampling), reliability and validity, bias and sensitive questions, designing questions and data collection sheets, interviews, pilots, problems in collected data, cleaning data, and controlling extraneous variables.

### Overview

All on both tiers except: (HT only) level of control as a way to reduce distortion; (HT only) control groups; and, on Edexcel, (HT only) the random response technique and matched pairs.

#### Board differences

- AQA 8382: 3.2.5 (B2b), 3.2.6 (B2c), 3.2.7 (B2d), 3.2.14 (B4), 3.2.15 (B5a), 3.2.16 (B5b), 3.2.17 (B5c). AQA's numbering puts sampling (3.2.8 to 3.2.13, file 03) between them.
- Edexcel 1ST0: 1d.01 to 1d.07, in the same order as here. Edexcel's Higher guidance names the random response technique (1d.03) and matched pairs (1d.07), and asks about pilots for questionnaires and pre-tests for experiments (1d.04).

### 3.2.5 / 1d.01 Sources of data

*Refs: AQA 3.2.5 (B2b) · Edexcel 1d.01. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Data can come from: experiments (laboratory, field, natural), simulation, questionnaires, observation, reference sources, a census of the population, or a sample.
- Sources of secondary data must be acknowledged (say where it came from).
- Know the strengths and weaknesses of each in context, and be able to design a data collection sheet.

#### Explained

Experiments:

- Laboratory: carried out in controlled conditions. Easy to control other variables and repeat, but may be artificial.
- Field: in the real world (a test of a new fertiliser on a real farm). More realistic, but harder to control other variables.
- Natural: the researcher does not control the explanatory variable; it changes naturally (comparing health before and after a town's factory closes). Useful when it would be unethical or impossible to change things deliberately, but many other variables change too.

Simulation: a model that imitates a real situation, often using random numbers (for example, a random number from 1 to 10 where 1 to 3 means "the bus is late" to model a 30% chance). Useful when the real thing is too slow, costly or dangerous.

Questionnaires: cheap for large groups, but there is a risk of non-response and misunderstood questions.

Observation: recording what is seen (counting cars that pass). Accurate for behaviour, but slow, and people may act differently if they know they are watched.

Reference sources: books, websites, official statistics. Quick, but secondary.

A data collection sheet is a table with tally and frequency columns, or columns for each variable, designed so that every result fits exactly one place.

#### Worked example

Design a data collection sheet to record the number of passengers in cars passing a school between 8 am and 9 am.

| Number of people in car | Tally | Frequency |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | | |
| 2 | | |
| 3 | | |
| 4 | | |
| 5 or more | | |

The "5 or more" row means every car fits somewhere.

Simulation: a shop finds 20% of customers pay in cash. Describe how to simulate 10 customers with random digits.

1. Let digits 0 and 1 mean "cash" (2 out of 10 = 20%), and 2 to 9 mean "card".
2. Take 10 random digits; count the 0s and 1s.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting a final open class ("5 or more"), so some results have nowhere to go.
- Mixing up field and natural experiments: in a field experiment the researcher still controls the explanatory variable.

### 3.2.6 / 1d.02 Reliability and validity

*Refs: AQA 3.2.6 (B2c) · Edexcel 1d.02. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Reliability: getting similar results when the measurement or survey is repeated.
- Validity: measuring what you intended to measure.

#### Explained

A scale that gives 61 kg, 61 kg and 61 kg for the same person is reliable. If the scale is set wrong and the person is really 64 kg, the results are reliable but not valid.

Asking students "How many hours did you revise last week?" may not be valid for "how hard do they work", because people overestimate, and revision time is not the same as effort.

Ways to improve:

- Reliability: larger samples, repeat measurements, standard instructions, consistent equipment.
- Validity: ask about exactly what the hypothesis needs, measure directly rather than by memory, define terms clearly.

#### Worked example

A researcher measures fitness by asking people how often they go to the gym. Comment on validity.

1. Gym visits are not the same as fitness: some people exercise elsewhere, and visiting a gym does not show how fit someone is.
2. A more valid measure would be a direct fitness test, such as recovery heart rate after a set exercise.

#### Common mistakes

- Using "reliable" and "valid" as if they mean the same.

### 3.2.7 / 1d.03 Bias, sensitivity and distortion

*Refs: AQA 3.2.7 (B2d) · Edexcel 1d.03. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- Bias is anything that makes results systematically differ from the truth.
- Sources: non-random samples, leading questions, people giving answers they think are acceptable, sensitive topics, interviewer influence, non-response.
- Sensitive questions (income, health, illegal behaviour) may get false answers or no answer. Reduce this with anonymous questionnaires, sealed answers or grouped answer options.
- Know how to minimise distortion of data.
- (HT only) Level of control: in an experiment, keeping other conditions fixed reduces distortion.
- (HT only, Edexcel) The random response technique for sensitive questions.

#### Explained

(HT only) Random response technique: each person privately does something random, such as tossing a coin. If heads, they answer the sensitive question truthfully; if tails, they just answer "yes". Nobody knows which, so people are more honest. The researcher knows that about half the people answered "yes" because of the coin, and works back to an estimate.

#### Worked example

(HT only) 200 people are asked "Have you ever cheated in a test?" Each tosses a coin: heads, answer truthfully; tails, say "yes". There are 130 "yes" answers. Estimate the proportion who have cheated.

1. Expected tails: 100 people, all saying "yes".
2. The other 30 "yes" answers came from the 100 who got heads.
3. Estimated proportion who have cheated: 30 ÷ 100 = 0.3, or 30%.

#### Common mistakes

- In the random response method, dividing by all 200 instead of the 100 who answered honestly.

### 3.2.14 / 1d.04 Planning data collection: questions and interviews

*Refs: AQA 3.2.14 (B4) · Edexcel 1d.04. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Avoid leading questions (that push towards an answer) and biased sources.
- Think about time factors: when and how long the collection takes, and questions about time periods.
- Open questions let people answer freely; closed questions give fixed options.
- Interview types: face-to-face, telephone, online; structured (fixed questions) or unstructured.
- Pilot survey: a small trial of a questionnaire. Pre-test: a trial run of an experiment.
- Design suitable questions and response boxes.

#### Explained

Good closed questions have a time frame, clear wording and response boxes that do not overlap and cover every possible answer.

Poor: "How much TV do you watch? A little / Some / A lot"
Better: "On a typical school day, how many hours of TV do you watch? 0 to less than 1 / 1 to less than 2 / 2 to less than 3 / 3 or more"

| | Open questions | Closed questions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Good | Detailed answers; nothing missed | Quick to answer; easy to analyse |
| Bad | Hard to analyse; slow | Limit answers; options may not fit |

| Interview | Good | Bad |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Face-to-face | High response; can explain questions | Costly; interviewer may influence answers; less honest on sensitive questions |
| Telephone | Cheaper; quick | Many refuse; no visual aids |
| Online / postal | Cheap; anonymous | Low response; questions cannot be explained |

#### Worked example

Criticise this question and write a better one: "Don't you agree that the canteen food is too expensive? Yes / No"

1. It is leading ("Don't you agree") and only allows yes or no.
2. Better: "How would you rate the prices in the canteen? Very cheap / Cheap / About right / Expensive / Very expensive"

#### Common mistakes

- Response boxes that overlap (0–1, 1–2) or leave gaps.
- No time frame ("How often do you exercise?").

### 3.2.15 / 1d.05 Problems with collected data

*Refs: AQA 3.2.15 (B5a) · Edexcel 1d.05. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Problems include missing data, incorrect formats, non-responses and incomplete responses.
- Know ways to deal with them.

#### Explained

- Missing values: leave the item out of calculations that need that value (and say so), or go back and collect it. Do not just treat it as zero.
- Incorrect formats: "five", "5 hrs" and "5" mean the same; convert them to one format. Dates written in different ways need standardising.
- Non-response: follow up, or take a bigger initial sample, and consider whether non-responders differ.
- Incomplete responses: use the answered questions but report the different totals for each question.

#### Worked example

In a survey of heights, some entries are "1.62", "158", "5 ft 4" and blank. What should be done?

1. Convert all to the same unit, such as cm: 162, 158, about 163.
2. Leave the blank out of calculations (so n is one smaller) and record that it was missing.

#### Common mistakes

- Replacing a missing value with 0, which drags the mean down.

### 3.2.16 / 1d.06 Cleaning data

*Refs: AQA 3.2.16 (B5b) · Edexcel 1d.06. Tier: F & H.*

#### What you need to know

- Data may need cleaning before processing: removing or correcting errors, impossible values, extra symbols (£, %, units) that stop software treating values as numbers, and duplicate entries.
- Spot errors using totals (rows and columns that should add up) and by checking values are consistent with each other.
- Outliers may be genuine or errors; check before removing (see file 08).

#### Explained

On a spreadsheet, a cell containing "£12" or "12 kg" is often treated as text, so it is left out of a SUM or AVERAGE without any warning. Strip the symbols and keep units in the column heading instead.

#### Worked example

A table shows Year 10 students' choices.

| | Art | Music | Drama | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Boys | 18 | 12 | 9 | 39 |
| Girls | 21 | 17 | 14 | 50 |
| Total | 39 | 29 | 23 | 89 |

Find the error.

1. Boys row: 18 + 12 + 9 = 39 ✓.
2. Girls row: 21 + 17 + 14 = 52, not 50 ✗.
3. Columns: Art 18 + 21 = 39 ✓, Music 12 + 17 = 29 ✓, Drama 9 + 14 = 23 ✓, and 39 + 29 + 23 = 91.
4. So the girls' total should be 52 and the grand total 91. The 89 was found by adding the wrong girls' total, 39 + 50, which is why it looked consistent down the Total column.

#### Common mistakes

- Only checking one direction (rows) and not columns.

### 3.2.17 / 1d.07 Extraneous variables and control groups

*Refs: AQA 3.2.17 (B5c) · Edexcel 1d.07. Tier: F & H (part Higher only).*

#### What you need to know

- An extraneous variable is any variable other than the explanatory variable that might affect the response. Identify and control it (keep it the same) so it does not distort results.
- (HT only) A control group gets no treatment (or the usual one) and is compared with the treatment group.
- (HT only, Edexcel) Matched pairs: each person in the treatment group is paired with a similar person (same age, gender, fitness) in the control group.

#### Explained

Testing whether a new fertiliser increases yield: extraneous variables include the amount of water, sunlight, soil type and plant variety. Keep these the same for every plant.

(HT only) Without a control group you cannot tell whether a change was due to the treatment or would have happened anyway. Matched pairs make the two groups as alike as possible, so differences are more likely due to the treatment.

#### Worked example

(HT only) A school tests whether a revision app improves test scores. Describe how to use a control group and matched pairs.

1. Choose students and split them into two groups. One group uses the app (treatment), the other revises as usual (control).
2. Matched pairs: pair students with similar previous test scores; one of each pair goes into each group (chosen at random).
3. Compare the improvement in scores between the groups; keep the test, time allowed and timing the same for both.

#### Common mistakes

- Naming an extraneous variable without saying how to control it.

### Check questions

1. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of a laboratory experiment compared with a field experiment. [2 marks]
2. Explain the difference between reliability and validity. [2 marks]
3. Write down two things wrong with this question: "How much do you spend on lunch? £0–£2 / £2–£4 / £4–£6" [2 marks]
4. Give one advantage of an online questionnaire and one disadvantage. [2 marks]
5. In a data set of ages of club members, one entry is "2O" (with the letter O) and one is 340. Explain how each should be dealt with. [2 marks]
6. (HT only) 400 people take part in a random response survey. Each rolls a fair dice: on a 6 they answer "yes"; otherwise they answer truthfully. There are 140 "yes" answers. Estimate the percentage of people for whom the true answer is "yes". [3 marks]
7. (HT only) Explain why a control group is used in an experiment to test a new medicine. [1 mark]
8. Describe how to use random numbers to simulate whether it rains on each day of a week, where the chance of rain on any day is 0.4. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Advantage: easier to control other variables / easier to repeat (1). Disadvantage: artificial, may not reflect real behaviour (1).
2. Reliability: repeat measurements give similar results (1). Validity: measures what it is meant to measure (1).
3. Overlapping classes (£2 is in two groups) (1); no option above £6 / no time frame (per day? per week?) (1).
4. Advantage: cheap / quick / anonymous / reaches many people (1). Disadvantage: low response rate / only people online / cannot explain questions (1).
5. "2O" is a typing error: change it to 20 if that is clearly meant (1). 340 is impossible: check the original record, or remove it from calculations (1).
6. Expected "yes" from rolling a 6: 400 × 1/6 ≈ 66.7 (1). Truthful answers: 400 − 66.7 = 333.3 people, of whom 140 − 66.7 = 73.3 said yes (1). 73.3 ÷ 333.3 ≈ 22% (1).
7. To compare with the treatment group, so any difference can be put down to the medicine and not to other factors (1).
8. Let digits 0, 1, 2, 3 mean rain and 4 to 9 mean no rain (1); generate 7 random digits, one for each day (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/30-discrete-random-variables.md -->

## Discrete random variables

This file covers discrete random variables given by tables or formulae, probabilities, expectation, variance, mode and median, the expectation of functions of a random variable, linear coding, the discrete uniform distribution (with proofs of its mean and variance), the mean and variance of the binomial distribution, and probabilities found by counting arrangements.

### Overview

Probability and the binomial distribution are in A-level Maths ([../maths/21-probability.md](../maths/21-probability.md), [../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md](../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md)). Sums of independent random variables are in [36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md](36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md); continuous variables in [35-continuous-random-variables.md](35-continuous-random-variables.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SA1 to SA7; Edexcel FS1 1.1; OCR 5.01a, 5.01b, 5.02a to 5.02e; MEI SR1 to SR10.
- AQA SA7 requires the proofs of the mean and variance of the discrete uniform distribution. AQA SA3 includes mode and median.
- OCR 5.01 includes probability by counting (permutations and combinations); the counting methods are in [59-combinatorics.md](59-combinatorics.md). OCR 5.02d and MEI SR9 ask for the binomial mean np and variance np(1 − p).

### SA1 Probability distributions

*Refs: AQA SA1, SA2 · Edexcel FS1 1.1 · OCR 5.01a, 5.01b, 5.02a · MEI SR1. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A discrete random variable X takes values x₁, x₂, ... with probabilities pᵢ = P(X = xᵢ), each between 0 and 1, with Σpᵢ = 1.
- The distribution may be given as a table or a formula (for example P(X = x) = kx for x = 1, 2, 3, 4).
- The cumulative distribution function F(x) = P(X ≤ x).

#### Worked example

P(X = x) = kx for x = 1, 2, 3, 4. Find k and P(X ≥ 3).

1. k(1 + 2 + 3 + 4) = 1, k = 1/10.
2. P(X ≥ 3) = 0.3 + 0.4 = 0.7.

(OCR) Three letters are chosen at random from the word ORANGE without replacement. X is the number of vowels chosen. Find the distribution of X.

1. Vowels O, A, E (3); consonants R, N, G (3). C(6, 3) = 20 selections.
2. P(X = 0) = 1/20, P(X = 1) = 3 × 3/20 = 9/20, P(X = 2) = 9/20, P(X = 3) = 1/20.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to check that probabilities sum to 1.

### SA3 Expectation and variance

*Refs: AQA SA3, SA4, SA5 · Edexcel FS1 1.1 · OCR 5.02b, 5.02c · MEI SR2, SR3, SR4, SR5, SR6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
E(X) = \mu = \sum x_ip_i,\qquad E(X^2) = \sum x_i^2p_i,\qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = E(X^2) - [E(X)]^2
```

- E(g(X)) = Σg(xᵢ)pᵢ.
- E(aX + b) = aE(X) + b; Var(aX + b) = a²Var(X).
- Standard deviation = √Var(X). Mode: the most likely value. Median m: the smallest value with F(m) ≥ ½.

#### Explained

E(X) is the long-run average of many observations. Variance measures spread. Adding b shifts every value, which changes the mean but not the spread; multiplying by a scales the spread by |a|, so the variance by a².

#### Worked example

| x | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| P(X = x) | 0.1 | 0.3 | 0.4 | 0.2 |

Find E(X), Var(X), E(1/(X + 1)) and Var(3X − 2).

1. E(X) = 0 + 0.3 + 0.8 + 0.6 = 1.7.
2. E(X²) = 0 + 0.3 + 1.6 + 1.8 = 3.7. Var(X) = 3.7 − 2.89 = 0.81.
3. E(1/(X + 1)) = 0.1 + 0.3/2 + 0.4/3 + 0.2/4 = 13/30.
4. Var(3X − 2) = 9 × 0.81 = 7.29.
5. Mode 2. F(1) = 0.4, F(2) = 0.8, so the median is 2.

#### Common mistakes

- Var(3X − 2) = 3Var(X) − 2.
- E(1/X) = 1/E(X).

### SA6 The discrete uniform distribution

*Refs: AQA SA6, SA7 · OCR 5.02e · MEI SR7, SR8, SR9. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- X is uniform on {1, 2, ..., n} if P(X = r) = 1/n for each r. Use it when every outcome is equally likely (a fair die, a random digit).

```latex
E(X) = \frac{n+1}{2},\qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac{n^2-1}{12}
```

#### Explained

Proof (AQA SA7):

```latex
E(X) = \frac1n\sum_{r=1}^n r = \frac1n\cdot\frac{n(n+1)}{2} = \frac{n+1}{2}
```

```latex
E(X^2) = \frac1n\cdot\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6} = \frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}
```

```latex
\mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{6} - \frac{(n+1)^2}{4} = \frac{(n+1)(4n+2-3n-3)}{12} = \frac{n^2-1}{12}
```

#### Worked example

A spinner shows 1 to 10 equally likely. Find the mean and variance of the score.

1. Mean 11/2 = 5.5. Variance 99/12 = 8.25.

Y is uniform on {2, 4, 6, ..., 20}. Find E(Y) and Var(Y).

1. Y = 2X with X uniform on 1 to 10. E(Y) = 11, Var(Y) = 4 × 8.25 = 33.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the formula for values that do not start at 1 without coding.

### Mean and variance of the binomial distribution

*Refs: OCR 5.02d · MEI SR10. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- If X ~ B(n, p), E(X) = np and Var(X) = np(1 − p).

#### Worked example

X ~ B(20, 0.3). Find E(X), Var(X) and P(X > E(X)).

1. E(X) = 6, Var(X) = 4.2.
2. P(X > 6) = 1 − P(X ≤ 6) = 1 − 0.6080 = 0.3920.

### Check questions

1. P(X = x) = c(x² + 1) for x = 0, 1, 2, 3. Find c, E(X) and Var(X). [6 marks]
2. X has E(X) = 4 and Var(X) = 2. Find E(5 − 2X), Var(5 − 2X) and E(X²). [4 marks]
3. A fair 8-sided die is rolled. Find the mean and standard deviation of the score, and the probability that the score is within one standard deviation of the mean. [5 marks]
4. Prove that the variance of the discrete uniform distribution on {1, ..., n} is (n² − 1)/12. [4 marks]
5. Two cards are drawn without replacement from five cards numbered 1 to 5. X is the larger number. Find the distribution of X, its mode, median and mean. [6 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. c(1 + 2 + 5 + 10) = 1, c = 1/18 (1). Probabilities 1/18, 2/18, 5/18, 10/18 (1). E(X) = (0 + 2 + 10 + 30)/18 = 7/3 (1). E(X²) = (0 + 2 + 20 + 90)/18 = 56/9 (1). Var = 56/9 − 49/9 = 7/9 (2).
2. 5 − 8 = −3 (1). 4 × 2 = 8 (1). E(X²) = 2 + 16 = 18 (2).
3. Mean 4.5 (1). Variance 63/12 = 5.25, s.d. 2.291 (2). Within 2.209 to 6.791: scores 3, 4, 5, 6 (1), probability ½ (1).
4. E(X) and E(X²) from standard sums (2). Subtract and simplify (2).
5. 10 equally likely pairs (1). P(X = 2) = 1/10, P(X = 3) = 2/10, P(X = 4) = 3/10, P(X = 5) = 4/10 (2). Mode 5 (1). F(4) = 0.6 ≥ ½, F(3) = 0.3, so median 4 (1). Mean (2 + 6 + 12 + 20)/10 = 4 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/22-statistical-distributions.md -->

## Statistical distributions: binomial and Normal

This file covers discrete probability distributions, the binomial distribution as a model, the Normal distribution as a model, standardising and inverse Normal problems, the Normal approximation to the binomial, and choosing a suitable distribution.

### Overview

AS: simple discrete distributions (including the discrete uniform distribution) and the binomial distribution. A-level only: the Normal distribution and its links to histograms, the binomial and points of inflection, and selecting between models. Use your calculator's binomial and Normal functions throughout.

#### Board differences

- AQA N1 to N3; Edexcel Statistics 4.1 to 4.3.
- OCR H240: 2.04a to 2.04c (AS); 2.04d (mean np) and 2.04e to 2.04h (A-level only). OCR expects the facts that about two-thirds of values lie within one standard deviation of the mean, about 95% within two, and almost all within three; the equation of the Normal curve is excluded.
- OCR MEI H640: MR1 to MR7 (AS, including mean = np and expected frequencies); MR8 to MR13 (A-level only). MEI includes the continuity correction and recognising from its shape when a binomial can be approximated by a Normal, but excludes knowing formal conditions for that approximation.
- AQA's N1 excludes calculating the mean and variance of general discrete random variables.
- Edexcel 4.1 names the discrete uniform distribution; Edexcel 4.2 includes approximating B(n, p) by N(np, np(1 − p)) with a continuity correction.

### N1 Discrete distributions and the binomial distribution
