*Refs: AQA N1 · Edexcel Statistics 4.1 · OCR 2.04a, 2.04b, 2.04c, 2.04d · MEI MR1, MR2, MR3, MR4, MR5, MR6, MR7. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A discrete random variable X takes separate values, each with a probability; the probabilities add to 1. It can be given as a table or a formula (probability function).
- The discrete uniform distribution: n equally likely values.
- The binomial distribution X ~ B(n, p) models the number of successes in n trials when:
  - there is a fixed number n of trials;
  - each trial has two outcomes (success or failure);
  - the probability of success p is the same for every trial;
  - the trials are independent.

```latex
P(X = r) = \binom{n}{r} p^r (1-p)^{n-r}, \quad r = 0, 1, \dots, n
```

- Find P(X = r), P(X ≤ r) and other cumulative probabilities with a calculator.
- (OCR: A-level only; MEI: AS) The mean of B(n, p) is np. Expected frequencies are n × probability.

#### Explained

**Cumulative probabilities.** Calculators give P(X ≤ r). Convert other forms:

| Want | Use |
| --- | --- |
| P(X < r) | P(X ≤ r − 1) |
| P(X ≥ r) | 1 − P(X ≤ r − 1) |
| P(X > r) | 1 − P(X ≤ r) |
| P(a ≤ X ≤ b) | P(X ≤ b) − P(X ≤ a − 1) |

**Criticising a binomial model.** Ask whether each condition really holds. Selecting people from a small group without replacement changes p; seeds planted in the same tray may not grow independently.

#### Worked example

X takes values 1, 2, 3, 4 with P(X = x) = kx. Find k and P(X ≥ 3).

1. k(1 + 2 + 3 + 4) = 1, so k = 1/10.
2. P(X ≥ 3) = 3/10 + 4/10 = 0.7.

X ~ B(12, 0.3). Find P(X = 4), P(X ≤ 4), P(X > 5) and P(2 ≤ X ≤ 6).

1. P(X = 4) = ¹²C₄ × 0.3⁴ × 0.7⁸ = 0.2311.
2. P(X ≤ 4) = 0.7237.
3. P(X > 5) = 1 − P(X ≤ 5) = 1 − 0.8822 = 0.1178.
4. P(2 ≤ X ≤ 6) = P(X ≤ 6) − P(X ≤ 1) = 0.9614 − 0.0850 = 0.8764.

15% of light bulbs from a factory are faulty. A box holds 20 bulbs. State a suitable model, find the probability that exactly 3 are faulty and that at least 5 are faulty, and state the expected number of faulty bulbs.

1. X ~ B(20, 0.15), assuming bulbs are faulty independently with the same probability.
2. P(X = 3) = 0.2428.
3. P(X ≥ 5) = 1 − P(X ≤ 4) = 0.1702.
4. Expected number: 20 × 0.15 = 3.

#### Common mistakes

- Using P(X ≤ 5) for P(X < 5).
- Using the binomial when trials are not independent or p changes.

### N2 The Normal distribution

*Refs: AQA N2 · Edexcel Statistics 4.2 · OCR 2.04e, 2.04f, 2.04g · MEI MR8, MR9, MR10, MR11, MR12. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) X ~ N(μ, σ²) is a continuous distribution: bell-shaped, symmetrical about μ, with points of inflection at μ ± σ. Probability is area under the curve, and the total area is 1.
- (A-level only) About 68% of values lie within μ ± σ, 95% within μ ± 2σ and 99.7% within μ ± 3σ.
- (A-level only) Standardise with

```latex
Z = \frac{X - \mu}{\sigma}, \qquad Z \sim N(0, 1)
```

- (A-level only) Find probabilities, and use the inverse Normal to find values or unknown μ and σ.
- (A-level only) Link to histograms of real data, and approximate a binomial B(n, p) by N(np, np(1 − p)) when n is large and p is near 0.5, using a continuity correction.

#### Explained

For a continuous distribution, P(X = a) = 0, so P(X < a) and P(X ≤ a) are the same.

**Finding μ or σ.** Write each piece of information as (x − μ)/σ = z, using the inverse Normal on the standard Normal to find z. One unknown needs one equation; two unknowns need two simultaneous equations.

**Normal approximation.** A binomial with large n has a roughly bell-shaped histogram. Each integer value r is represented by the interval r − 0.5 to r + 0.5 under the Normal curve (the continuity correction). For example, P(X ≤ 35) ≈ P(Y < 35.5) and P(X ≥ 45) ≈ P(Y > 44.5).

**Is a Normal model sensible?** Check the data are roughly symmetrical and bell-shaped, that about 95% lie within two standard deviations, and that the model does not give large probabilities to impossible values (such as negative lengths).

#### Worked example

(A-level only) X ~ N(50, 16). Find P(X < 56), P(45 < X < 55) and the value a with P(X > a) = 0.1.

1. σ = 4. P(X < 56) = P(Z < 1.5) = 0.9332.
2. P(45 < X < 55) = P(−1.25 < Z < 1.25) = 0.7887.
3. P(X > a) = 0.1, so (a − 50)/4 = 1.2816, a = 55.13.

(A-level only) X is Normal with P(X < 30) = 0.2 and P(X > 50) = 0.1. Find μ and σ.

1. (30 − μ)/σ = −0.8416 and (50 − μ)/σ = 1.2816.
2. Subtract: 20/σ = 2.1232, so σ = 9.42.
3. μ = 30 + 0.8416 × 9.42 = 37.9.

(A-level only) X ~ B(100, 0.4). Use a Normal approximation to estimate P(X ≤ 35).

1. np = 40, np(1 − p) = 24. Y ~ N(40, 24).
2. P(X ≤ 35) ≈ P(Y < 35.5) = P(Z < −0.9186) = 0.1792.
3. (The exact binomial value is 0.1795, so the approximation is very good.)

(A-level only) The lengths of 200 leaves have a symmetrical, bell-shaped histogram with mean 8.2 cm and standard deviation 1.1 cm. Explain why N(8.2, 1.1²) is a suitable model, and use the histogram to find the points of inflection.

1. The shape is symmetrical and bell-shaped, matching a Normal curve.
2. Points of inflection at 8.2 ± 1.1, that is 7.1 cm and 9.3 cm.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Using σ² instead of σ when standardising: N(50, 16) has σ = 4.
- (A-level only) Getting the sign of z wrong for values below the mean.
- (A-level only) Leaving out the continuity correction in a Normal approximation.

### N3 Choosing a distribution

*Refs: AQA N3 · Edexcel Statistics 4.3 · OCR 2.04h · MEI MR13. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Choose a suitable distribution for a context, with reasons, including recognising when a binomial model is not appropriate.
- (A-level only) Recognise when a Normal model is not appropriate, and choose between binomial and Normal models.

#### Explained

| Situation | Model |
| --- | --- |
| a count of successes in a fixed number of independent trials, constant p | binomial |
| a continuous measurement, symmetrical and bell-shaped | Normal |
| several equally likely discrete outcomes | discrete uniform |

A Normal model is not appropriate for strongly skewed data (such as incomes or waiting times), or where it would give a large probability to impossible values. A binomial model is not appropriate when the number of trials is not fixed, or the trials affect each other.

#### Worked example

Suggest a model for each, with a reason: (a) the number of sixes in 30 rolls of a fair die; (b) the heights of adult women in the UK; (c) the number of customers arriving at a shop in an hour.

1. (a) B(30, 1/6): fixed number of independent trials, constant probability.
2. (b) (A-level only) Normal: a continuous measurement that is roughly symmetrical about a mean.
3. (c) Not binomial: there is no fixed number of trials. (A different model, met in Further Mathematics, is used.)

(A-level only) Waiting times at a clinic have mean 12 minutes and standard deviation 9 minutes. Explain why a Normal model is unsuitable.

1. Under N(12, 9²), P(T < 0) = P(Z < −1.33) ≈ 0.09, a large probability for an impossible negative time. Waiting times are also likely to be positively skewed.

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing a model without giving a reason linked to the context.

### Check questions

1. P(X = x) = k(x + 1) for x = 0, 1, 2, 3. Find k and P(X ≥ 2). [3 marks]
2. X ~ B(20, 0.15). Find (a) P(X = 3); (b) P(X ≤ 2); (c) P(X ≥ 5). [4 marks]
3. A student says the number of girls in a family of 4 children follows B(4, 0.5). Give one reason why this model may not be suitable. [1 mark]
4. (A-level only) X ~ N(120, 25). Find P(X > 128) and P(110 < X < 125). [4 marks]
5. (A-level only) The masses of bags of flour are Normal with mean 500 g. 5% of bags weigh less than 490 g. Find the standard deviation. [3 marks]
6. (A-level only) X is Normal with P(X < 20) = 0.3 and P(X > 35) = 0.15. Find μ and σ. [5 marks]
7. (A-level only) X ~ B(80, 0.5). Use a suitable approximation to estimate P(X ≥ 45), stating the distribution you use. [4 marks]
8. (A-level only) Give two features of a histogram of data that would suggest a Normal model is suitable. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 10k = 1, k = 0.1 (1); P(X = 2) + P(X = 3) = 0.3 + 0.4 (1); = 0.7 (1).
2. (a) 0.2428 (1). (b) 0.4049 (1). (c) 1 − P(X ≤ 4) (1) = 0.1702 (1).
3. e.g. the sexes of children in one family may not be independent, or the probability of a girl is not exactly 0.5 (1).
4. σ = 5 (1); P(Z > 1.6) = 0.0548 (1); P(−2 < Z < 1) (1) = 0.8186 (1).
5. (490 − 500)/σ = −1.6449 (2); σ = 6.08 g (1).
6. (20 − μ)/σ = −0.5244 (1); (35 − μ)/σ = 1.0364 (1); 15 = 1.5608σ (1); σ = 9.61 (1); μ = 25.0 (1).
7. N(40, 20) (1); continuity correction: P(Y > 44.5) (1); P(Z > 1.006) (1) = 0.157 (1).
8. Symmetrical (1); bell-shaped / single peak, with about 95% of values within 2 standard deviations of the mean (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/35-continuous-random-variables.md -->

## Continuous random variables, rectangular and exponential distributions

This file covers probability density functions, probabilities as areas, cumulative distribution functions, the median, quartiles and mode, the mean and variance (and E(g(X))), linear functions of a continuous variable, the distribution of a function of X, the continuous uniform (rectangular) distribution and the exponential distribution.

### Overview

Integration is from A-level Maths ([../maths/14-integration-basics-and-areas.md](../maths/14-integration-basics-and-areas.md)). Discrete variables are in [30-discrete-random-variables.md](30-discrete-random-variables.md); sums of variables in [36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md](36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SD1 to SD7 (including the rectangular distribution) and SF1 to SF4 (the exponential distribution, with proofs of its mean and variance and its link to the Poisson). Only AQA includes the exponential.
- Edexcel FS2 2.1 to 2.4 (including the continuous uniform distribution with derivations of its mean, variance and cdf).
- OCR 5.03a to 5.03g; 5.03g is finding the cdf of a function of X (such as Y = X²).
- MEI SR19 to SR27.

### SD1 Probability density functions

*Refs: AQA SD1, SD2 · Edexcel FS2 2.1, 2.2 · OCR 5.03a, 5.03b · MEI SR19, SR20, SR21. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A continuous random variable X has a pdf f(x) ≥ 0 with ∫f(x) dx = 1 over all x.
- P(a < X < b) = ∫ₐᵇ f(x) dx. P(X = a) = 0, so < and ≤ give the same probability.
- A pdf can be defined piecewise.

#### Explained

Probability is area under the pdf, not the height. f(x) can be greater than 1, as long as the total area is 1.

#### Worked example

f(x) = kx² for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2, and 0 otherwise. Find k and P(X > 1).

1. k[x³/3]₀² = 8k/3 = 1, k = 3/8.
2. P(X > 1) = (3/8)[x³/3]₁² = (8 − 1)/8 = 7/8.

#### Common mistakes

- Reading f(a) as P(X = a).

### SD6 Cumulative distribution functions, median and quartiles

*Refs: AQA SD3, SD6 · Edexcel FS2 2.2 · OCR 5.03e, 5.03f, 5.03g · MEI SR23, SR24, SR25, SR26. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
F(x) = P(X \le x) = \int_{-\infty}^{x} f(t)\,dt,\qquad f(x) = F'(x)
```

- F is 0 below the range and 1 above it, and never decreases.
- Median m: F(m) = 0.5. Quartiles: F(q₁) = 0.25, F(q₃) = 0.75. Percentiles similarly.
- Mode: the value of x where f(x) is greatest (use calculus or a sketch).
- (OCR 5.03g) For Y = g(X) with g increasing, G(y) = P(Y ≤ y) = P(X ≤ g⁻¹(y)) = F(g⁻¹(y)). Differentiate for the pdf of Y.

#### Explained

For a piecewise pdf, F for each later piece must include the full area of earlier pieces, so that F is continuous.

#### Worked example

For f(x) = (3/8)x² on [0, 2], find F(x), the median and the mode.

1. F(x) = x³/8 for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2 (0 below, 1 above).
2. m³/8 = ½, m = ∛4 = 1.587.
3. f is increasing, so the mode is 2.

A piecewise pdf: f(x) = x for 0 ≤ x ≤ 1, f(x) = ½ for 1 < x ≤ 2. Find F(x) and the median.

1. Check area: ½ + ½ = 1 ✓.
2. 0 ≤ x ≤ 1: F = x²/2. 1 < x ≤ 2: F = ½ + (x − 1)/2 = x/2.
3. F(1) = ½, so the median is 1.

(OCR) For f(x) = (3/8)x² on [0, 2], find the pdf of Y = X².

1. G(y) = P(X ≤ √y) = (√y)³/8 = y^{3/2}/8 for 0 ≤ y ≤ 4.
2. g(y) = (3/16)y^{1/2}.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting the earlier pieces' area when building F.

### SD4 Mean and variance

*Refs: AQA SD4, SD5 · Edexcel FS2 2.3 · OCR 5.03c, 5.03d · MEI SR22, SR27. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
E(X) = \int x f(x)\,dx,\quad E(X^2) = \int x^2 f(x)\,dx,\quad \mathrm{Var}(X) = E(X^2) - [E(X)]^2
```

- E(g(X)) = ∫g(x)f(x) dx. E(aX + b) = aE(X) + b, Var(aX + b) = a²Var(X).

#### Worked example

For f(x) = (3/8)x² on [0, 2], find E(X), Var(X) and E(1/X).

1. E(X) = (3/8)∫₀² x³ dx = (3/8)(4) = 1.5.
2. E(X²) = (3/8)(32/5) = 2.4. Var(X) = 2.4 − 2.25 = 0.15.
3. E(1/X) = (3/8)∫₀² x dx = (3/8)(2) = 0.75.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to multiply by x (or x²) before integrating.

### SD7 The rectangular (continuous uniform) distribution

*Refs: AQA SD7 · Edexcel FS2 2.4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- X ~ U[a, b]: f(x) = 1/(b − a) for a ≤ x ≤ b.
- E(X) = (a + b)/2, Var(X) = (b − a)²/12, F(x) = (x − a)/(b − a).
- Use it for rounding errors, or a random point on an interval.

#### Worked example

A length is rounded to the nearest cm. Find the mean and standard deviation of the rounding error.

1. Error ~ U[−0.5, 0.5]. Mean 0, variance 1/12, s.d. 0.289 cm.

X ~ U[2, 8]. Find P(X > 6.5) and Var(X).

1. P = 1.5/6 = 0.25. Var = 36/12 = 3.

### SF1 The exponential distribution

*Refs: AQA SF1, SF2, SF3, SF4. Level: A-level only (AQA option).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
f(x) = \lambda e^{-\lambda x}\ (x \ge 0),\qquad F(x) = 1 - e^{-\lambda x},\qquad E(X) = \frac1\lambda,\qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac1{\lambda^2}
```

- If events occur as a Poisson process with rate λ, the time between events (and the time to the first event) is exponential with parameter λ.
- The exponential is memoryless: P(X > s + t | X > s) = P(X > t).

#### Explained

Proofs: E(X) = ∫₀^∞ λxe^{−λx} dx; integrating by parts gives [−xe^{−λx}]₀^∞ + ∫e^{−λx} dx = 1/λ. Similarly E(X²) = 2/λ², so Var = 1/λ². The link with Poisson: the time T to the first event exceeds t exactly when there are no events in [0, t], so P(T > t) = e^{−λt}.

#### Worked example

Buses arrive as a Poisson process with rate 0.5 per minute. Find the probability of waiting more than 3 minutes, and the median wait.

1. T ~ Exp(0.5). P(T > 3) = e^{−1.5} = 0.223.
2. 1 − e^{−0.5m} = ½, m = 2 ln 2 = 1.39 minutes.

#### Common mistakes

- Using 1/λ as the parameter in the pdf.

### Check questions

1. f(x) = k(4 − x²) for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2. Find k, E(X) and P(X < 1). [6 marks]
2. X has cdf F(x) = (x² − 1)/8 for 1 ≤ x ≤ 3. Find the pdf, the median and the interquartile range. [6 marks]
3. f(x) = ¼(4x − x³) for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2. Find the mode. [3 marks]
4. X ~ U[0, 10]. Find E(X²) and P(|X − 5| < 2). [3 marks]
5. (AQA) The lifetime of a component is exponential with mean 200 hours. Find the probability it lasts more than 300 hours, and, given it has lasted 100 hours, the probability it lasts a further 300 hours. [4 marks]
6. (OCR) X ~ U[0, 1]. Find the pdf of Y = √X. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. k[4x − x³/3]₀² = 16k/3 = 1, k = 3/16 (2). E(X) = (3/16)∫(4x − x³) dx = (3/16)(8 − 4) = 3/4 (2). P(X < 1) = (3/16)(4 − ⅓) = 11/16 (2).
2. f(x) = x/4 on [1, 3] (1). (m² − 1)/8 = ½, m = √5 = 2.236 (2). q₁ = √3, q₃ = √7 (2). IQR = 0.914 (1).
3. f′ = ¼(4 − 3x²) = 0 (1), x = 2/√3 = 1.155 (1), a maximum since f″ < 0 (1).
4. E(X²) = 100/3 (or Var + mean² = 25/3 + 25) (2). P(3 < X < 7) = 0.4 (1).
5. λ = 1/200. P(T > 300) = e^{−1.5} = 0.223 (2). Memoryless: same, 0.223 (2).
6. G(y) = P(√X ≤ y) = P(X ≤ y²) = y² (2). g(y) = 2y for 0 ≤ y ≤ 1 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md -->

## Sampling, estimation and the central limit theorem

This file covers why sample size and random sampling matter, unbiased estimates of the population mean and variance, the distribution of the sample mean and its standard error, the central limit theorem, normal models and normal probability plots, and simulation.

### Overview

Sampling methods are in A-level Maths ([../maths/19-statistical-sampling.md](../maths/19-statistical-sampling.md)). Linear combinations are in [36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md](36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md). Confidence intervals and tests built on these results are in [38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md](38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md).

#### Board differences

- Edexcel FS1 5.1 (central limit theorem), FS2 5.1 (unbiased estimates, standard error).
- OCR 5.05a, 5.05b (distribution of the sample mean; unbiased estimates).
- MEI Sx1 to Sx3 (sampling, AS), SR30 to SR32 (normal models, normal probability plots), SI1 to SI6 (estimation, standard error, central limit theorem), SZ1, SZ2 (simulation with spreadsheets).
- AQA uses these results inside its confidence interval work (SH) rather than listing them.

### Sampling

*Refs: MEI Sx1, Sx2, Sx3. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Larger samples give more reliable estimates (smaller standard error), but cost more.
- Sampling is needed when the population is large, testing is destructive, or a census is impractical.
- A random sample (each member chosen independently with equal chance) allows probability-based inference and avoids bias.

#### Common mistakes

- Thinking a large but biased sample is reliable.

### Unbiased estimates and standard error

*Refs: Edexcel FS2 5.1 · OCR 5.05b · MEI SI1, SI2, SI3, SI4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
\bar x = \frac{\sum x}{n},\qquad s^2 = \frac{1}{n-1}\left(\sum x^2 - \frac{(\sum x)^2}{n}\right)
```

- x̄ is an unbiased estimate of μ, and s² (dividing by n − 1) is an unbiased estimate of σ².
- The sample mean X̄ is a random variable: E(X̄) = μ and Var(X̄) = σ²/n.
- The standard error of the mean is σ/√n, estimated by s/√n.

#### Explained

Dividing by n − 1 corrects for the fact that the data are closer to their own mean x̄ than to the true mean μ. "Unbiased" means the estimator is right on average over many samples.

#### Worked example

A sample of 10 gives Σx = 52 and Σx² = 290. Find unbiased estimates of μ and σ².

1. x̄ = 5.2.
2. s² = (290 − 52²/10)/9 = (290 − 270.4)/9 = 2.178.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing by n for the variance estimate.

### The central limit theorem

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 5.1 · OCR 5.05a · MEI SI5, SI6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- If X ~ N(μ, σ²), then X̄ ~ N(μ, σ²/n) exactly.
- Central limit theorem: for a large sample (n ≥ about 30) from any distribution with mean μ and variance σ², X̄ is approximately N(μ, σ²/n).

#### Explained

Averages smooth out individual oddities, so whatever the parent distribution's shape, the sample mean is close to normal for large n. The approximation is better when the parent is roughly symmetric.

#### Worked example

X has mean 4 and variance 9 (distribution unknown). A sample of 50 is taken. Estimate P(X̄ > 4.5).

1. By the CLT, X̄ ≈ N(4, 9/50).
2. P(X̄ > 4.5) = P(Z > 0.5/0.4243) = P(Z > 1.179) = 0.119.

X ~ Po(3). Estimate the probability that the mean of 40 observations is less than 2.7.

1. X̄ ≈ N(3, 3/40).
2. P(X̄ < 2.7) = Φ(−0.3/0.2739) = Φ(−1.095) = 0.137.

#### Common mistakes

- Using σ instead of σ/√n.
- Saying the CLT makes the data (rather than the mean) normal.

### Normal models and probability plots

*Refs: MEI SR30, SR31, SR32. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The normal distribution is a good model for many measured quantities (lengths, masses) affected by many small independent factors.
- A normal probability plot (data against expected normal scores) is close to a straight line if the data are normal; curvature shows skew.
- Use the normal distribution, when appropriate, to approximate other distributions.

### Simulation

*Refs: MEI SZ1, SZ2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Spreadsheets can generate random samples from given distributions (for example =NORM.INV(RAND(), μ, σ)).
- Simulation can estimate probabilities or check the behaviour of a statistic (for example, the distribution of X̄) when theory is hard.

#### Worked example

Describe a simulation to check the CLT for the score on a fair die with samples of 5.

1. Generate 5 random integers 1 to 6 in each of 1000 rows; find each row's mean.
2. Draw a histogram of the 1000 means: it should be roughly normal with mean 3.5 and variance (35/12)/5 = 0.583.

### Check questions

1. A sample of 12 gives Σx = 120 and Σx² = 1530. Find unbiased estimates of the mean and variance. [3 marks]
2. X has mean 8 and standard deviation 2. Find, approximately, the probability that the mean of 30 observations is less than 7.5. State the theorem used. [4 marks]
3. Explain why s² uses n − 1 rather than n. [2 marks]
4. The mean of a sample of 36 from a population with σ = 1.2 is used to estimate μ. Find the probability that the estimate is more than 0.2 above μ. [3 marks]
5. (MEI) Give one reason a sample might be preferred to a census, and one advantage of a larger sample. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. x̄ = 10 (1). s² = (1530 − 1200)/11 = 30 (2).
2. Central limit theorem (1). X̄ ≈ N(8, 4/30) (1). P(Z < −0.5/0.3651 = −1.369) = 0.0855 (2).
3. The sample values are closer to x̄ than to μ, so dividing by n underestimates σ² on average (1); n − 1 gives an unbiased estimate (1).
4. Standard error 0.2 (1). P(Z > 1) (1) = 0.159 (1).
5. E.g. a census is too costly or testing destroys the item (1). Larger samples give smaller standard errors / more reliable estimates (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/40-correlation-and-regression.md -->

## Correlation and regression

This file covers bivariate data and scatter diagrams, the product moment correlation coefficient (pmcc) and its hypothesis test, Spearman's rank correlation coefficient and its test, choosing between them, least squares regression lines, the effect of coding, residuals, and using regression lines for prediction.

### Overview

Scatter diagrams and interpreting correlation are in A-level Maths ([../maths/20-data-presentation-and-interpretation.md](../maths/20-data-presentation-and-interpretation.md)), with the pmcc test in [../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md](../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md) for some boards.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel FS2 1.1, 1.2 (least squares regression, residuals and residual sum of squares), 3.1 to 3.3 (pmcc, Spearman, tests).
- OCR 5.08a to 5.08g, 5.09a to 5.09e. OCR expects you to know when the pmcc test is valid (bivariate normal) and to choose between tests.
- MEI Sb1 to Sb16 includes the regression line of x on y as well as y on x (Sb13), effect size (Sb7), and interpreting computer output.
- AQA 7367 does not include correlation and regression.

### Product moment correlation

*Refs: Edexcel FS2 3.1, 3.3 · OCR 5.08a, 5.08b, 5.08c, 5.08d · MEI Sb1, Sb2, Sb3, Sb4, Sb5, Sb6, Sb7. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
S_{xx} = \sum x^2 - \frac{(\sum x)^2}{n},\quad S_{xy} = \sum xy - \frac{\sum x\sum y}{n},\quad r = \frac{S_{xy}}{\sqrt{S_{xx}S_{yy}}}
```

- −1 ≤ r ≤ 1. r measures linear association only. Correlation does not imply causation.
- Coding x and y by linear transformations does not change r (except for sign if one scale is reversed).
- Test H₀: ρ = 0 against ρ > 0, ρ < 0 or ρ ≠ 0 using tables of critical values for n. The test assumes the data come from a bivariate normal distribution (an elliptical scatter).

#### Worked example

x: 2, 4, 5, 7, 8, 10. y: 15, 22, 24, 30, 35, 40. Find r and test at 5% for positive correlation.

1. Σx = 36, Σx² = 258, Σy = 166, Σy² = 5010, Σxy = 1128.
2. S_xx = 42, S_yy = 417.33, S_xy = 132.
3. r = 132/√(42 × 417.33) = 0.997.
4. H₀: ρ = 0, H₁: ρ > 0. Critical value for n = 6 at 5% one-tailed: 0.7293. Reject H₀: strong evidence of positive correlation.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the pmcc test when the scatter is clearly not elliptical.

### Spearman's rank correlation

*Refs: Edexcel FS2 3.2, 3.3 · OCR 5.08e, 5.08f, 5.08g · MEI Sb8, Sb9, Sb10. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
r_s = 1 - \frac{6\sum d^2}{n(n^2-1)}
```

- Rank each variable, find differences d. (With tied ranks, give the mean rank and use the pmcc formula on the ranks.)
- Spearman measures monotonic association (whether one tends to increase as the other does), not just linear.
- Test H₀: no association, using tables for Spearman.
- Choose Spearman when the data are ranks, the relationship is non-linear but monotonic, or the data are not bivariate normal.

#### Worked example

Two judges rank seven entries: A 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 and B 2, 1, 4, 3, 7, 5, 6. Test at 5% for agreement.

1. d² values: 1, 1, 1, 1, 4, 1, 1, Σd² = 10.
2. r_s = 1 − 60/(7 × 48) = 0.821.
3. H₁: positive association. Critical value for n = 7 at 5% one-tailed: 0.7143. Reject H₀: evidence the judges agree.

#### Common mistakes

- Ranking one variable in ascending order and the other descending.

### Least squares regression

*Refs: Edexcel FS2 1.1, 1.2 · OCR 5.09a, 5.09b, 5.09c, 5.09d, 5.09e · MEI Sb11, Sb12, Sb13, Sb14, Sb15, Sb16. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- The regression line of y on x, y = a + bx, minimises the sum of squared vertical distances (residuals):

```latex
b = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}},\qquad a = \bar y - b\bar x
```

- Use y on x when x is the independent (controlled) variable and y is to be predicted. (MEI) The line of x on y is x = a′ + b′y with b′ = S_xy/S_yy; both lines pass through (x̄, ȳ).
- Residual = observed y − predicted y. Residual sum of squares RSS = S_yy − (S_xy)²/S_xx (Edexcel).
- Interpolation (inside the data range) is reliable; extrapolation is not.
- Coding: if X = (x − p)/q and Y = (y − r)/s, find the line in X and Y and substitute back.

#### Worked example

For the data above, find the regression line of y on x, the residual at x = 5, and the RSS.

1. b = 132/42 = 3.143. a = 27.667 − 3.143 × 6 = 8.81. y = 8.81 + 3.14x.
2. At x = 5: predicted 24.52, residual 24 − 24.52 = −0.52.
3. RSS = 417.33 − 132²/42 = 2.48.

Interpret the gradient if x is hours of practice and y is a test score.

1. Each extra hour of practice is associated with about 3.1 more marks.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the y on x line to predict x.
- Extrapolating far outside the data.

### Check questions

1. For 8 data pairs, Σx = 40, Σy = 96, Σx² = 240, Σy² = 1300, Σxy = 530. Find r and the regression line of y on x. [6 marks]
2. A pmcc of −0.62 is found for 10 pairs. Test at 5% (two-tailed) whether there is correlation (critical value 0.6319). [3 marks]
3. Explain when Spearman's coefficient is preferable to the pmcc. [2 marks]
4. Find r_s for ranks (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6) and (3, 1, 2, 6, 4, 5), and test for positive association at 5% (critical value 0.8286). [5 marks]
5. The data were coded with X = (x − 100)/5 and Y = y/10. The coded regression line is Y = 2.4 + 0.6X. Find the line in terms of x and y. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. S_xx = 40, S_yy = 148, S_xy = 50 (3). r = 50/√5920 = 0.650 (1). b = 1.25, a = 12 − 1.25 × 5 = 5.75 (1). y = 5.75 + 1.25x (1).
2. |−0.62| < 0.6319 (1). Do not reject H₀ (1). No significant evidence of correlation (1).
3. When data are ranked, or the relationship is monotonic but not linear, or the data are not bivariate normal (2).
4. d: −2, 1, 1, −2, 1, 1; Σd² = 12 (2). r_s = 1 − 72/210 = 0.657 (2). 0.657 < 0.8286, no evidence of positive association (1).
5. y/10 = 2.4 + 0.6(x − 100)/5 (1). y = 24 + 1.2(x − 100) (1) = 1.2x − 96 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md -->

## Statistical hypothesis testing

This file covers the language of hypothesis testing, binomial tests for a proportion (one-tailed and two-tailed, with critical regions and p-values), tests for the mean of a Normal distribution with known variance, and tests for correlation using a given correlation coefficient.

### Overview

AS: the language (null and alternative hypotheses, significance level, test statistic, one- and two-tailed tests, critical value, critical and acceptance regions, p-value) and the binomial test. A-level only: the distribution of the sample mean, the Normal test for a mean, and correlation tests.

#### Board differences

- AQA O1 to O3; Edexcel Statistics 5.1 to 5.3.
- OCR H240: 2.05a to 2.05c (AS); 2.05d to 2.05g (A-level only).
- OCR MEI H640: MH1 to MH6 (AS); MH7 to MH11 (A-level only).
- All boards: you are not asked to calculate a correlation coefficient. You interpret a given coefficient against a given critical value or p-value. OCR and MEI refer to Pearson's product-moment correlation coefficient; AQA and Edexcel refer to correlation coefficients as measures of how close points lie to a straight line.

### O1 The language of hypothesis testing; correlation tests

*Refs: AQA O1 · Edexcel Statistics 5.1 · OCR 2.05a, 2.05f, 2.05g · MEI MH1, MH2, MH4, MH10, MH11. Level: AS & A (part A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- **Null hypothesis H₀**: the assumption being tested, stating a parameter value (for example p = 0.3).
- **Alternative hypothesis H₁**: what you accept if the evidence is strong enough (p > 0.3, p < 0.3, or p ≠ 0.3).
- **One-tailed test**: H₁ has > or <. **Two-tailed test**: H₁ has ≠; split the significance level between the two tails.
- **Significance level**: the probability of rejecting H₀ when it is in fact true; the threshold for "strong enough evidence".
- **Test statistic**: the value calculated from the sample (for example the number of successes).
- **Critical region**: the set of values of the test statistic that lead to rejecting H₀. **Critical value**: the first value in the critical region. **Acceptance region**: the rest.
- **p-value**: the probability, assuming H₀ is true, of a result at least as extreme as the one observed. Reject H₀ if the p-value is less than the significance level.
- (A-level only) A correlation coefficient measures how close points lie to a straight line (−1 to 1). Test for correlation using a given coefficient and a given critical value or p-value.

#### Explained

Hypotheses are always about the population parameter (p, μ or ρ), never about the sample.

A conclusion has two parts: a statistical decision ("reject H₀" or "do not reject H₀"), and a statement in context that is not too definite ("there is sufficient evidence at the 5% level to suggest that the proportion has increased"). Not rejecting H₀ does not prove it true.

The actual significance level of a binomial test is the probability of the critical region, which is usually a little less than the stated level because the distribution is discrete.

**(A-level only) Correlation tests.** The population correlation coefficient is ρ.

- H₀: ρ = 0 (no correlation in the population).
- H₁: ρ > 0, ρ < 0 (one-tailed) or ρ ≠ 0 (two-tailed).
- Compare the sample value r with the critical value from tables (for the sample size n and significance level). Reject H₀ if |r| is bigger than the critical value (and r has the sign in H₁). Or compare a given p-value with the significance level.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) For 20 days, the correlation between daily rainfall and hours of sunshine has r = −0.42. The critical value for a one-tailed test at 5% with n = 20 is 0.3783. Test whether there is negative correlation.

1. H₀: ρ = 0. H₁: ρ < 0.
2. |−0.42| = 0.42 > 0.3783, and r is negative.
3. Reject H₀. There is evidence at the 5% level of negative correlation between rainfall and sunshine.

(A-level only) A test for correlation in a sample of 12 gives r = 0.52 and a two-tailed p-value of 0.083. Test at 5%.

1. H₀: ρ = 0. H₁: ρ ≠ 0.
2. 0.083 > 0.05. Do not reject H₀.
3. Insufficient evidence at the 5% level of correlation.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing hypotheses in terms of the sample (x̄ or r) instead of the parameter.
- Concluding "H₀ is true" or "this proves" instead of "insufficient evidence".
- (A-level only) Comparing r with the critical value for the wrong number of tails.

### O2 Binomial tests for a proportion

*Refs: AQA O2 · Edexcel Statistics 5.2 · OCR 2.05b, 2.05c · MEI MH3, MH5, MH6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Test a proportion p using X ~ B(n, p) under H₀, and interpret the result in context.
- Find critical regions for one- and two-tailed tests, and the actual significance level.
- A sample is being used to make an inference about a population; the significance level is the probability of wrongly rejecting H₀.

#### Explained

Steps:

1. Define X (the number of successes in the sample) and state its distribution under H₀.
2. Write H₀ and H₁ using p.
3. Either find the p-value, P(X ≥ observed) for an upper tail or P(X ≤ observed) for a lower tail, or find the critical region.
4. Compare with the significance level (half of it in each tail for a two-tailed test).
5. Conclude in context.

For an upper-tail critical region at level α, find the smallest c with P(X ≥ c) ≤ α. For a lower tail, find the largest c with P(X ≤ c) ≤ α.

#### Worked example

A website says 30% of visitors buy something. After a redesign, 10 of 20 visitors buy something. Test at the 5% level whether the proportion has increased.

1. X = number of buyers in 20. Under H₀, X ~ B(20, 0.3).
2. H₀: p = 0.3. H₁: p > 0.3.
3. P(X ≥ 10) = 1 − P(X ≤ 9) = 0.0480.
4. 0.0480 < 0.05, so reject H₀.
5. There is evidence at the 5% level that the proportion of buyers has increased.

Find the critical region for this test, and its actual significance level.

1. P(X ≥ 10) = 0.0480 ≤ 0.05; P(X ≥ 9) = 0.1133 > 0.05.
2. Critical region X ≥ 10. Actual significance level 4.80%.

A coin is tossed 25 times to test whether it is fair, at the 5% level. Find the critical region.

1. H₀: p = 0.5. H₁: p ≠ 0.5. X ~ B(25, 0.5). 2.5% in each tail.
2. Lower: P(X ≤ 7) = 0.0216 ≤ 0.025; P(X ≤ 8) = 0.0539. So X ≤ 7.
3. Upper: P(X ≥ 18) = 0.0216; P(X ≥ 17) = 0.0539. So X ≥ 18.
4. Critical region X ≤ 7 or X ≥ 18. Actual significance level 0.0432.

40% of people in a town own a dog. In a sample of 30 from one street, 6 own a dog. Test at 5% whether the proportion is lower in that street.

1. H₀: p = 0.4. H₁: p < 0.4. X ~ B(30, 0.4).
2. P(X ≤ 6) = 0.0172 < 0.05. Reject H₀.
3. There is evidence at the 5% level that dog ownership is lower in that street.

#### Common mistakes

- Using P(X = observed) instead of P(X ≥ observed).
- For an upper tail, using 1 − P(X ≤ observed) instead of 1 − P(X ≤ observed − 1).
- Forgetting to halve the significance level in a two-tailed test.

### O3 Testing the mean of a Normal distribution

*Refs: AQA O3 · Edexcel Statistics 5.3 · OCR 2.05d, 2.05e · MEI MH7, MH8, MH9. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- (A-level only) If X ~ N(μ, σ²), the mean of a random sample of size n has distribution

```latex
\bar{X} \sim N\!\left(\mu, \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right)
```

- (A-level only) Test H₀: μ = μ₀ against a one- or two-tailed H₁ when σ is known, given or assumed.
- (A-level only) Find the critical region for x̄.

#### Explained

The sample mean varies less than single values: its standard deviation is σ/√n. Standardise:

```latex
z = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma / \sqrt{n}}
```

Compare z with the critical value from the standard Normal (1.6449 for 5% one-tailed, ±1.96 for 5% two-tailed, 2.3263 for 1% one-tailed, ±2.5758 for 1% two-tailed), or find the p-value.

#### Worked example

(A-level only) Bags of flour have masses N(μ, 4²) grams, with μ = 50 claimed. A sample of 25 bags has mean 51.8 g. Test at 5% whether the mean is greater than 50.

1. H₀: μ = 50. H₁: μ > 50.
2. Under H₀, X̄ ~ N(50, 16/25), standard deviation 0.8.
3. z = (51.8 − 50)/0.8 = 2.25 > 1.6449. (p-value = P(Z > 2.25) = 0.0122.)
4. Reject H₀. There is evidence at the 5% level that the mean mass is greater than 50 g.

(A-level only) Find the critical region for x̄ in that test.

1. x̄ > 50 + 1.6449 × 0.8 = 51.32.

(A-level only) IQ scores have σ = 15. A sample of 36 people from one area has mean 115.2. Test at 1% whether the mean differs from 120.

1. H₀: μ = 120. H₁: μ ≠ 120.
2. z = (115.2 − 120)/(15/6) = −1.92.
3. Critical values ±2.5758. |−1.92| < 2.5758, so do not reject H₀.
4. Insufficient evidence at the 1% level that the mean in this area differs from 120.

#### Common mistakes

- (A-level only) Using σ instead of σ/√n.
- (A-level only) Using 1.6449 for a two-tailed 5% test.

### Check questions

1. Explain what is meant by (a) a critical region; (b) the significance level of a test. [2 marks]
2. A seed company claims 60% of its seeds germinate. A gardener plants 15 seeds and thinks the rate is lower. (a) Find the critical region for a test at the 5% level. (b) 5 seeds germinate. State the conclusion. [6 marks]
3. A spinner should land on red with probability 0.25. In 40 spins it lands on red 15 times. Test at the 5% level whether the probability of red is greater than 0.25. [5 marks]
4. It is thought that 20% of a population carry a gene. Find the critical region for a two-tailed test at the 5% level using a sample of 50, and state the actual significance level. [5 marks]
5. (A-level only) The times to complete a task are N(μ, 6²) minutes, and μ has been 80. After training, a sample of 16 times has mean 83.1. Test at the 5% level whether the mean time has increased. Give the p-value. [6 marks]
6. (A-level only) The lengths of rods are Normal with σ = 1.2 cm. A sample of 30 rods has mean 24.3 cm. Test at the 5% level whether the mean length differs from 25 cm. [6 marks]
7. (A-level only) For 30 pairs of data, r = 0.33. The critical value for a one-tailed test at 5% is 0.3061. Test for positive correlation. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) The set of values of the test statistic that lead to rejecting H₀ (1). (b) The probability of rejecting H₀ when H₀ is true (1).
2. (a) H₀: p = 0.6, H₁: p < 0.6, X ~ B(15, 0.6) (1); P(X ≤ 5) = 0.0338 (1); P(X ≤ 6) = 0.0950 (1); critical region X ≤ 5 (1). (b) 5 is in the critical region, reject H₀ (1); evidence that the germination rate is lower than 60% (1).
3. H₀: p = 0.25, H₁: p > 0.25 (1); X ~ B(40, 0.25) (1); P(X ≥ 15) = 0.0544 (1); 0.0544 > 0.05, do not reject H₀ (1); insufficient evidence that the probability of red is greater than 0.25 (1).
4. X ~ B(50, 0.2), 2.5% each tail (1); P(X ≤ 4) = 0.0185 and P(X ≤ 5) = 0.0480, so X ≤ 4 (1); P(X ≥ 17) = 0.0144 and P(X ≥ 16) = 0.0308, so X ≥ 17 (1); critical region X ≤ 4 or X ≥ 17 (1); actual significance 0.0329 (1).
5. H₀: μ = 80, H₁: μ > 80 (1); X̄ ~ N(80, 36/16) (1); z = 3.1/1.5 = 2.07 (1); p-value = 0.0194 (1); < 0.05, reject H₀ (1); evidence that the mean time has increased (1).
6. H₀: μ = 25, H₁: μ ≠ 25 (1); standard error 1.2/√30 = 0.219 (1); z = −3.20 (1); critical values ±1.96 (1); reject H₀ (1); evidence that the mean length is not 25 cm (1).
7. H₀: ρ = 0, H₁: ρ > 0 (1); 0.33 > 0.3061 (1); reject H₀: evidence of positive correlation (1).

## Chi-squared tests: goodness of fit and contingency tables

This file covers the χ² statistic, tests for association in contingency tables, goodness of fit tests for given and fitted distributions, degrees of freedom, combining classes when expected frequencies are small, Yates' correction, and interpreting where association comes from.

### Overview

Hypothesis testing language is in A-level Maths ([../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md](../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md)). The distributions being fitted are in [30-discrete-random-variables.md](30-discrete-random-variables.md), [31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md](31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md) and [35-continuous-random-variables.md](35-continuous-random-variables.md). Chi-squared tests for a variance are in [39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md](39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SE1 to SE5 (contingency tables only, including Yates' correction for 2 × 2 tables). AQA does not include goodness of fit.
- Edexcel FS1 6.1: goodness of fit (discrete uniform, binomial, Poisson, geometric and others) and contingency tables. Edexcel does not use Yates' correction.
- OCR 5.06a to 5.06d: contingency tables and goodness of fit; 5.06c (Stage 2) extends to continuous distributions and others.
- MEI SH1 to SH4: contingency tables and goodness of fit, interpreting contributions to the statistic.
- All boards combine classes so that expected frequencies are at least 5.

### SE2 The chi-squared statistic

*Refs: AQA SE2, SE3 · Edexcel FS1 6.1 · OCR 5.06a, 5.06d · MEI SH1, SH2. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
X^2 = \sum\frac{(O_i - E_i)^2}{E_i}
```

- Under H₀, X² approximately follows χ²_ν, where ν (degrees of freedom) = number of cells (after combining) − number of constraints.
- Reject H₀ if X² is greater than the critical value. The test is always one-tailed (upper tail).
- Each Eᵢ should be at least 5; combine neighbouring classes if not.

#### Explained

Large differences between observed and expected frequencies make X² big. The degrees of freedom count how many expected frequencies are free to vary: the total is always fixed (one constraint), and each parameter estimated from the data costs one more.

### SE1 Contingency tables

*Refs: AQA SE1, SE2, SE3, SE4, SE5 · Edexcel FS1 6.1 · OCR 5.06a · MEI SH1, SH2. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- H₀: no association between the two factors (they are independent). H₁: there is association.
- Expected frequency = (row total × column total)/grand total.
- For an r × c table, ν = (r − 1)(c − 1).
- (AQA) For a 2 × 2 table use Yates' correction: Σ(|O − E| − 0.5)²/E.
- Identify sources of association by comparing the largest contributions (O − E)²/E and whether O > E or O < E.

#### Worked example

60 pupils in each of two year groups chose a favourite club. Test at 5% for association.

| | Art | Chess | Drama |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Year 10 | 30 | 20 | 10 |
| Year 11 | 20 | 25 | 15 |

1. H₀: no association between year and club choice.
2. Column totals 50, 45, 25; grand total 120. Expected values: 25, 22.5, 12.5 in each row.
3. In each row the (O − E)² values are 25, 6.25, 6.25, so X² = 2 × [25/25 + 6.25/22.5 + 6.25/12.5] = 2 × [1 + 0.278 + 0.5] = 3.556.
4. ν = (2 − 1)(3 − 1) = 2. Critical value 5.991.
5. 3.556 < 5.991: do not reject H₀. No evidence of association.
6. The largest contributions (Art) show Year 10 chose art more than expected.

(AQA) 2 × 2 table with Yates' correction:

| | Pass | Fail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Revised | 25 | 15 |
| Did not | 10 | 20 |

1. Expected: 20, 20 / 15, 15. |O − E| = 5 each.
2. X² = 4.5²(1/20 + 1/20 + 1/15 + 1/15) = 20.25 × 0.2333 = 4.725.
3. ν = 1, critical value 3.841 at 5%. Reject H₀: evidence of association; those who revised passed more often than expected.

#### Common mistakes

- Using percentages instead of frequencies.
- Forgetting to combine cells with E < 5 (which also reduces ν).

### Goodness of fit

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 6.1 · OCR 5.06b, 5.06c, 5.06d · MEI SH3, SH4. Level: AS & A (OCR 5.06c A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- H₀: the data come from the stated distribution.
- Expected frequency = total × probability under H₀.
- ν = (number of classes after combining) − 1 − (number of parameters estimated from the data).

#### Worked example

A die is rolled 120 times: 15, 22, 18, 25, 16, 24. Test at 5% whether it is fair.

1. H₀: uniform, each E = 20.
2. X² = (25 + 4 + 4 + 25 + 16 + 16)/20 = 4.5.
3. ν = 5, critical value 11.07. Not significant: no evidence the die is biased.

Over 100 time intervals, counts of 0, 1, 2, 3 or more events were 30, 38, 20, 12, with sample mean 1.18. Test at 5% whether a Poisson model fits.

1. H₀: Poisson. Estimate λ = 1.18.
2. Expected: 100P(0) = 30.73, 100P(1) = 36.26, 100P(2) = 21.39, 100P(≥3) = 11.62.
3. X² = 0.017 + 0.084 + 0.091 + 0.012 = 0.204.
4. ν = 4 − 1 − 1 = 2, critical value 5.991. Not significant: a Poisson model fits well.

A genetics model predicts ratios 1 : 2 : 1. Observed 10, 28, 22 out of 60. Test at 5%.

1. E: 15, 30, 15. X² = 25/15 + 4/30 + 49/15 = 5.067.
2. ν = 2, critical value 5.991. Not significant at 5%.

#### Common mistakes

- Not subtracting 1 for each estimated parameter.
- Leaving the last class as "3" instead of "3 or more", so probabilities do not add to 1.

### Check questions

1. In a survey, 80 people were asked which of three brands they prefer, by age group. (a) State the null hypothesis. (b) Explain how to find an expected frequency. (c) The X² value is 7.2 for a 2 × 3 table. Complete the test at 5%. [5 marks]
2. (AQA) A 2 × 2 table has observed frequencies 18, 12 / 22, 28. Test for association at 5% using Yates' correction. [7 marks]
3. A spinner has five equal sectors. 100 spins give 14, 25, 19, 26, 16. Test at 10% whether it is fair. [5 marks]
4. 200 families with 3 children are recorded by number of girls: 0, 1, 2, 3 girls in 30, 80, 70, 20 families. Fit a binomial model B(3, p) estimating p from the data, and test the fit at 5%. [8 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) No association between age group and preferred brand (1). (b) Row total × column total ÷ grand total (1). (c) ν = 2, critical value 5.991 (1). 7.2 > 5.991, reject H₀ (1). Evidence of association between age and brand (1).
2. Totals: rows 30, 50; columns 40, 40; grand 80 (1). Expected 15, 15 / 25, 25 (1). |O − E| = 3, corrected 2.5 (1). X² = 6.25(2/15 + 2/25) = 1.333 (2). ν = 1, critical value 3.841 (1). Not significant: no evidence of association (1).
3. E = 20 each (1). X² = (36 + 25 + 1 + 36 + 16)/20 = 5.7 (2). ν = 4, critical value at 10% 7.779 (1). Not significant: no evidence the spinner is unfair (1).
4. Mean girls = (80 + 140 + 60)/200 = 1.4, p = 1.4/3 = 0.4667 (2). Expected: 200(0.5333)³ = 30.3, 200 × 3(0.4667)(0.5333)² = 79.6, 200 × 3(0.4667)²(0.5333) = 69.7, 200(0.4667)³ = 20.3 (2). X² ≈ 0.01 (2). ν = 4 − 1 − 1 = 2, critical value 5.991 (1). Very good fit: accept the binomial model (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/41-non-parametric-tests.md -->

## Non-parametric tests

This file covers what a non-parametric test is, the sign test, the Wilcoxon signed-rank test for a single sample or paired samples, the Wilcoxon rank-sum test for two independent samples, and normal approximations for large samples.

### Overview

OCR only. These tests replace t-tests ([38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md](38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md)) when the population cannot be assumed normal. The sign test uses the binomial distribution ([../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md](../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md)).

#### Board differences

- OCR 5.07a to 5.07f. Critical values are read from the tables in the OCR formula booklet. No other board includes these tests.

### Non-parametric tests and the sign test

*Refs: OCR 5.07a, 5.07b, 5.07c, 5.07d. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A non-parametric test makes no assumption about the form of the population distribution. It tests a median rather than a mean.
- Sign test for a median m₀: count the values above m₀ (ignore values equal to it). Under H₀ the count is B(n, ½).
- Wilcoxon signed-rank test: also uses the sizes of the differences, so it is more powerful, but assumes the distribution is symmetric.
- Paired samples: test the differences; independent samples need a different test (rank-sum).

#### Worked example

Eight readings: 5.1, 4.2, 6.3, 3.8, 4.6, 3.2, 4.0, 3.5. Use a sign test at 5% to test whether the median is less than 5.

1. H₀: median 5, H₁: median < 5.
2. Above 5: 2 (5.1 and 6.3); below: 6.
3. P(X ≤ 2) for B(8, ½) = 37/256 = 0.145 > 0.05. Do not reject H₀.

#### Common mistakes

- Counting values equal to the hypothesised median.

### The Wilcoxon signed-rank test

*Refs: OCR 5.07b, 5.07c, 5.07d, 5.07f. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

1. Find d = x − m₀ (or the paired differences); discard zeros.
2. Rank |d| from smallest (1) to largest; ties get the mean of their ranks.
3. P = sum of ranks of positive d, Q = sum for negative d (P + Q = n(n + 1)/2).
4. T = the relevant sum (the smaller one for a two-tailed test; for H₁: median < m₀, T = P). Reject H₀ if T ≤ the critical value.
- For large n, T ≈ N(n(n + 1)/4, n(n + 1)(2n + 1)/24), with a continuity correction.

#### Worked example

Same data, Wilcoxon signed-rank test at 5% for median < 5.

1. d: 0.1, −0.8, 1.3, −1.2, −0.4, −1.8, −1.0, −1.5.
2. Ranks of |d|: 1, 3, 6, 5, 2, 8, 4, 7.
3. P = 1 + 6 = 7, Q = 29.
4. Critical value for n = 8, one-tailed 5%: 5. T = 7 > 5. Do not reject H₀ (just).

For n = 30 a statistic T = 140 is found. Use a normal approximation to test at 5% (one-tailed).

1. Mean 232.5, variance 30 × 31 × 61/24 = 2363.75, s.d. 48.62.
2. z = (140.5 − 232.5)/48.62 = −1.892 < −1.645. Reject H₀.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting the continuity correction in the normal approximation.

### The Wilcoxon rank-sum test

*Refs: OCR 5.07e, 5.07f. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- For two independent samples (sizes m ≤ n), H₀: the populations have the same median (identical distributions).
- Rank all m + n values together. W = sum of ranks of the smaller sample. Compare the smaller of W and m(m + n + 1) − W with the table.
- Large samples: W ≈ N(½m(m + n + 1), mn(m + n + 1)/12).

#### Worked example

Group A (5 values): 23, 28, 31, 35, 40. Group B (6 values): 19, 22, 26, 27, 30, 33. Test at 5% (two-tailed) for a difference.

1. Combined ranks: 19 (1), 22 (2), 23 (3), 26 (4), 27 (5), 28 (6), 30 (7), 31 (8), 33 (9), 35 (10), 40 (11).
2. W for A = 3 + 6 + 8 + 10 + 11 = 38. m(m + n + 1) − W = 60 − 38 = 22.
3. Test statistic 22. Critical value (m = 5, n = 6, 5% two-tailed): 18. 22 > 18: do not reject H₀.

#### Common mistakes

- Ranking each sample separately.

### Check questions

1. State one advantage and one disadvantage of a non-parametric test compared with a t-test. [2 marks]
2. Ten people rate a product: the median claimed is 7. Scores: 8, 6, 9, 9, 5, 8, 10, 7, 8, 9. Use a sign test at 5% to test whether the median is greater than 7. [4 marks]
3. Use a Wilcoxon signed-rank test on the paired differences 1.2, −0.4, 2.1, 0.8, 1.5, −0.2, 0.9, 1.7 to test at 5% whether the population median difference is positive (critical value 5). [5 marks]
4. Explain why the Wilcoxon signed-rank test needs a symmetric distribution while the sign test does not. [1 mark]

### Mark scheme

1. Advantage: no assumption of normality (1). Disadvantage: less powerful when the data are normal / uses less information (1).
2. Exclude the 7: n = 9, above 7: 8 (1). P(X ≥ 8 | B(9, ½)) = 10/512 = 0.0195 (2). < 0.05: evidence the median is greater than 7 (1).
3. Ranks of |d|: 0.2 (1), 0.4 (2), 0.8 (3), 0.9 (4), 1.2 (5), 1.5 (6), 1.7 (7), 2.1 (8) (2). Negative ranks sum Q = 3 (1). T = 3 ≤ 5 (1). Reject H₀: evidence the median difference is positive (1).
4. It uses the sizes of deviations on both sides, which only balance under H₀ if the distribution is symmetric about the median (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md -->

## Linear combinations of random variables

This file covers the expectation and variance of sums and other linear combinations of random variables, the difference between X₁ + X₂ and 2X, and linear combinations of independent normal variables.

### Overview

Expectation and variance of single variables are in [30-discrete-random-variables.md](30-discrete-random-variables.md) and [35-continuous-random-variables.md](35-continuous-random-variables.md). The normal distribution is in [../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md](../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md). The sample mean as a linear combination leads to [37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md](37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SD8 (E and Var of X + Y for independent X, Y). Edexcel FS2 4.1 (linear combinations of normal variables). OCR 5.04a, 5.04b. MEI SR28, SR29.
- AQA lists only the general results for X + Y; Edexcel, OCR and MEI apply them to normal distributions.

### SD8 Expectation and variance of combinations

*Refs: AQA SD8 · Edexcel FS2 4.1 · OCR 5.04a · MEI SR29. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
E(aX + bY) = aE(X) + bE(Y)
```

```latex
\mathrm{Var}(aX \pm bY) = a^2\,\mathrm{Var}(X) + b^2\,\mathrm{Var}(Y)\quad(X, Y\text{ independent})
```

- The expectation rule holds always; the variance rule needs independence.
- Var(X − Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y): variances add even when you subtract.
- X₁ + X₂ + ... + Xₙ (n independent copies of X) has mean nμ and variance nσ². But nX (one observation multiplied by n) has variance n²σ².

#### Explained

Subtracting a random quantity adds uncertainty, which is why the variances add. The difference between X₁ + X₂ and 2X matters: two separate bags of flour vary less in total, relative to their mean, than one bag doubled.

#### Worked example

X and Y are independent with E(X) = 5, Var(X) = 2, E(Y) = 3, Var(Y) = 1. Find E(2X − 3Y) and Var(2X − 3Y).

1. E = 10 − 9 = 1.
2. Var = 4(2) + 9(1) = 17.

X has variance 4. Compare Var(X₁ + X₂ + X₃) and Var(3X).

1. Var(X₁ + X₂ + X₃) = 12. Var(3X) = 36.

#### Common mistakes

- Var(X − Y) = Var(X) − Var(Y).
- Writing 3X when the question means three independent observations.

### Combinations of independent normal variables

*Refs: Edexcel FS2 4.1 · OCR 5.04b · MEI SR28, SR29. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Any linear combination of independent normal variables is normal, with mean and variance from the rules above.

#### Worked example

Bags of flour have masses N(1000, 15²) grams. Find the probability that four bags have total mass less than 4050 g.

1. T ~ N(4000, 4 × 225 = 900), s.d. 30.
2. P(T < 4050) = Φ(50/30) = Φ(1.667) = 0.952.

X ~ N(50, 9) and Y ~ N(60, 16) independently. Find P(X > Y).

1. X − Y ~ N(−10, 25).
2. P(X − Y > 0) = P(Z > 2) = 0.0228.

A tin A has mass N(120, 4) and a lid B has mass N(38, 1). Find the probability that a tin weighs more than three times a lid.

1. A − 3B ~ N(120 − 114, 4 + 9) = N(6, 13).
2. P(A − 3B > 0) = Φ(6/√13) = Φ(1.664) = 0.952.

#### Common mistakes

- Using 3²σ² for the total of three independent lids.

### Check questions

1. X ~ N(20, 4), Y ~ N(15, 9) independently. Find the distribution of 3X − 2Y and P(3X − 2Y > 35). [5 marks]
2. A lift has a safe load of 1200 kg. Adults' masses are N(75, 12²). Find the probability that 15 adults exceed the safe load. [4 marks]
3. Eggs have masses N(60, 4²) grams and empty boxes N(30, 2²) grams. A full box holds 6 eggs. Find the probability that a full box weighs less than 380 g. [4 marks]
4. Two independent spinners give X with E(X) = 2, Var(X) = 1.5, and Y with E(Y) = 3.5, Var(Y) = 2.9. Find E(X + 2Y) and Var(X − 2Y). [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Mean 60 − 30 = 30 (1). Variance 9(4) + 4(9) = 72 (2). P(Z > 5/√72 = 0.589) = 0.278 (2).
2. Total ~ N(1125, 15 × 144 = 2160) (2). P(T > 1200) = P(Z > 75/46.48 = 1.614) = 0.0533 (2).
3. Total ~ N(390, 6 × 16 + 4 = 100) (2). P(T < 380) = Φ(−1) = 0.159 (2).
4. E(X + 2Y) = 9 (1). Var(X − 2Y) = 1.5 + 4(2.9) = 13.1 (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md -->

## Confidence intervals and tests for means (z and t)

This file covers confidence intervals for a population mean (variance known, large samples, and small samples using the t-distribution), interpreting intervals, tests for a mean using z and t, tests and intervals for the difference between two means, and the paired t-test.

### Overview

Sampling distributions and the central limit theorem are in [37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md](37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md). The test for a normal mean with known variance is in A-level Maths ([../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md](../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md)). Tests for variances are in [39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md](39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SG1 (one-sample t-test), SH1 to SH4 (z and t confidence intervals for one mean).
- Edexcel FS2 5.1 to 5.5 (z intervals and tests, difference of two means, large samples), 7.1 to 7.3 (t-test and interval for one mean, paired t-test, two-sample t-test with pooled variance).
- OCR 5.05c, 5.05d (normal tests and intervals for a mean, using the CLT for large samples). OCR uses non-parametric tests rather than t (file 41).
- MEI SI7 to SI13 (confidence intervals, including t and for paired samples), SH5, SH6 (tests for a mean using z and t, and paired samples).

### SH1 Confidence intervals for a mean

*Refs: AQA SH1, SH2, SH3, SH4 · Edexcel FS2 5.2, 5.3, 5.5, 7.1 · OCR 5.05d · MEI SI7, SI8, SI9, SI12, SI13. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Known σ (normal population, or any population with large n):

```latex
\bar x \pm z\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}\quad(z = 1.645, 1.960, 2.576\text{ for }90\%, 95\%, 99\%)
```

- Large sample, σ unknown: use s in place of σ (CLT).
- Small sample from a normal population, σ unknown:

```latex
\bar x \pm t_{n-1}\frac{s}{\sqrt n}
```

- A 95% interval is a random interval that contains μ in 95% of repeated samples. Wider for higher confidence, narrower for larger n.
- A value of μ outside the interval would be rejected by a two-tailed test at the matching level.

#### Worked example

A sample of 25 from N(μ, 16) has mean 52.3. Find a 95% confidence interval for μ.

1. 52.3 ± 1.96 × 4/5 = 52.3 ± 1.568.
2. (50.73, 53.87).

A sample of 100 has mean 3.42 and s = 0.8. Find a 95% interval for μ.

1. Large sample: 3.42 ± 1.96 × 0.08 = (3.26, 3.58).

Eight lengths (cm): 12.1, 11.8, 12.4, 12.0, 11.6, 12.3, 11.9, 12.5. Assuming normality, find a 95% interval for μ.

1. x̄ = 12.075, s = 0.3105, t₇ = 2.365.
2. 12.075 ± 2.365 × 0.3105/√8 = 12.075 ± 0.260 = (11.82, 12.33).

#### Common mistakes

- Using z for a small sample with unknown σ.
- Saying "there is a 95% probability μ is in this interval".

### SG1 Tests for a mean

*Refs: AQA SG1 · Edexcel FS2 5.4, 7.1 · OCR 5.05c · MEI SH5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- z-test (σ known, or large n): Z = (x̄ − μ₀)/(σ/√n).
- t-test (small sample, normal population, σ unknown): T = (x̄ − μ₀)/(s/√n) with n − 1 degrees of freedom.

#### Worked example

For the eight lengths above, test at 5% whether the mean is less than 12.3 cm.

1. H₀: μ = 12.3, H₁: μ < 12.3.
2. t = (12.075 − 12.3)/(0.3105/√8) = −2.049.
3. Critical value −t₇(5%) = −1.895. −2.049 < −1.895: reject H₀. Evidence the mean is less than 12.3 cm.

#### Common mistakes

- Using n instead of n − 1 for the degrees of freedom.

### Two means and paired data

*Refs: Edexcel FS2 5.4, 7.2, 7.3 · MEI SI10, SI11, SH6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Difference of means, large samples or known variances:

```latex
Z = \frac{(\bar x_1 - \bar x_2) - (\mu_1 - \mu_2)}{\sqrt{\dfrac{\sigma_1^2}{n_1} + \dfrac{\sigma_2^2}{n_2}}}
```

- (Edexcel) Two small samples from normal populations with a common variance: pool the variance and use t with n₁ + n₂ − 2 degrees of freedom:

```latex
s_p^2 = \frac{(n_1-1)s_1^2 + (n_2-1)s_2^2}{n_1+n_2-2}
```

- Paired data (the same subjects measured twice): work with the differences d and do a one-sample test on them with n − 1 degrees of freedom.
- (MEI SI10) Pair the data when each value in one sample is naturally linked to one value in the other.

#### Worked example

Sample 1: n = 40, mean 68.2, s = 5. Sample 2: n = 50, mean 65.9, s = 6. Test at 5% for a difference in population means.

1. H₀: μ₁ = μ₂, H₁: μ₁ ≠ μ₂.
2. z = 2.3/√(25/40 + 36/50) = 1.983.
3. Critical values ±1.96. 1.983 > 1.96: reject H₀ (just). Evidence of a difference.

(Edexcel) Two normal samples: n₁ = 6, x̄₁ = 24.5, s₁² = 4.1; n₂ = 8, x̄₂ = 22.1, s₂² = 3.6. Test at 5% (two-tailed) for a difference, assuming equal variances.

1. s_p² = (5 × 4.1 + 7 × 3.6)/12 = 3.808.
2. t = 2.4/√(3.808(1/6 + 1/8)) = 2.277.
3. t₁₂(2.5%) = 2.179. Reject H₀: evidence the means differ.

Eight students sit a test before and after a course. Differences (after − before): 3, −1, 4, 2, 5, 0, 3, 2. Test at 5% whether scores improved.

1. d̄ = 2.25, s_d = 1.982.
2. t = 2.25/(1.982/√8) = 3.211. t₇(5%) = 1.895.
3. Reject H₀: evidence of improvement.

#### Common mistakes

- Treating paired data as two independent samples.

### Check questions

1. A sample of 16 from N(μ, 9) has mean 20.4. Find a 99% confidence interval for μ. [3 marks]
2. Ten observations from a normal population have x̄ = 7.3 and s = 1.2. Find a 90% confidence interval for μ. [4 marks]
3. A 95% interval for μ is (14.2, 17.8) from a sample of 25 with σ known. Find x̄ and σ. [3 marks]
4. A machine is set to fill 500 ml. A sample of 12 gives x̄ = 497.5 ml, s = 3.8 ml. Test at 5% whether the machine under-fills. State an assumption. [6 marks]
5. Six athletes' times (s) before and after training: before 12.4, 12.9, 13.1, 12.6, 12.8, 13.0; after 12.2, 12.8, 12.7, 12.6, 12.5, 12.9. Test at 5% whether training reduced times. [7 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 20.4 ± 2.576 × 3/4 (2) = (18.47, 22.33) (1).
2. t₉(5%) = 1.833 (1). 7.3 ± 1.833 × 1.2/√10 (2) = (6.60, 8.00) (1).
3. x̄ = 16 (1). 1.96σ/5 = 1.8 (1), σ = 4.59 (1).
4. H₀: μ = 500, H₁: μ < 500 (1). t = −2.5/(3.8/√12) = −2.279 (2). t₁₁(5%) = −1.796 (1). Reject H₀: evidence of under-filling (1). Assume volumes are normally distributed (1).
5. Differences (before − after): 0.2, 0.1, 0.4, 0, 0.3, 0.1 (1). d̄ = 0.1833, s_d = 0.1472 (2). t = 0.1833/(0.1472/√6) = 3.05 (2). t₅(5%) = 2.015 (1). Reject H₀: evidence training reduced times (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/32-hypothesis-tests-errors-and-power.md -->

## Hypothesis tests: Poisson and geometric tests, Type I and II errors, power

This file covers hypothesis tests for the mean of a Poisson distribution and for the parameter of a geometric distribution, extended binomial tests, critical regions and actual significance levels, Type I and Type II errors, and the power of a test.

### Overview

The language of hypothesis testing and the binomial test are in A-level Maths ([../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md](../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md)). The distributions are in [31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md](31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md). Tests using normal and t distributions are in [38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md](38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SB5 (Poisson mean test), SC1 (Type I and Type II errors for Poisson tests), SC2 (power for tests based on normal, binomial or Poisson distributions).
- Edexcel FS1 4.1 (tests for binomial and Poisson parameters, including critical regions), 4.2 (tests for a geometric parameter), 8.1 (Type I and Type II errors, size and power, power functions).
- OCR and MEI do not list Type II errors or power; their Poisson-based inference is through chi-squared and normal tests.

### SB5 Testing a Poisson mean

*Refs: AQA SB5 · Edexcel FS1 4.1. Level: A-level (option).*

#### What you need to know

- H₀: λ = λ₀ against H₁: λ > λ₀, λ < λ₀ or λ ≠ λ₀, using a single observation (or a total over several intervals, which is Poisson with the scaled mean).
- Compare P(X ≥ x) (or P(X ≤ x)) under H₀ with the significance level, or find the critical region.
- The actual significance level is the probability of the critical region under H₀.
- For a two-tailed test at α, put at most α/2 in each tail.

#### Explained

Because the distribution is discrete, the actual significance level is usually less than the stated one. Always write the conclusion in context, and never say H₀ is "proved".

#### Worked example

A call centre normally receives 4 complaints a day. After a change, 8 complaints arrive in one day. Test at the 5% level whether the rate has increased.

1. H₀: λ = 4, H₁: λ > 4. X ~ Po(4) under H₀.
2. P(X ≥ 8) = 1 − P(X ≤ 7) = 0.0511 > 0.05.
3. Not significant: insufficient evidence that complaints have increased.
4. Critical region: P(X ≥ 9) = 0.0214, so X ≥ 9; actual significance level 2.14%.

Find the critical region for a two-tailed test of H₀: λ = 10 at the 5% level.

1. Lower tail: P(X ≤ 3) = 0.0103, P(X ≤ 4) = 0.0293 > 0.025, so X ≤ 3.
2. Upper tail: P(X ≥ 17) = 0.0270 > 0.025, P(X ≥ 18) = 0.0143, so X ≥ 18.
3. Critical region X ≤ 3 or X ≥ 18; actual significance level 0.0246.

Over 2 weeks a shop expects 2.5 returns per week. It records 3 returns in the 2 weeks. Test at 5% whether the rate has fallen.

1. Total ~ Po(5) under H₀. P(X ≤ 3) = 0.2650 > 0.05. Not significant.

#### Common mistakes

- Using P(X = 8) instead of P(X ≥ 8).
- Forgetting to scale λ for several intervals.

### Testing a geometric parameter

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 4.2. Level: A-level (option).*

#### What you need to know

- Use one observation of X ~ Geo(p). A large X suggests p is smaller than claimed; a small X suggests p is larger.
- P(X ≥ x) = (1 − p)^{x−1}; P(X ≤ x) = 1 − (1 − p)ˣ.

#### Worked example

A game claims a 1 in 5 chance of winning on each play. A player's first win comes on the 14th play. Test at 5% whether the claim overstates p.

1. H₀: p = 0.2, H₁: p < 0.2.
2. P(X ≥ 14) = 0.8¹³ = 0.0550 > 0.05. Not significant at 5%.

#### Common mistakes

- Using 0.8¹⁴ for P(X ≥ 14).

### SC1 Type I and Type II errors

*Refs: AQA SC1 · Edexcel FS1 8.1. Level: A-level (option).*

#### What you need to know

- Type I error: rejecting H₀ when it is true. P(Type I error) = actual significance level (the size of the test).
- Type II error: accepting H₀ when it is false. Its probability depends on the true parameter value.
- Reducing one error type (at fixed sample size) increases the other.

#### Worked example

For the complaints test (critical region X ≥ 9), find P(Type I error), and P(Type II error) if the true rate is 7.

1. P(Type I) = P(X ≥ 9 | λ = 4) = 0.0214.
2. P(Type II) = P(X ≤ 8 | λ = 7) = 0.7291.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving the stated level (5%) as P(Type I error) for a discrete test.

### SC2 The power of a test

*Refs: AQA SC2 · Edexcel FS1 8.1. Level: A-level (option).*

#### What you need to know

- Power = P(reject H₀ | H₀ false) = 1 − P(Type II error).
- A power function gives the power for each possible true value of the parameter. A better test has higher power for the same size.
- For a normal mean test with X̄ ~ N(μ, σ²/n), find the critical value under H₀, then the probability of the critical region under the alternative μ.

#### Worked example

Power of the complaints test when λ = 7.

1. 1 − 0.7291 = 0.2709.

X ~ B(20, p). H₀: p = 0.3, H₁: p < 0.3, 5% level. Find the critical region, the size, and the power when p = 0.1.

1. P(X ≤ 2) = 0.0355, P(X ≤ 3) = 0.1071. Critical region X ≤ 2, size 0.0355.
2. Power = P(X ≤ 2 | p = 0.1) = 0.6769.

(AQA) X ~ N(μ, 16). A sample of 16 is used to test H₀: μ = 50 against H₁: μ > 50 at 5%. Find the power when μ = 52.

1. X̄ ~ N(50, 1) under H₀. Critical value 50 + 1.645 = 51.645.
2. Power = P(X̄ > 51.645 | μ = 52) = P(Z > −0.355) = 0.639.

#### Common mistakes

- Calculating the power under H₀ instead of under the alternative.

### Check questions

1. Accidents at a junction average 1.5 per month. After new lights, there are 2 accidents in 6 months. Test at the 5% level whether the rate has fallen. [5 marks]
2. X ~ Po(λ). Find the critical region for testing H₀: λ = 6 against H₁: λ < 6 at the 5% level, and the actual significance level. [3 marks]
3. For the test in question 2, find the probability of a Type II error when λ = 2, and the power. [3 marks]
4. (Edexcel) A machine produces faulty items with probability 0.02. To test whether p has increased, items are inspected until the first faulty one; X is the number inspected. Find the critical region at the 5% level and its actual significance level. [3 marks]
5. X ~ N(μ, 25), samples of 25. H₀: μ = 100, H₁: μ ≠ 100, 5% level. Find the acceptance region for x̄ and the probability of a Type II error when μ = 102. [5 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. H₀: λ = 9, H₁: λ < 9 (6-month total) (1). P(X ≤ 2 | Po(9)) = 0.0062 (2). < 0.05, reject H₀ (1). Evidence the accident rate has fallen (1).
2. P(X ≤ 1) = 0.0174, P(X ≤ 2) = 0.0620 (1). Critical region X ≤ 1 (1). Size 0.0174 (1).
3. P(Type II) = P(X ≥ 2 | λ = 2) = 1 − 0.4060 = 0.5940 (2). Power 0.4060 (1).
4. Small X supports H₁: P(X ≤ x) = 1 − 0.98ˣ ≤ 0.05 (1). 0.98ˣ ≥ 0.95 gives x ≤ 2.54, so the critical region is X ≤ 2 (1). Actual level 1 − 0.98² = 0.0396 (1).
5. X̄ ~ N(100, 1) (1). Accept 98.04 < x̄ < 101.96 (2). P(98.04 < X̄ < 101.96 | μ = 102) = P(−3.96 < Z < −0.04) (1) = 0.484 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md -->

## Poisson, geometric and negative binomial distributions

This file covers the Poisson distribution (conditions, probabilities, mean and variance, sums of independent Poissons, approximating the binomial), the geometric distribution and (Edexcel) the negative binomial distribution, with their means and variances.

### Overview

The binomial distribution is in A-level Maths ([../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md](../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md)); expectation and variance in [30-discrete-random-variables.md](30-discrete-random-variables.md). Tests using these distributions are in [32-hypothesis-tests-errors-and-power.md](32-hypothesis-tests-errors-and-power.md). The exponential distribution of the gaps between Poisson events is in [35-continuous-random-variables.md](35-continuous-random-variables.md).

#### Board differences

- Poisson: AQA SB1 to SB4; Edexcel FS1 2.1 to 2.3; OCR 5.02i to 5.02n; MEI SR11 to SR15.
- Geometric: Edexcel FS1 3.1, 3.2; OCR 5.02f to 5.02h; MEI SR16 to SR18. AQA does not include it.
- Negative binomial: Edexcel FS1 3.1, 3.3 only.
- The Poisson approximation to the binomial is named by Edexcel FS1 2.3 and MEI SR12.

### SB1 The Poisson distribution

*Refs: AQA SB1, SB2, SB3 · Edexcel FS1 2.1, 2.2 · OCR 5.02i, 5.02j, 5.02k, 5.02l, 5.02m · MEI SR11, SR13, SR14. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
X\sim\mathrm{Po}(\lambda):\quad P(X=x) = \frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^x}{x!},\quad x = 0, 1, 2, \dots
```

- E(X) = Var(X) = λ.
- Conditions: events occur singly, independently, at random, and at a constant average rate. The rate scales with the interval: 3 per hour means 1.5 per half hour.
- A mean close to the variance in data supports a Poisson model.

#### Explained

The Poisson counts how many times something happens in a fixed interval of time or space: calls to a helpline in an hour, flaws in a metre of cable. Use the formula for single values and the calculator's cumulative function for ranges. "At least 5" is 1 − P(X ≤ 4).

#### Worked example

Emails arrive at a rate of 2.5 per hour. Find the probability of exactly 3 in an hour, at most 3, and more than 4.

1. P(X = 3) = e^{−2.5} × 2.5³/6 = 0.2138.
2. P(X ≤ 3) = 0.7576.
3. P(X > 4) = 1 − P(X ≤ 4) = 0.1088.

Cars pass a point at 6 per minute. Find P(3 ≤ X ≤ 5) in one minute.

1. P(X ≤ 5) − P(X ≤ 2) = 0.4457 − 0.0620 = 0.3837.

Data: mean 1.95, variance 2.04. Comment on a Poisson model.

1. Mean and variance are close, which supports a Poisson model (as long as the conditions are plausible).

#### Common mistakes

- Not rescaling λ to the interval in the question.
- P(X > 4) = 1 − P(X ≤ 3).

### SB4 Sums of independent Poisson variables

*Refs: AQA SB4 · Edexcel FS1 2.2 · OCR 5.02n · MEI SR15. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- If X ~ Po(λ) and Y ~ Po(μ) are independent, X + Y ~ Po(λ + μ).

#### Worked example

Faults in type A cable occur at 1.2 per 100 m and in type B at 2.1 per 100 m, independently. Find the probability of exactly 3 faults in total in 100 m of each.

1. Total ~ Po(3.3). P(T = 3) = e^{−3.3} × 3.3³/6 = 0.2209.

Find the probability of 2 faults in A and 1 in B.

1. 0.2169 × 0.2572 = 0.0558 (multiply, as they are independent).

#### Common mistakes

- Adding λs of variables that are not independent.

### The Poisson approximation to the binomial

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 2.3 · MEI SR12. Level: A-level (Edexcel), AS & A (MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- If n is large and p is small, B(n, p) ≈ Po(np).

#### Worked example

X ~ B(200, 0.01). Estimate P(X ≤ 3).

1. np = 2. Po(2): P(X ≤ 3) = 0.8571. (Exact binomial 0.8580.)

### The geometric distribution

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 3.1, 3.2 · OCR 5.02f, 5.02g, 5.02h · MEI SR16, SR17, SR18. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- X ~ Geo(p) is the number of trials up to and including the first success, with independent trials and constant p.

```latex
P(X = x) = (1-p)^{x-1}p,\qquad P(X > x) = (1-p)^x,\qquad E(X) = \frac1p,\qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac{1-p}{p^2}
```

#### Explained

P(X > x) = (1 − p)ˣ because the first x trials must all fail. That gives P(X ≤ x) = 1 − (1 − p)ˣ, which avoids summing many terms.

#### Worked example

A player scores a penalty with probability 0.8. X is the number of the penalty on which they first miss. Find P(X = 4), P(X > 5) and E(X).

1. Here "success" is a miss, p = 0.2.
2. P(X = 4) = 0.8³ × 0.2 = 0.1024. P(X > 5) = 0.8⁵ = 0.3277. E(X) = 5.

#### Common mistakes

- Using (1 − p)ˣp instead of (1 − p)^{x−1}p.

### The negative binomial distribution

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 3.1, 3.3. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- X is the number of trials needed to get r successes:

```latex
P(X = x) = \binom{x-1}{r-1}p^r(1-p)^{x-r},\quad x = r, r+1, \dots
```

- E(X) = r/p, Var(X) = r(1 − p)/p².

#### Explained

The last trial must be the rth success; the other r − 1 successes can be anywhere in the first x − 1 trials. With r = 1 it is the geometric distribution. X is a sum of r independent geometric variables, which explains the mean and variance.

#### Worked example

p = 0.4. Find the probability that the third success comes on the seventh trial, and the mean and variance of the number of trials needed for 3 successes.

1. C(6, 2) × 0.4³ × 0.6⁴ = 15 × 0.064 × 0.1296 = 0.1244.
2. E(X) = 3/0.4 = 7.5. Var(X) = 3 × 0.6/0.16 = 11.25.

#### Common mistakes

- Using C(x, r) instead of C(x − 1, r − 1).

### Check questions

1. A shop sells on average 3.5 kettles a week. (a) State two conditions for a Poisson model. (b) Find the probability of selling exactly 5 in a week, and at least 8 in two weeks. [6 marks]
2. X ~ Po(λ) and P(X = 2) = P(X = 3). Find λ and P(X ≤ 2). [4 marks]
3. A die is rolled until a six appears. Find the probability that more than 6 rolls are needed, and the variance of the number of rolls. [4 marks]
4. (Edexcel) A coin with P(head) = 0.3 is tossed until the fourth head. Find the probability that exactly 10 tosses are needed, and the expected number of tosses. [4 marks]
5. X ~ Po(1.5) and Y ~ Po(2.5) independently. Find P(X + Y = 4) and P(X = 1 | X + Y = 4). [5 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) Sales occur independently, singly, at a constant average rate (2). (b) Po(3.5): P(X = 5) = 0.1322 (2). Po(7): P(Y ≥ 8) = 1 − 0.5987 = 0.4013 (2).
2. e^{−λ}λ²/2 = e^{−λ}λ³/6 (1), λ = 3 (1). P(X ≤ 2) = e^{−3}(1 + 3 + 4.5) = 0.4232 (2).
3. P(X > 6) = (5/6)⁶ = 0.3349 (2). Var = (5/6)/(1/36) = 30 (2).
4. C(9, 3) × 0.3⁴ × 0.7⁶ = 84 × 0.0081 × 0.117649 (1) = 0.0800 (1). E = 4/0.3 = 13.3 (2).
5. X + Y ~ Po(4): P = e^{−4} × 4⁴/24 = 0.1954 (2). P(X = 1, Y = 3)/P(X + Y = 4) (1) = (0.3347 × 0.2138)/0.1954 = 0.3662 (2). (Exactly C(4, 1)(0.375)(0.625)³ = 0.3662.)
